# Agentic CCP: Target Architecture for AI-Native Compliance

Nov 9, 2026 · @Claude

**Bottom line.** Keep CCP's core, because it is what no incumbent documents and what auditors and buyers will test: write-once raw evidence, append-only observations and verdicts, bitemporal state, extract-then-rule and forced row-level security. Build the target around one rule: code decides every status from complete, versioned facts and abstains when the facts are incomplete; models read, extract, investigate and draft only through one permission-checked tool registry; and every AI claim is a pointer that code checks before a person, or later a measured policy, accepts it. The agent stack follows what frontier teams do now (6.4 to 6.13): harness changes ship as releases behind evals, each prompt is rebuilt from the run log, agents search through layered, per-tenant retrieval that is evaluated on its own, and guardrails and authorization sit outside the model. Run it as one Python service on one Postgres per cell, with S3 Object Lock, a per-tenant tamper-evident record and the Anthropic API, and add no workflow engine, broker, second database or sandbox until a named trigger fires. Get there in three steps: ten weeks of fixes inside today's architecture that close the verified false-pass and fail-open paths and take the first real-model measurements (the [first report](https://claude.ai/artifact/WRau5vs9uFjkgBqzbC1Arj)'s Q1 to Q13, plus tenant-first keys); by month 8, a production cell with a connector contract that proves completeness, an authenticated approval service, one tool registry and evals that gate every release; then the differentiators, which are pull-request fixes rendered by code, evidence review and questionnaires with machine-checked citations, packages auditors can verify, and autonomy that reversible actions earn from measured false-pass rates. CCP will not out-integrate Vanta soon, but it can be the platform whose every PASS is complete, checkable and accountable, which is what buyers and auditors now scrutinize.

**How to read this report.** Linked statements were checked against their source on 9 Nov 2026. "Inferred" or "in our reading" marks reasoning across sources, and "proposed" or "Proposed for CCP" marks a design choice. Code references are to the `agentic` repository at `3386899`, relative to `ccp/` unless a path says otherwise.

## 1. Current architecture assessment

CCP is a 21,515-line Python build of the whole compliance loop, written from `spec.md` v1.3 and run against two fixture tenants. Its core already has the shape an audit-grade product needs: a write-once raw zone, append-only observations, a bitemporal state table, and "models extract, code decides". Its edges are teaching-scale by design: three connectors with one live client, ten tests, a command line, one polling worker, and AI quality that has never been measured against a real model. The target keeps the core and rebuilds the edges. The [first report](https://claude.ai/artifact/WRau5vs9uFjkgBqzbC1Arj) (Section A) holds the line-level evidence. Code was read on 9 Nov 2026 at `agentic` `3386899`.

| Dimension | CCP today |
| --- | --- |
| Tenants | 2 fixture tenants in `config/tenants.yaml` |
| Connectors | GitHub (fixtures and a live REST and GraphQL client), a mock HRIS, manual uploads (Markdown, text, text-layer PDF) |
| Tests and controls | 10 tests (7 deterministic, 2 extract-then-rule, 1 fixed workflow) behind 9 controls, mapped to a 6-criterion SOC 2 slice and an 8-control ISO 27001 slice |
| Agents | Tier-3 evaluator, Investigator, Policy Agent and Copilot, all LangGraph, sharing 13 read-only tools |
| Stack | Python 3.11; Postgres 17 with pgvector and forced row-level security; LangGraph with PostgresSaver; Anthropic API (`claude-haiku-4-5`, `claude-sonnet-5`); Voyage embeddings; Langfuse Cloud |
| Size | 21,515 lines in `ccp/`, 15 SQL migrations, 383 test functions |
| Interface | Command line and a terminal Copilot; no API, UI or user identity |

### 1.1 One workflow end to end

The flagship case: a person terminated in the HR system still has GitHub access.

| Step | What CCP does | What holds up | What is missing |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Connect | Reads a token from `GITHUB_TOKEN`; tenants come from a YAML file | One `pull()` contract for fixture and live sources | OAuth, stored credentials, token refresh, connection health and schedules (`connectors/base.py:49-54`) |
| Collect | A full pull every run: 7 REST endpoints and one GraphQL query | Provider ids and timestamps are kept; the payload is untouched | Retries, backoff and rate limits; any non-404 error aborts the pull mid-stream (`github/client.py:55-60`) |
| Store | The raw file is written once; `raw_events` dedups on the provider's event id; only new rows reach `event_log` | A replayable record, append-only by database grant | Local disk only; no completeness signal per pull |
| Normalize and link | Pure mappers append `Person` and `AccessGrant` observations; HR and GitHub accounts link into one person, and unclear pairs go to review | Every identity comparison is logged with its features and thresholds | Live mode cannot see a revoked grant (`github_mapper.py:303,321`); unchanged member, branch-protection, `SECURITY.md` and file-listing snapshots re-land on every pull (`github/client.py:129`, `:179`, `:189`, `:222`) |
| Evaluate | Each change maps to (test, subject) pairs, coalesced for 60 s; one verdict per `evaluation_key` | The tier is fixed when a test is written; an infrastructure failure parks or dead-letters, never becomes a verdict | The mapping is hard-coded (`evaluators/impact.py:149-209`); only full runs resolve identities and refresh the index, so change-driven runs skip both (`compliance_state/run.py:65-67`) |
| Record state | One bitemporal `compliance_state` row per verdict, under a per-subject lock | Separates "what was true" from "what we believed" | A verdict is committed, then announced in a second transaction; a crash in between loses it (`evaluators/runner.py:93-106`) |
| Investigate | Each fail or `needs_review` goes to the Investigator graph | Read-only tools; budgets on tokens, dollars, time and tool calls | Tool results reach the model unfenced and unscanned (`llm/client.py:126-140`) |
| Approve and fix | One append-only approvals table; dry run by default; one live action behind five gates; a re-pull closes the loop | A payload hash pins what was approved | `reject` or a typo approves (`approvals/cli.py:88-90`); nothing checks the hash on resume; rollback skips the gates (`remediation/runner.py:243-289`); reviewers are typed names |

### 1.2 Assessment by area

| Area | CCP today | Verdict | Why |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Connectors | Three connectors; `pull()` is the whole contract | Rebuild the contract | Breadth needs a connection lifecycle, cursors, completeness, rate limits and a conformance harness first |
| Raw zone and ingestion | Write-once files on local disk; dedup on provider ids; an event log | Keep; move to object storage with a retention lock | The replayable record is what replay and auditors rely on; the stored hash covers CCP's re-serialization, not the bytes received (`ingest/raw_zone.py:62`), so the move should hash the response bytes and keep the provider's request id |
| Background work | A Postgres table as a queue: ordered and parallel topics, 5 attempts, dead-letter, parking; worker processes that each poll one consumer (`cli.py:552-563`) | Harden, or move to a durable engine (Section 3) | Safe only as one process; one bad record stalls an ordered topic (`ingest/consumers.py:61-72`) |
| Normalization and identity | Pure mappers; append-only observations; logged identity scoring with a review queue | Keep | Add an identity provider as a source and a review screen |
| Evidence and state | Insert-only grants on history tables; bitemporal `compliance_state` | Keep | Answers "what did we believe on audit day" |
| Knowledge | 9 controls and two framework slices in YAML | Grow | Needs a versioned common-control catalogue and mapping review |
| Evaluation | Three tiers fixed at authoring time; pure rules decide | Keep the tiers; harden citations | Citation checks confirm presence, not support (`evaluators/citations.py:42-47`) |
| Agents | Four graphs, per-graph tool allowlists, the tenant bound in code, budgets | Keep the contract | Injection handling only detects; quality is unmeasured |
| Approvals and remediation | One append-only table, a payload hash, dry run, five gates | Keep the design; fix the defects | Fails open on an unknown decision; no authenticated reviewer |
| Evals and observability | Golden, judge and adversarial suites; a JSONL step log; Langfuse; replay | Keep; run them for real | All 27 recorded eval runs are local, git-ignored files in which every model-backed case was skipped for lack of an API key; CI has never run because the repo has no remote; replay breaks once a cited document changes |
| Security and tenancy | `TenantContext`, `ScopedDB` and forced row-level security on every tenant table, vectors and checkpoints included; 21 guardrails, each with a test | Keep | Upload paths are not contained (`manual/uploads.py:88`); secrets are redacted only in prompts |
| Product surface | Command line and terminal Copilot; Postgres in Docker; tenants in YAML | Build | No API, UI, user accounts, RBAC, onboarding or cloud deployment |

### 1.3 What to keep, and what limits growth

Keep: raw capture with provider ids and timestamps; append-only observations and verdicts keyed by `evaluation_key`; the bitemporal state table; extract-then-rule with pure rules; "graphs read, runners write"; per-graph tool allowlists with the tenant bound in code; budgets on every run; failure handling in which an outage parks or dead-letters work instead of producing a verdict; approvals pinned by a payload hash; three layers of tenant isolation; and guardrail and eval suites tied to tests.

What limits growth, most severe first:

1. **Live evidence is not trustworthy yet, and CCP cannot see what it has not assessed.** The connector contract has no cursor, completeness signal or snapshot semantics, so revocations are invisible and unchanged data re-lands on every pull. The live client also reads any 404 as "nothing there" (`connectors/github/client.py:55-60`), and GitHub answers 404 rather than 403 to a request not authorized for a private resource ([GitHub](https://docs.github.com/en/rest/using-the-rest-api/troubleshooting-the-rest-api)), so a terminated person whose grants CCP could not see passes GH-1 with "no GitHub grants on record" (`evaluators/deterministic/gh1.py:42-49`). The rollup counts a test as pending only when it has no state rows at all (`knowledge/rollup.py:76-78`), so a subject never evaluated, or whose re-evaluation parked or dead-lettered, drops out of view while its old PASS keeps counting.
2. **Citation checks confirm presence, not support.** On the repo's own fixtures, a training record marked "In progress" verified as completed and passed, and tier 3 fixes retrieval quality at 1.0 (`agent/graphs/tier3.py:189`).
3. **The approval path can fail open.** An unknown decision approves, no resume checks the payload hash, rollback bypasses the five gates, and reviewers are typed names.
4. **Background work is safe only as one process.** A verdict can be lost between two transactions, one bad record stalls an ordered topic, and parallel consumers could skip rows or replace a fresh verdict with a stale one.
5. **AI quality is unmeasured.** No eval has run against a real model, and the nightly gate would block on noise (5.2).
6. **Injection handling only detects.** Tool results reach the model unfenced and are cut at 20,000 characters, possibly mid-JSON, without the model being told (`llm/client.py:136`); detection is 15 fixed English phrases (`security/prompt_guard.py:28-45`).
7. **Replay and change propagation have gaps.** Replay breaks once a cited document changes, and tool loops cannot be replayed because only their first request and last response are kept (`agent/core.py:328-330`). Evaluators read the latest observations at processing time and cite entity ids, so a verdict cannot be re-derived from what it saw (`evaluators/context.py:116-129`). Verdicts store no rule version, and because the evaluation key omits it, a corrected rule run over unchanged data is dropped as a duplicate until the data changes or the 30-day revalidation runs (`evaluators/verdict.py:72-90`, `:107`; `evaluators/runner.py:200`; `evaluators/clock.py:61`). The impact mapping is code, and one daily model budget also parks deterministic tests.
8. **There is no product surface.** No API, UI, authenticated users, connection onboarding or cloud deployment.

## 2. Competitive technical research

The six incumbents share one architecture: a current-state store fed mostly by scheduled syncs, deterministic tests over it, and models around the pass or fail decision rather than inside it. In 2026 all of them shipped agents, and the best-documented teams converged on rules CCP already follows: tools designed around information, verifiers inside the loop, and proposals that wait for approval. They differ on evidence integrity and on how far agents may write. None publicly documents an immutable event ledger, bitemporal state or citations checked in code, and the open-source code of the leading AI-native entrant shows how an agent product gets approvals and result integrity wrong.

Linked statements were checked against the source on 9 Nov 2026. "In our reading" marks inference. Vendors' internal systems are private, and nothing here states one as fact unless the vendor published it.

### 2.1 How the incumbents are built

| Company | Data, sync and checks | Agents and autonomy | Quality and evals |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Vanta | 400+ integrations by its count. Tests read Vanta's stored copy of synced resources ([tests](https://developer.vanta.com/docs/concepts/tests.md)); test snapshots keep raw JSON and, for API tests, the requests made ([snapshot API](https://developer.vanta.com/api-reference/audits/get-test-snapshot-detail-for-an-evidence-row.md)). MongoDB Atlas is the primary database ([Vanta](https://www.vanta.com/resources/how-we-navigated-database-limits-with-a-growing-product)) | One general agent with 280+ tools (75k+ tokens) on a model-agnostic harness that runs on Gemini; loading tools by domain raised task completion 5% and cut cost per run 48% ([Vanta, Sep 2026](https://www.vanta.com/resources/the-cache-miss-that-made-our-agent-faster)). Every product action is one authorized service method exposed to REST, CLI, agent and MCP ([Vanta, Aug 2026](https://www.vanta.com/resources/give-your-agents-capabilities)). Custom agents stop at "Needs approval" ([help](https://help.vanta.com/en/articles/16397022-create-schedule-and-manage-custom-agents)); Auto Mode never pre-approves destructive actions ([updates](https://help.vanta.com/en/articles/11345422-product-updates)) | An evidence verifier checks admissibility and adequacy ([Vanta, Aug 2026](https://www.vanta.com/resources/build-verifiers-for-messy-problems)); adding it to the browser agent doubled task completion ([Vanta, Jun 2026](https://www.vanta.com/resources/vantas-agent-development-principles)). Mapping precision rose from below 35% to 78%, and judge agreement with experts from about 65-70% to 85-90% ([Vanta, Jun 2026](https://www.vanta.com/resources/vanta-ai-quality-evaluation-maturity-model)) |
| Drata | Tests run daily, and each run is stored with raw JSON ([monitoring](https://help.drata.com/en/articles/13391811-monitoring-overview-new-experience)). Custom tests are JSON rules; AI summaries of failures are "assistive, not authoritative" ([AI summaries](https://help.drata.com/en/articles/10008053-enable-ai-summaries-for-tests)). A 2025 post describes a database partition per customer ([Drata](https://drata.com/blog/securing-data-at-scale)) | The vendor-risk agent shows source excerpts and asks before every write ([TPRM Agent](https://help.drata.com/en/articles/16880143-getting-started-with-the-tprm-agent)). Autonomy presets exist, and it withholds a recommendation when too many criteria are inconclusive ([autonomy](https://help.drata.com/en/articles/16880162-tprm-agent-autonomy-settings)). The mapping agent proposes with confidence; nothing applies until a user maps ([mapping](https://help.drata.com/en/articles/16630433-framework-mapping-agent-ai-assisted-control-mapping-for-custom-frameworks)) | An offline LLM pipeline wrote 1,000+ deterministic cloud tests, each validated against Drata's own test API (up to 3 attempts) and reviewed by auditors, at about $20-30 per resource ([Drata, Dec 2025](https://drata.com/blog/AI-compliance-tests)). Braintrust is used for offline evaluation only ([trust page](https://trust.drata.com/)) |
| Secureframe | Daily syncs. Tests evaluate only the latest sync, and no integration test history is kept in the app ([connections](https://support.secureframe.com/en/articles/15111617-managing-your-integration-connections)). Changes to built-in tests ship as versioned updates after a countdown ([updates](https://support.secureframe.com/en/articles/15111262-test-activity-dashboard)) | An MCP server with 100+ tools including create, update and delete, and no read-only mode ([MCP](https://support.secureframe.com/en/articles/15111553-secureframe-mcp-server-ai-driven-compliance-insights)). Cloud fixes are copy-paste IaC and CLI ([remediation](https://support.secureframe.com/en/articles/15111264-comply-ai-cloud-remediation-test-guidance)) | AI evidence validation is advisory only ([validation](https://support.secureframe.com/en/articles/15111239-ai-evidence-validation-for-upload-test)) |
| Sprinto | Ingestion "plans" (transform, map, insert, evaluate) into customer-definable entity types ([ingestion](https://docs.sprinto.com/ingestion/get-started-with-ingestion-in-sprinto.md)); most checks refresh every 4 to 12 hours ([FAQ](https://docs.sprinto.com/monitors/frequently-asked-questions.md)) | Playbooks give each workflow a charter (goal, scope, guardrails) and ordered agent steps that can pause at approval forms ([Jul 2026](https://docs.sprinto.com/product-updates/july-2026.md)). A browser extension changes customer consoles through the user's session after confirmation ([Sprinto Assist](https://docs.sprinto.com/sprinto-assist/fix-failing-monitors-with-sprinto-assist.md)) | Questionnaires run in passes: verbatim fact extraction, answer direction, then writing, with a grounding check that can retry once; 0.995 faithfulness on an internal 30-question benchmark ([Aug 2026](https://docs.sprinto.com/product-updates/august-2026.md)) |
| Scrut | Direct API connectors with 24-hour syncs ([example](https://help.scrut.io/docs/integrating-15five.md)); vendor-authored tests from a CIS-based library ([tests](https://help.scrut.io/docs/getting-started-with-tests-in-scrut.md)); every REST write needs an `Idempotency-Key` ([API](https://developer.scrut.io/)) | Agents propose rather than act ([Teammates](https://help.scrut.io/docs/getting-started-with-scrut-teammates.md)); MCP has 19 read tools and 3 narrow writes ([MCP](https://help.scrut.io/docs/supported-scrut-mcp-tools.md)) | A validation agent reproduces each vulnerability finding before publishing it as likely true or false positive ([Sep 2026](https://help.scrut.io/docs/september-2026.md)) |
| Thoropass | Evidence belongs to one audit, and evidence from previous audits "can never be used" ([help](https://help.thoropass.com/en/articles/9293724-why-can-t-i-reuse-evidence-from-previous-audits)) | MCP writes only comments, attachments and status on evidence requests ([MCP](https://help.thoropass.com/en/articles/16976070-connecting-to-the-thoropass-mcp-server)) | Its own CPA firm audits; LLMs draft first-pass test results that auditors review and own ([Thoropass, Sep 2026](https://www.thoropass.com/blog/mcp-for-audit-ai-auditor-judgment)) |

What makes each effective, in our reading: Vanta's discipline of verifiers before autonomy and tools shaped around information; Drata's use of AI offline to write deterministic tests, plus a rule to withhold judgement when evidence is thin; Sprinto's separation of fact extraction from answer writing; Scrut's reproduce-before-publish; and Thoropass's evidence frozen per audit.

### 2.2 What open-source code shows

Two AI-native GRC products and one established open-source platform publish their code, which shows what docs do not.

| Project | What the code shows | Lesson for CCP |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Comp AI (`150fed5`, 4 Nov 2026) | No agent runtime: each feature calls the AI SDK directly with a hard-coded model ([assistant](https://github.com/trycompai/comp/blob/150fed5ade35be5c37f142b126aa10b1859aa26e/apps/api/src/assistant-chat/assistant-chat.controller.ts#L121-L129)). AWS remediation runs whatever SDK command the model names, guarded by a 13-command denylist that rollback skips ([executor](https://github.com/trycompai/comp/blob/150fed5ade35be5c37f142b126aa10b1859aa26e/apps/api/src/cloud-security/aws-command-executor.ts#L772-L865)). Approval is the string "acknowledged", after which another AI call regenerates the fix steps ([remediation](https://github.com/trycompai/comp/blob/150fed5ade35be5c37f142b126aa10b1859aa26e/apps/api/src/cloud-security/remediation.service.ts#L512-L681)). Failing checks are held as "inconclusive" and hidden from the customer until a self-heal agent decides ([evaluation](https://github.com/trycompai/comp/blob/150fed5ade35be5c37f142b126aa10b1859aa26e/apps/api/src/integration-platform/utils/task-check-evaluation.ts#L30-L67)). Findings are reconciled only from complete, successful scans ([reconciliation](https://github.com/trycompai/comp/blob/150fed5ade35be5c37f142b126aa10b1859aa26e/apps/api/src/cloud-security/reconciliation.service.ts#L15-L71)) | What not to do: no denylists, no regeneration after approval, and no AI deciding whether a customer sees a failure. The complete-scan rule matches the sync contract CCP needs |
| Probo (`7bbe06a`, 9 Nov 2026) | Its own Go agent runtime: Postgres `SKIP LOCKED` claims with leases and heartbeats, a checkpoint after every tool turn, and approval interrupts whose decisions must cover exactly the pending tool calls ([service](https://github.com/getprobo/probo/blob/7bbe06ab6180d7099f4c4dd20e662520f792f348/pkg/agentexecution/service.go#L100-L157)). No production agent enables approvals or guardrails ([Slack agent](https://github.com/getprobo/probo/blob/7bbe06ab6180d7099f4c4dd20e662520f792f348/pkg/probot/channel/slack/execution_adapter.go#L163-L201)); MCP tools take `organization_id` from the model ([resolver](https://github.com/getprobo/probo/blob/7bbe06ab6180d7099f4c4dd20e662520f792f348/pkg/server/api/mcp/v1/resolver.go#L149-L230)); there is no row-level security ([scope](https://github.com/getprobo/probo/blob/7bbe06ab6180d7099f4c4dd20e662520f792f348/pkg/coredata/scope.go#L30-L98)) | Copy the interrupt design. Make approval mandatory per tool class, not opt-in; bind the tenant on the server and keep row-level security underneath |
| CISO Assistant (`a867086`, 9 Nov 2026) | "Proposals, not actions": the assistant never writes to the database ([architecture](https://github.com/intuitem/ciso-assistant-community/blob/a86708675840cc1628a927a3132a119afd4cf645/backend/chat/ARCHITECTURE.md#L49-L574)). Its MCP read-only profile does not register write tools at all (45 of 102), and an action ledger tracks proposed, approved, rejected and expired actions. It ships evals for tool-selection accuracy against tool-surface size and gold sets per framework ([MCP server](https://github.com/intuitem/ciso-assistant-community/blob/a86708675840cc1628a927a3132a119afd4cf645/cli/ca_mcp/server.py#L312-L370)) | The open design closest to CCP. Copy the hard read-only MCP profile and the tool-surface eval |

### 2.3 AI-native entrants and adjacent agent companies

AI-native GRC vendors document their agents loosely. The clearest engineering comes from agent companies in law, customer service, finance and healthcare, and it converges on CCP's own design. State, side effects and final decisions stay in code or an approved policy; models read, extract and draft. These companies add three things CCP lacks: a policy layer for consequential actions, versioned releases gated by evals, and calibrated monitors in production.

| Company | What is technically different | What it shows for CCP |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Anecdotes (GRC) | Deterministic "analysis rules" run over evidence normalized from 230+ systems; agents react to rule outcomes and re-run the rule after a fix; a recipe drafts new rules. Approvals, permissions and evals are not documented ([Agent Studio](https://www.anecdotes.ai/agent-studio)) | Models can draft rules that code then runs; a fix is confirmed by re-running the test, not by the agent's report |
| Trustero (GRC) | Publishes "p90 consistency across repeated control evaluations" of 92-93%, with no dataset or method (a vendor claim) ([Trustero](https://www.trustero.com/trustero-ai)) | Verdict stability is a quality buyers can see; CCP should define it (pass^k) and publish the method |
| Complyance, Mycroft (GRC) | Complyance's pages contradict each other: agents "execute actions autonomously" versus "All actions are surfaced for human approval". Mycroft's agents change customer systems, with approval stated only for configuration drift ([Complyance](https://www.complyance.com/resources/what-is-a-grc-ai-agent)) | Autonomy in AI-native GRC is mostly undocumented; a precise approval model is a credibility advantage |
| Conveyor (questionnaires) | MCP write tools limited by the capabilities granted to each key; low-confidence or high-risk answers routed to reviewers by question type ([May 2026](https://www.conveyor.com/blog/may-2026-product-updates)) | If CCP ever offers MCP writes, scope them per credential |
| Hyperproof | Deterministic tests for structured data and AI for documents; its help page warns that an unresolved confirmation "might be applied to the next work item" ([help](https://help.hyperproof.app/en/articles/14791978-hyperproof-ai-validation-test-library)) | Approvals must bind to the exact payload, as CCP's hash does |
| Fieldguide, Optro (audit side) | Auditor-side agents run engagement phases; Fieldguide's reviewer "confirms every conclusion traces to its evidence" ([Fieldguide](https://www.fieldguide.com/products/agents/overview)). Optro (formerly AuditBoard) bought Midship, whose agents test raw client evidence ([May 2026](https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/optro-leads-the-global-audit-transformation-with-the-acquisition-of-ai-native-midship-302763559.html)) | Auditors' agents will check traceability, so CCP's exports need stable ids, hashes and citations from each conclusion to raw evidence |
| Harvey (legal) | A tool-call policy engine outside the model: permit, deny or ask the user; it fails closed, workspace settings can only tighten it, and tool definitions are pinned ([Oct 2026](https://www.harvey.ai/blog/building-harveys-mcp-policy-engine)). Citations point to sentence indices instead of fuzzy-matched text, and attorneys preferred the result four times as often ([Apr 2026](https://www.harvey.ai/blog/rebuilding-harveys-review-algorithm)) | One deterministic policy point for all tools; citations as pointers that code renders and checks |
| Sierra (customer service) | Each release is an immutable snapshot of code, prompts, model versions and knowledge, with rollback ([Sierra](https://sierra.ai/blog/agent-development-life-cycle)). Supervisors switch from observe to intercept on high-risk topics ([monitoring](https://sierra.ai/blog/agent-monitoring)). On its τ-knowledge benchmark the best model scored 37.4% on one try and 20.6% across four ([May 2026](https://sierra.ai/blog/tau-knowledge)) | Verdicts should name a release bundle; blocking checks belong only where output is irreversible; agents that must search documents and act still fail often |
| Ramp (finance) | Agents answer approve, reject or needs review instead of giving a confidence number; deterministic limits sit on top; the total exposure an agent can create is capped by a budget ([trust](https://builders.ramp.com/post/how-to-build-agents-users-can-trust), [risk](https://builders.ramp.com/post/agentic-risk-operations)) | Cap automated status changes per tenant; never treat users' acceptance of a verdict as ground truth |
| Decagon, Anterior, Parcha | Decagon keeps critical actions in code beneath natural-language procedures ([Decagon](https://decagon.ai/resources/from-sops-to-agent-operating-procedures)). Anterior orders human review by risk and compares judge and human decisions continuously ([talk](https://ai.engineer/talks/mission-critical-evals-at-scale-learnings-from-100-000-medical-decisions)). Parcha found that separating extraction from judgement improved document checks ([Parcha](https://blog.parcha.ai/building-ai-agents-in-production/)) | Independent confirmation of extract-then-rule, plus a production review loop CCP lacks |

A new standard is also forming: [AIUC-1](https://www.aiuc-1.com/) certifies AI agents after 1,000 to 5,000 red-team scenarios and an accredited audit, with quarterly retests.

### 2.4 Where CCP stands

| Capability | CCP today | Best public practice | CCP's position |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Integration breadth | 3 connectors, 1 live | 400+ (Vanta); 590 in Comp AI's catalogue, with long-tail checks kept as a versioned JSON DSL in the database | Far behind. Breadth needs a connector SDK and declarative checks |
| Sync semantics | Snapshots only; a full pull each run | Push APIs that replace per type or per session (Vanta, Drata, Sprinto); reconcile only from complete scans (Comp AI) | Behind. Needs a completeness contract |
| Evidence history | Write-once raw zone, append-only verdicts, bitemporal state | Run logs and snapshots (Vanta, Drata); evidence frozen per audit (Thoropass) | Ahead in design, unproven at scale |
| Check logic | Pure rules over typed entities; tier fixed per test | Deterministic rules over stored data; AI-written tests validated offline (Drata) | At par. Adopt offline generation |
| AI output checks | Citations checked in code, for presence only | Verifiers scored against experts and auditors (Vanta); grounding checks (Sprinto); no machine-checked citations documented | A lead once support checks land |
| Agent autonomy | Graphs read, runners write; one approval stage that fails open today | Approval before every write (Drata); destructive actions never pre-approved (Vanta); proposals only (CISO Assistant) | Strongest contract on paper; defects to fix |
| Agent interface | Internal tools only | Hosted MCP, from 3 narrow writes (Scrut) to 100+ full-write tools (Secureframe); a hard read-only profile (CISO Assistant) | Behind. Generate MCP from the tool registry, read-only first |
| Evals | Golden, judge and adversarial suites, never run on a real model | Published numbers only from Vanta and Sprinto | Behind until run. Published results would be rare in the category |
| Tenant isolation | Forced row-level security on every tenant table, vectors and checkpoints included | A partition per customer (Drata); Vanta reported a cross-tenant leak after a filter was removed ([RCA](https://www.vanta.com/resources/rca-inc-868)) | Ahead in design |
| Background work | Postgres queue with `SKIP LOCKED`, dead-letter, parking and budgets; safe for only one process per topic today | Temporal in a Drata job posting; Hatchet on Vanta's subprocessor list | Behind on durability and scale |
| Remediation | Dry run; one live action behind five gates | Draft PRs through the customer's coding agent (Vanta, [docs](https://developer.vanta.com/docs/quickstart/remediate-with-mcp.md)); copy-paste fixes (Secureframe); console changes through a browser extension (Sprinto); model-chosen AWS commands under a denylist (Comp AI) | Safest design, least shipped |

Incumbents lead on breadth and product surface. CCP's possible lead is evidence integrity and agent contracts, which matter more as buyers and auditors scrutinize AI after the 2026 [allegations against Delve](https://techcrunch.com/2026/03/22/delve-accused-of-misleading-customers-with-fake-compliance/), which Delve denies.

## 3. AI agent architecture research

The teams that publish most about production agents (Anthropic, OpenAI, Google, Cognition, LangChain and HumanLayer) agree on four points. Code owns the control flow. Models get a few well-designed tools. The durable record lives outside the model's context. Consequential actions pause for a person. CCP's design already follows all four, so the research mostly confirms it and shows where it must harden. Much of the evidence comes from coding and research agents on Claude models, the family CCP uses, but most of it is the vendors' own internal measurement. Evals and guardrails are covered in Section 5, and 6.4 to 6.13 turn the newest practice from frontier labs and agent companies into a decision for each layer.

In the tables, the middle column is checked against the linked page (read 9 Nov 2026), and the last column is our proposal for CCP.

### 3.1 Workflows first; agents only where the path is unknown

| Lesson | What the source shows | Proposed for CCP |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Start with the simplest design | Anthropic separates workflows, where code fixes the path, from agents, where the model directs its own steps. It recommends "the simplest solution possible" and stopping conditions such as an iteration cap ([Anthropic, Dec 2024](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/building-effective-agents)) | Keep CCP's split. Tiers 1 and 2 and the tier-3 workflow stay fixed paths; only the Investigator, Policy Agent and Copilot loop, inside the budgets CCP already enforces |
| Use agents for judgement and unstructured input | OpenAI reserves agents for complex judgement, rules that are hard to maintain, or heavy unstructured data, and names vendor security reviews as an example ([OpenAI guide, undated](https://cdn.openai.com/business-guides-and-resources/a-practical-guide-to-building-agents.pdf)) | Vendor reviews, questionnaires and review of uploaded evidence qualify. Checks over structured API data stay deterministic |
| Orchestrate in code | Orchestration by code is "more deterministic and predictable, in terms of speed, cost and performance" ([OpenAI Agents SDK docs](https://openai.github.io/openai-agents-python/multi_agent/)). Production agents are "mostly deterministic code, with LLM steps sprinkled in" ([HumanLayer 12-factor agents](https://github.com/humanlayer/12-factor-agents)) | CCP already does this: a runner, not the graph, persists every output. Keep it as a hard rule |
| Mix code and model steps in one graph | Google's ADK 2.0 added graph workflows that combine agent nodes with deterministic nodes ([ADK](https://adk.dev/workflows/)) | CCP's LangGraph graphs already mix the two, so no framework change is needed for this |

### 3.2 One writer: where several agents help

| Finding | What the source shows | Proposed for CCP |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Parallel readers help breadth, at a price | An orchestrator with worker agents beat a single agent by 90.2% on Anthropic's internal research eval, using about 15 times the tokens of a chat. It fits poorly when every agent needs shared context ([Anthropic, Jun 2025](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/multi-agent-research-system)) | Consider it only for read-heavy breadth such as audit prep or vendor research, and only after measuring the single Investigator on the same task. The eval and its data are not public |
| Parallel writers conflict | "Actions carry implicit decisions, and conflicting decisions carry bad results" ([Cognition, Jun 2025](https://cognition.com/blog/dont-build-multi-agents)). In 2026 Cognition found that in the multi-agent patterns that work, "writes stay single-threaded" ([Cognition, Apr 2026](https://cognition.com/blog/multi-agents-working)) | Keep "graphs read, runners write": code is the only writer of verdicts, state and approvals |
| Generators grade themselves too kindly | "agents reliably skew positive when grading their own work". A separate, sceptical evaluator was easier to tune. The full setup cost $200 against $9 for a solo run, and only the full setup produced a working app ([Anthropic, Mar 2026](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/harness-design-long-running-apps)) | Check AI conclusions in code first (citations, rules), then with a reviewer that starts from a clean context, for high-value outputs only. CCP's judge runs on the same model as tier 2 (`claude-haiku-4-5`); give it a different model: a different Claude model, inside the zero-retention organization, for anything that reads customer evidence, and a different model family only on fixture and synthetic suites (Section 5). Anthropic calls it "best practice to evaluate with a different model from the one used to generate the evaluated output" ([Anthropic](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/test-and-evaluate/develop-tests.md)) |
| Autonomy needs a strong verifier | Sixteen parallel agents wrote a C compiler of about 100,000 lines over about 2,000 sessions for about $20,000. It worked because the test suite was a near-perfect check ([Anthropic, Feb 2026](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/building-c-compiler)) | Compliance has only partial verifiers (rules and citation checks), so autonomy stays low. CCP's five remediation gates are the right default |

### 3.3 Tools are the contract between code and model

Anthropic describes tools as "a contract between deterministic systems and non-deterministic agents" ([Anthropic, Sep 2025](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/writing-tools-for-agents)). The published practice:

- **Few, task-level tools.** Not one wrapper per endpoint. Use namespaced names, meaningful identifiers instead of UUIDs, concise or detailed response modes, pagination and actionable errors (same source).
- **Arguments that make mistakes hard.** Requiring absolute file paths removed a whole class of errors on SWE-bench ([Anthropic, Dec 2024](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/building-effective-agents)).
- **Expect wrong tools and wrong parameters.** These are the most common failures. Loading tool definitions on demand cut their context by 85% and raised Opus 4 from 49% to 74% on MCP evals; usage examples raised accuracy on complex parameters from 72% to 90% ([Anthropic, Nov 2025](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/advanced-tool-use)). These are Anthropic's internal figures on Claude.
- **Code execution saves tokens but needs a sandbox.** Having the agent write code that calls tools cut one workflow from about 150,000 tokens to about 2,000, at the cost of a sandbox, resource limits and monitoring ([Anthropic, Nov 2025](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/code-execution-with-mcp)).
- **Rate every tool's risk.** Low, medium or high, by write access, reversibility, permissions and financial impact ([OpenAI guide](https://cdn.openai.com/business-guides-and-resources/a-practical-guide-to-building-agents.pdf)).
- **MCP is now stateless and stricter.** The 2026-07-28 revision removes sessions ([changelog](https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/2026-07-28/changelog)). Servers "MUST NOT accept or transit any other tokens" ([authorization](https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/2026-07-28/basic/authorization)), and clients must treat annotations such as `readOnlyHint` as untrusted ([tools](https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/2026-07-28/server/tools)). MCP moved to the Linux Foundation's Agentic AI Foundation in Dec 2025 ([MCP blog](https://blog.modelcontextprotocol.io/posts/2025-12-09-mcp-joins-agentic-ai-foundation/)), and the official registry is still a preview for public servers only ([registry](https://modelcontextprotocol.io/registry/about)).

**Proposed for CCP.** CCP's tool module is already close: the tenant comes from a closure, extra arguments are rejected, and off-list calls get an error result. Grow it into one typed registry that is the source of truth. Each tool declares Pydantic input and output schemas, a class (read, propose or act), scopes, a rate limit and a timeout. Tools return evidence by id, version and hash, require explicit ids and as-of times, and report missing data as an error, so an empty list is never read as compliant. The same registry generates the agents' tool specs and REST, and later a customer-facing MCP server (not in v1). Read-only behaviour is enforced in the gateway, never by annotations. Credentials stay in a broker that neither the model nor any sandbox can reach, as in Anthropic's Managed Agents, which fetches them from a vault through a proxy ([Anthropic, Apr 2026](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/managed-agents)).

### 3.4 Context and memory: the record is not the prompt

- Anthropic treats context as "a finite resource with diminishing marginal returns". It loads data just in time by identifier, clears old tool results first when compacting, keeps notes outside the window, and has sub-agents return summaries of 1,000-2,000 tokens ([Anthropic, Sep 2025](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/effective-context-engineering-for-ai-agents)).
- Google's ADK rebuilds every prompt from durable stores: "Context is a compiled view over a richer stateful system" ([Google, Dec 2025](https://developers.googleblog.com/en/architecting-efficient-context-aware-multi-agent-framework-for-production/)).
- LangChain's Deep Agents writes the original messages to files as "a canonical record" before it summarizes them ([LangChain, Jan 2026](https://www.langchain.com/blog/context-management-for-deepagents)).
- Anthropic's memory guidance warns that "an incorrect note can be carried into every session". It keeps injected memory small and re-checks notes against the source transcripts; it does not cover memory poisoning or multi-tenant isolation ([Anthropic, Nov 2026](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/components-of-agent-memory)).

**Proposed for CCP.** CCP's step log already records each step with OpenTelemetry GenAI attribute names, and replay rebuilds a verdict from it. Promote that log from a JSONL file to the system of record: an append-only run log in Postgres under row-level security, holding the inputs, each tool call and result, the exact model request and response, and the prompt, model, tool and harness versions. The prompt is a view rebuilt from that log on each call, so compaction can never destroy what the agent saw. Stable content (instructions, the control catalogue) comes first so it can be cached; tenant evidence comes last, by reference. When the Copilot gains memory, keep it per tenant under row-level security, with its source and a checked or unconfirmed flag. Memory may steer retrieval but never counts as evidence.

### 3.5 Durable execution and approvals

Agent frameworks leave durability and approval security to the application:

- LangGraph re-runs the whole interrupted node on resume ("the runtime restarts the entire node from the beginning"), so a model call in the same node as an interrupt is sampled again ([LangGraph](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/interrupts)).
- The OpenAI Agents SDK warns that "Approval permits a tool call to execute; it does not guarantee exactly-once execution" and that "Possession of a run ID or decision ID is not authorization" ([OpenAI Agents SDK](https://openai.github.io/openai-agents-python/human_in_the_loop/)).
- Anthropic's Managed Agents keeps no state in the harness. Every event goes to an append-only session log, and a crashed harness resumes from it ([Anthropic, Apr 2026](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/managed-agents)).

CCP's own queue already gets the hard parts right: idempotent verdicts keyed by `evaluation_key`, dead-letter, parking, and model errors classified so an outage never becomes a verdict. Four gaps sit beneath any engine choice, and each is verified at `3386899`:

- The verdict and its announcement commit in two transactions, so a crash between them strands the verdict (`evaluators/runner.py:93-106`).
- A tier-3 approval is recorded only after the resumed graph and the verdict commit succeed, so a model outage at that moment loses the reviewer's decision (`approvals/actions.py:51-53`).
- A failed record becomes claimable again at once, with no backoff, so a burst of rate-limit errors can use up all five attempts in seconds (`ingest/event_log.py:110-115`).
- Tier 3 starts a fresh thread on every attempt, so a re-delivery repeats the whole run and its model spend (`agent/tier3.py:157-158`).

Fixing these four gaps keeps CCP on its own queue, which is the recommendation; an engine comes in only when a named trigger fires. The engines that could take over, for a small Python team on Postgres:

| Option | What the source shows | Proposed for CCP |
| --- | --- | --- |
| DBOS Transact | An MIT library with no server: checkpoints, queues and schedules live in Postgres ([architecture](https://docs.dbos.dev/architecture)). A transaction can commit an application write and DBOS's record of it together ([transactions](https://docs.dbos.dev/python/tutorials/transaction-tutorial)). Queues can be partitioned by a key such as the tenant ([queues](https://docs.dbos.dev/python/tutorials/queue-tutorial)), and schedules fire once across processes in a named time zone ([schedules](https://docs.dbos.dev/python/tutorials/scheduled-workflows)). It recovers only workflows that match the running code version ([upgrading](https://docs.dbos.dev/python/tutorials/upgrading-workflows)), and its FAQ says recovery across several processes needs its paid Conductor, which its recovery page contradicts ([FAQ](https://docs.dbos.dev/faq)) | The leading candidate if the hardened queue falls short. Run its spike in the medium term (kill a worker mid-step, overlap two deploys) and adopt it only if the spike passes and a trigger fires: a workflow with more than three durable steps across external systems, two or more queue incidents a quarter, or more than about 20 worker processes (Section 9, Decision 4). If adopted, use its portable JSON serializer rather than pickle, pass only ids as inputs, because its tables carry no row-level security, and give its system database a direct connection, because its FAQ says PgBouncer works "only in session mode" ([FAQ](https://docs.dbos.dev/faq)). LangGraph is not among its documented integrations ([AI quickstart](https://docs.dbos.dev/ai/ai-quickstart)), so CCP's graphs would keep their own checkpointer |
| LangGraph with PostgresSaver | Checkpoints save asynchronously by default, so a crash can lose the last step ([source](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/langchain-ai/langgraph/main/libs/langgraph/langgraph/pregel/main.py)). An encrypting serializer exists, and checkpoints pile up unless pruned ([persistence](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/persistence)). Self-hosting LangGraph's server needs an Enterprise licence ([LangSmith](https://docs.langchain.com/langsmith/deploy-to-self-hosted-overview)) | Keep the MIT library for the agent graphs, with synchronous checkpoints, encryption, pruning on the evidence-retention schedule and a graph version on every thread. Skip the hosted server |
| Temporal | Workflow code runs in a sandbox that forbids I/O, so database and model calls must move into activities ([sandbox](https://docs.temporal.io/develop/python/python-sdk-sandbox)). Version pinning does not work with rolling deploys ([versioning](https://docs.temporal.io/production-deployment/worker-deployments/worker-versioning)). The LangGraph plugin is a 0.1.0 Public Preview that keeps checkpoints in memory ([Temporal](https://temporal.io/blog/temporal-langgraph-plugin-durable-execution)), and the generally available agent plugin targets OpenAI's SDK ([Temporal](https://temporal.io/blog/announcing-openai-agents-sdk-partnership)). Cloud starts at $50 per million actions ([pricing](https://temporal.io/pricing)) | The upgrade path if agent work becomes long-running or multi-agent. Today it would mean rewriting CCP's agent core into activities and sending evidence into Temporal's history unless a payload codec encrypts it |
| Pydantic AI | Deferred tools end a run with pending approvals, and the application resumes it later with typed approve or deny decisions; the docs warn that "Approval is not an authorization boundary" ([deferred tools](https://pydantic.dev/docs/ai/tools-toolsets/deferred-tools/)). Its durable API is mid-migration, with the original wrappers due to be removed in v3 ([DBOS page](https://pydantic.dev/docs/ai/integrations/durable_execution/dbos/)) | Borrow its approval semantics now; reassess it as a runtime after v3 |
| Hatchet, Procrastinate, Restate, Claude Agent SDK | Hatchet is a separate engine with per-tenant fairness, but its cron runs in UTC only and skips ticks missed while it is down ([cron](https://docs.hatchet.run/home/cron-runs)). Procrastinate is an MIT Postgres task queue with heartbeats and periodic tasks, but no multi-step durability ([docs](https://procrastinate.readthedocs.io/en/stable/howto/production/retry_stalled_jobs.html)). Restate advises against handlers that run for days ([versioning](https://docs.restate.dev/operate/versioning)). The Claude Agent SDK spawns a CLI process per session whose local files can leak between tenants in a shared container ([hosting](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/agent-sdk/hosting)) | If the hardened queue falls short and DBOS fails its spike, Hatchet is the second choice and Procrastinate the fallback. Restate and the Claude Agent SDK do not fit CCP's evaluator and agent graphs |

Inferred across these sources: every engine pushes towards short runs. DBOS counts a waiting workflow against its queue's concurrency limit, and recovers it only on the same code version; Restate advises against handlers that run for days; and Temporal can pin a long workflow to its code version only with rainbow deployments. So a review that takes days should be a row in a table, not a paused workflow.

**Proposed for CCP: approvals as data, whatever the engine.**

1. An agent run ends when it stores a proposal.
2. An approval is an authenticated, append-only row. The server checks the decision value, the reviewer's rights, the stored request and its payload hash, and rejects replays.
3. The approval starts a separate, idempotent apply step keyed by proposal id and payload hash, which checks the target system before acting.
4. Each model call is its own recorded step, so a resume never re-samples a model whose output fed a verdict.
5. A run is pinned to its harness version (prompts, graph, tools, budgets). A deploy that changes the version cancels and restarts any run still in progress, with the reason recorded. A stored proposal waiting for a person is not a run: it stays bound to its stored artifacts and their hashes and is never sampled again. Anthropic notes that scaffolding goes stale as models improve ([Anthropic, Apr 2026](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/managed-agents)), so the version is stored on every verdict.

This replaces CCP's tier-3 interrupt, where a LangGraph checkpoint waits for a CLI command and nothing re-checks the payload hash on resume.

### 3.6 Human approval that adds assurance

Anthropic found that users approve 93% of permission prompts. Claude Code's auto mode therefore lets read-only actions through, sends the rest to a classifier, and escalates to a person after 3 consecutive or 20 total denials; the classifier still misses 17% of real over-eager actions ([Anthropic, Mar 2026](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/claude-code-auto-mode)). OpenAI brings in a person when failure thresholds are exceeded and for sensitive, irreversible or high-stakes actions, until the agent's reliability is established ([OpenAI guide](https://cdn.openai.com/business-guides-and-resources/a-practical-guide-to-building-agents.pdf)).

**Proposed for CCP.** Tier approvals by risk. Deterministic results publish without review; AI drafts that pass the verifiers get sampled review; any write to customer-visible state or to a customer's systems needs a per-item approval until it graduates under 4.4; status changes, mappings, external publication, merges and changes in a customer's cloud never graduate. Track how often reviewers approve without changes: a gate that approves everything adds no assurance and should be redesigned. Before any customer relies on CCP's approval stage, it needs authenticated reviewers and server-side validation of the decision, because today a typo approves.

### 3.7 Models, data terms and observability

CCP is Anthropic-first: tier 2 and the judge run `claude-haiku-4-5`, and tier 3 and the agents run `claude-sonnet-5` (`spec.md`, Stack). The platform has moved under both pins, and five of the changes below alter how CCP must treat models.

| Topic | What the source shows | Proposed for CCP |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Model lifecycle | The current models are Fable 5.2, Opus 5.5, Sonnet 5.5 and Haiku 5.5; CCP's Haiku 4.5 and Sonnet 5 are listed as legacy but still available ([models](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/about-claude/models/overview.md)). Haiku 4.5's commitment ("Not sooner than October 15, 2026") has passed, so it can now retire on at least 60 days' notice; Sonnet 5 is committed to at least 30 Jun 2027 ([deprecations](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/about-claude/model-deprecations.md)). Older aliases such as `claude-haiku-4-5` resolve to the latest snapshot; only dated ids are pinned ([model ids](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/about-claude/models/model-ids-and-versions.md)) | Pin dated ids (`claude-haiku-4-5-20251001`) and store the model each response reports. Start the Haiku 4.5 to Haiku 5.5 migration now, through the eval gates. Budget about one forced migration per model tier per year |
| Determinism | API version 2026-10-21 removes `temperature`, `top_p` and `top_k`; requests then sample at 1.0, and "No other setting replaces it" ([migration](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/api/migration-2026-10-21.md)). Infrastructure updates can shift behaviour even under a fixed model id ([model ids](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/about-claude/models/model-ids-and-versions.md)). Python SDK 2.0.0 sends the new version by default ([release notes](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/release-notes/overview.md)) | Reproducible means rebuilt from stored artifacts, not re-run; consistency becomes a measured gate (pass^k, Section 5). CCP's lock file holds SDK 1.5.0 (`uv.lock:29-30`), but `pyproject.toml:17` accepts any later version, and CCP sends `temperature: 0` to Haiku 4.5 (`llm/anthropic_client.py:62-73`), so a routine lock upgrade to SDK 2.x would make every tier-2 and judge call fail with a 400 and dead-letter. Remove the parameter and pin the API version in code first |
| Typed output and citations | Structured outputs are generally available on every model and platform. Enabling the Citations feature together with structured outputs returns a 400 ([citations](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/citations.md)) | Keep structured outputs plus quotes verified in code (5.4). Test native citations only as a separate quote-selection pass |
| Refusals | The 5.5, Opus 5 and Fable models run safety classifiers that end a response with `stop_reason: "refusal"`, and "Benign cybersecurity work can also trigger this category" ([refusals](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/refusals-and-fallback.md)) | Compliance evidence is security material. A refusal becomes needs\_review with its category, never a verdict. Measure the refusal rate per test before migrating |
| Caching and batches | The minimum cacheable prompt is 4,096 tokens on Haiku 4.5 and 512 on the 5.5 models ([caching](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/prompt-caching.md)). Batches cost half price but keep results for 29 days, so they are not eligible for zero data retention ([batches](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/batch-processing.md)) | CCP's spec records that tier-2 prompts never reach Haiku 4.5's minimum, so they are never cached (`spec.md` A-62); caching becomes a real cost lever after the migration. Use batches only for evals and other non-customer data |
| Cost tracking | CCP's own code: `price_for()` returns $0 for any model it does not know, which today includes `claude-haiku-5-5` (`observability/cost.py:43-50`) | Fix the price table before any model switch, or the per-run dollar caps never trip |
| Data retention | By default Anthropic deletes API inputs and outputs within 30 days, and flagged content can be kept up to 2 years ([privacy](https://privacy.claude.com/en/articles/7996866-how-long-do-you-store-my-organization-s-data)). Zero data retention excludes batches, the Files API, code execution, the MCP connector and Managed Agents, and the Fable models require 30-day retention ([retention](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/manage-claude/api-and-data-retention.md)) | Run production evidence through a zero-retention organization with online calls only; keep evals and batches in a separate organization; state the terms in the customer agreement |
| Residency and failover | `inference_geo` offers only global or US (at 1.1x), and Haiku 4.5 rejects it ([residency](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/manage-claude/data-residency.md)). Bedrock serves the same models but not Message Batches, and Amazon becomes the processor, with its own lifecycle ([Bedrock](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/claude-in-amazon-bedrock.md)). Priority Tier is no longer sold ([service tiers](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/api/service-tiers.md)) | Use Bedrock as the one secondary platform, for online calls; pause batch work during a failover. Log `usage.inference_geo` per call as location evidence. Treat the spend-cap 429, which carries no retry-after, as a reason to park work, not to retry |
| Embeddings | `voyage-3-large` has been legacy since 15 Jan 2026; `voyage-4-large` costs $0.12 per million tokens against $0.18 and supports 1,024 dimensions ([lifecycle](https://www.mongodb.com/docs/voyageai/models/lifecycle/), [pricing](https://docs.voyageai.com/docs/pricing)). Self-serve Voyage data may be used for training unless the organization opts out ([MongoDB](https://www.mongodb.com/docs/atlas/tutorial/manage-organization-settings/)) | Re-embed with `voyage-4-large`, keeping the `vector(1024)` schema; opt out of training; list MongoDB as a subprocessor |
| Tracing | OpenTelemetry's GenAI conventions are still in Development and capture no message content by default ([conventions](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/open-telemetry/semantic-conventions-genai/main/docs/gen-ai/README.md)). Self-hosting Langfuse means running ClickHouse; its cloud plans cost $29 to $2,499 a month ([Langfuse](https://langfuse.com/docs/open-source), [Terraform module](https://github.com/langfuse/langfuse-terraform-gcp)) | Keep Langfuse Cloud as a view, sending production tenants' metadata only (ids, hashes, token counts, latency, cost and scores); full content goes only from the eval organization's synthetic runs. The run log stays the system of record (3.4). Pin the conventions version CCP emits |

### 3.8 What the evidence does not settle

- Most of the agent evidence comes from the vendors' own internal evals, much of it on Claude models. That transfers to CCP better than to other model families, but none of it was measured on compliance tasks, so CCP must measure on its own.
- There are no public results for multi-agent designs on compliance tasks.
- No source says which reproducibility standard auditors will accept for AI-produced verdicts: replaying the stored output, or agreement across re-runs (pass^k), and at what threshold.
- No source publishes how often Claude verdicts flip across re-runs now that sampling runs at temperature 1.0, or how often the 5.5 models refuse benign security evidence. CCP must measure both on its golden set before it migrates.
- Three sources could not be opened: Google's agent whitepapers (Sep 2024 and Nov 2025) and OpenAI's "Harness engineering" post (Feb 2026).

## 4. Deterministic vs agentic decision framework

The thesis holds, with four refinements. Deterministic code should establish the facts, and it must also make the decisions. Facts a model reads out of a document are candidates until code verifies them. Agents should propose, not act. And every decision must record its method and be allowed to abstain. Refined: **code decides from verified facts; models read, extract, draft and investigate; people approve anything consequential; every decision records its method, and any step may abstain.**

### 4.1 The thesis, tested

| Part of the thesis | Holds? | Refinement | Evidence |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| "Deterministic systems establish verifiable facts" | Partly | Code must also decide: a pass or fail is a versioned function over facts, so the rule version is part of the record | CCP's pure rules (1.2); Drata labels AI summaries "assistive, not authoritative" (2.1) |
| Facts come from systems | Not always | A field a model reads from a document is a candidate fact until code checks that its quote supports it | CCP's presence-only citation check (1.3); no incumbent documents machine-checked citations (2.1) |
| "Agents interpret, investigate" | Yes, if bounded | Investigation and research are where agents earn their cost. Interpretation that sets a status ends in a rule | Anthropic and OpenAI guidance (3.1); Sprinto's staged questionnaire pipeline (2.1) |
| "and act on those facts within controlled boundaries" | No | Boundaries inside an agent are not enough. Agents propose; a deterministic executor acts only on an approved payload | Comp AI's denylist and regeneration after approval (2.2); "writes stay single-threaded" (3.2) |
| Not stated: method and abstention | Add | Every decision records its rule, model, prompt and evidence versions; insufficient facts give needs\_review | Drata withholds a recommendation when evidence is inconclusive (2.1); pass^k (Section 5) |

### 4.2 Choosing the execution model

> **Diagram:** Decision path · four questions, five outcomes, one abstain rule (rendered in ccp-target-architecture.pdf).

Tiers 1 and 2 are CCP's existing evaluator tiers. Question 4 applies to every outcome, including deterministic ones: a rule that would publish to a customer still goes through the approval stage when its effect is consequential. Autonomy is earned later, by measurement (4.4).

### 4.3 Workflow by workflow

| Workflow | Execution model | Why | Who decides |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Integration setup | Deterministic | Connecting an account is a fixed protocol (OAuth or a cross-account role); a model adds risk and nothing else | The customer authorizes; code verifies the connection |
| API evidence collection | Deterministic | Structured data that must be complete, timestamped and reproducible | Code, with a completeness record per sync |
| Test execution | Deterministic (tier 1) | A rule over stored observations is cheap, explainable and replayable | A versioned rule |
| Identity matching | Deterministic scoring; people for the unclear band | The features are explainable, and a wrong merge can hide a terminated user | Code within thresholds; a reviewer between them |
| Policy interpretation | Extract-then-rule (tier 2) | The question fits a schema, and quotes can be checked | A rule over cited fields; a failed citation gives needs\_review |
| Evidence review of uploads | Extract-then-rule plus a verifier | Admissibility breaks into checkable fields: dates, scope, issuer | A rule; a reviewer below the confidence threshold |
| Questionnaires | Extract-then-rule for answers; a person sends | Answers must cite evidence, and they go to a customer | Code checks citations; a person approves what is sent |
| Control mapping | Agent proposes, person approves | Judgement over catalogues; one wrong mapping affects every verdict that uses it | A mapping reviewer |
| Risk assessment | Agent drafts, person decides | Accepting a risk is a management decision with a named owner | The risk owner |
| Exception handling | Person decides; code enforces | An exception is an accountable decision with an expiry | A person; code enforces the expiry |
| Investigation | Bounded read-only agent | The path is unknown in advance; the output is a cited proposal | No decision: it changes no status |
| Audit prep | Code assembles; an agent lists gaps | The package must be reproducible as of a date; finding gaps is open-ended | Code builds the package; a person submits it |
| Remediation | Agent proposes; a person approves the payload; code executes | It writes to customer systems | A person approves; a deterministic executor acts |
| Connector drafting | Agent drafts offline; tests and a reviewer gate it | Generated code can be checked, as Drata's 1,000 generated tests show (2.1) | The conformance harness and a reviewer |

### 4.4 Earning more autonomy

A workflow moves to lighter human review only on measured evidence, never by default:

- **Accuracy.** The false-pass rate on the expert-labelled set is below the agreed limit, with confidence intervals (5.2).
- **Consistency.** pass^k meets the target over repeated runs on the same evidence (Section 5).
- **Reviewer signal.** Blind, sampled re-review by an expert agrees with the workflow at the agreed false-accept bound. The rate of approvals without changes is tracked only to detect rubber-stamping, never as evidence of correctness (Ramp, 2.3).
- **Reversibility.** Only reversible actions graduate. Irreversible ones, such as revoking access or publishing to customers, keep per-item approval, as Vanta's Auto Mode does (2.1).

A regression demotes the workflow automatically: when a metric falls below its gate, review returns to the previous level.

## 5. Evals and guardrails

Both are built before any new agent ships, and both are enforced in code. Graders decide what passes, a policy gateway decides what an agent may do, and model-based defenses only raise flags. The research is consistent on why: published injection defenses fail against adaptive attackers, and model judges miss many defects, so neither can be the control that prevents a false pass. 6.6 adds what frontier practice in 2026 changes: harness edits treated as releases, judges that never see the agent's reasoning, evidence kept out of the user turn, and traces that carry no evidence text.

> **Diagram:** Eval pipeline · four gates, production monitoring, and the loop back to datasets (rendered in ccp-target-architecture.pdf).

A change to a prompt, model, tool schema or retrieval setting must clear all four gates. Production checks every run cheaply, judges a sample nightly, and sends confirmed failures back into the registry as new cases.

### 5.1 Graders and where each can be trusted

| Grader | Use it for | Do not use it for | Evidence |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Code assertions | Verdict against the expert label, final state, schema, citation span and typed value checks, tool allowlist, tenant scoping, no write before approval | Judging prose quality | Grade "what the agent produced, not the path it took" ([Anthropic, Jan 2026](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/demystifying-evals-for-ai-agents)) |
| Model judges | Prose quality and narrow binary defect detectors, each with a reference answer, an "unknown" option and a judge from a different model (a different family offline; a different Claude model on customer data) | Verdicts and close calls | GPT-4 agreed with experts 85% of the time but held its answer under position swaps only 65% of the time ([Zheng et al.](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2306.05685v4)); judges caught only 30-60% of inconsistent summaries ([Yan](https://eugeneyan.com/writing/llm-evaluators/)) |
| Entailment checkers | A second line on narrative answers: contradicted claims are rejected, "not enough information" goes to review | Sole acceptance gate | The best grounded fact-checkers average about 75-80% on mixed benchmarks ([LLM-AggreFact](https://llm-aggrefact.github.io/)); NLI citation checks reach kappa 0.53-0.70 against humans ([ALCE](https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.14627)) |
| Human labels | Gold sets, judge calibration (100-200 labels per failure mode, TPR and TNR reported separately), weekly error analysis | Volume checks a computer can do | Below 60 labels the confidence intervals are too wide ([Husain](https://hamel.dev/blog/posts/llm-judge/)); trained annotators agree only moderately on attribution ([AIS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2112.12870)) |

### 5.2 What to measure

- **Correctness against an expert label,** split into false PASS and false FAIL. A false PASS that reaches an auditor or a customer's trust page is the most expensive error, so it carries the highest cost weight.
- **Consistency.** pass^k, the chance that all k runs of a case succeed, plus verdict flips on unchanged evidence. In [τ-bench](https://arxiv.org/html/2406.12045), a model with over 60% single-run success fell below 25% at k = 8, and auditors would see that as flapping.
- **Escalation as a graded behaviour:** coverage, error among automatic decisions, over-escalation and under-escalation. Reasoning-tuned models abstain 24% less ([AbstentionBench](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09038)), so model upgrades must gate on under-escalation.
- **Grounding:** share of citations whose span verifies, share passing the typed value check, and entailment results.
- **Tool use:** right tool, right arguments (tenant and date ranges included), and invariants such as approval-skipped = 0.
- **Security:** attack success and utility under attack, per model, for static and adaptive attacks.
- **Cost and speed:** tokens, cost per completed task, p95 latency, and cost per tenant.

Statistics matter at this scale. Compare versions on the same cases with paired differences and standard errors clustered by tenant or source document, which can be over three times naive errors ([Anthropic](https://www.anthropic.com/research/statistical-approach-to-model-evals)). Count errored and unscored runs as failures. CCP's current gate, which blocks on any single flip, would fire about 92% of the time on noise alone (1 − 0.95^50) with 50 cases that are each 95% reliable.

### 5.3 Gates through the lifecycle

| Stage | What runs | Gate rule |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Development | Capability suites that start with a low pass rate, plus error analysis | None; this is where quality is built |
| PR CI | Keyless deterministic and replayed cases; citation, schema and safety invariants | Any failure blocks |
| Nightly | Real models, 5 runs per case, regression suites, static and adaptive attacks | Block a release when the lower confidence bound drops below the agreed margin |
| Pre-migration | Full paired comparison whenever a model, judge, prompt, tool schema or retrieval setting changes | Written sign-off; under-escalation gated. Anthropic commits each model for about a year and then gives at least 60 days' notice ([deprecations](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/about-claude/model-deprecations.md)), so expect about one forced migration per model tier per year |
| Shadow | A new workflow or model runs beside human reviewers and changes nothing | Launch only at the agreed false-accept rate |
| Production | Cheap checks on every run, model judges on a nightly sample, 50-100 traces reviewed weekly | Alerts on confidence bounds, not point estimates |

Seed each suite with 20-50 cases from real failures, then grow to 100-200 per failure mode, labelled by one named domain expert with a second annotator on about 20% for a human ceiling. Every suite needs negative controls: an empty, refused or do-nothing output must fail. Flawed grading changed one benchmark score from 42% to about 95% ([Anthropic](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/demystifying-evals-for-ai-agents)), so graders are tested too. Vanta's pattern is worth copying: score the evidence verifier by agreement with the auditor's accept-or-flag decision ([Vanta](https://www.vanta.com/resources/build-verifiers-for-messy-problems)).

Keep datasets, graders, rubrics and thresholds in the repo in a vendor-neutral format. Hosted eval products are churning: OpenAI's Evals platform shuts down on 30 Nov 2026 ([deprecations](https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/deprecations)), and Google's recommended Gen AI evaluation SDK is still in Preview.

### 5.4 Citation verification, hardened

CCP's checker confirms that a quote exists, not that it supports the value: on the repo's own fixtures, a training record marked "In progress" verified as completed and passed. The target is a layered, fail-closed check:

1. **Provenance at ingest.** Every document version is hashed, and each citation is stored as a span selector: document version, hash, offsets and the exact text with context ([W3C annotation model](https://www.w3.org/TR/annotation-model/)).
2. **Span match.** Exact match after normalization, or the same tokens in the same order. No bag-of-words fallback, and no ellipsis splicing across sentences.
3. **Typed value check, mandatory for every cited field.** Status values match a lexicon with an exclusivity rule and a label anchor; dates parse to ISO and must be equal; numbers and frequencies are compared in canonical units; negation cues route to review ([negation is underrepresented in NLI data](https://aclanthology.org/2020.emnlp-main.732)).
4. **Entailment as a second line** for narrative claims, judged by a different Claude model inside the zero-retention organization. A contradiction rejects the field; not enough information goes to review.
5. **Rules decide.** An unverified field becomes "needs review", never PASS and never "Not applicable".
6. **People review** with the spans highlighted, measured on 200-300 labelled field-span pairs that include splices, swapped dates and negations. CI allows zero false accepts on the deterministic classes.

### 5.5 Guardrails enforced outside the model

| Control | Where it is enforced | What it stops | Basis |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Tenant binding | The tool gateway takes the tenant from the authenticated session; agent tools use a read-only database role under row-level security | An injected or guessed id reading another customer's data | [OWASP LLM06](https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm062025-excessive-agency/): authorize downstream, not in the model |
| Rule of Two per session | A session that has read untrusted content and holds private data loses act-class tools; a taint flag switches them off. Propose-class tools stay, because every proposal waits for a person, and the rule allows all three properties when the agent "requires supervision" | A vendor PDF steering an agent to flip a verdict or leak data | [Meta](https://ai.meta.com/blog/practical-ai-agent-security/), [Willison](https://simonwillison.net/2025/Jun/16/the-lethal-trifecta/), [Google](https://storage.googleapis.com/gweb-research2023-media/pubtools/1018686.pdf) |
| Tool classes | Every tool is labelled read, propose or act by us, never by trusting a server's hints; strict schemas, budgets, rate limits | Tool misuse and runaway loops | [MCP tools spec](https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/latest/server/tools): annotations from untrusted servers are untrusted |
| Models extract, code decides | Extraction calls have no tools and run per chunk; rules decide; every verdict is recorded inside CCP with its method, and a tier-3 draft awaiting review shows `needs_review`; an AI-derived PASS is never asserted outside CCP (a trust page, an auditor package, a sent questionnaire) without review until the test graduates (4.4) | A poisoned chunk corrupting more than its own fields | [Design patterns for securing agents](https://arxiv.org/html/2506.08837v3) |
| Approvals | Code writes the diff and risk summary; the approval binds a payload hash and a real approver, who must differ from the requester for high-impact actions; approvals are capped per session | Approval fatigue and model-written justifications | [OWASP agentic top 10](https://genai.owasp.org/download/52117/?tmstv=1765059207), [Microsoft failure taxonomy](https://cdn-dynmedia-1.microsoft.com/is/content/microsoftcorp/microsoft/final/en-us/microsoft-brand/documents/Taxonomy-of-Failure-Mode-in-Agentic-AI-Systems-Whitepaper.pdf) |
| Credential broker | Agents and runners never hold real credentials; a broker checks the approved operation and issues a short-lived, single-action credential; pull requests preferred | A compromised workflow acting with standing access | [Anthropic sandboxing](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/claude-code-sandboxing) |
| Egress proxy | No server-side fetch of model-chosen URLs; one proxy blocks private and metadata ranges, checks redirects and pins DNS | Server-side request forgery, if a future feature fetches URLs that a customer or a model chooses. CCP has none today; until one exists, no tool fetches a URL | [MCP security best practices](https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/latest/basic/security_best_practices) |
| Render sanitization | AI text is shown with no images, only cited or allowlisted links, and an HTML allowlist | Data leaking through crafted image URLs | [Google](https://storage.googleapis.com/gweb-research2023-media/pubtools/1018686.pdf) |
| Provenance on content | Each chunk carries its source, author permission, uploader, hash and trust tier; GitHub text from users without write access is dropped | Outsiders planting instructions in issues or tags | [GitHub Copilot agent](https://docs.github.com/en/copilot/concepts/agents/coding-agent/risks-and-mitigations) |
| Memory hygiene | Vector namespaces keyed by organization id; AI output never re-ingested as trusted; approved answers labelled, with expiry and rollback | Self-reinforcing poisoning of the answer library | OWASP agentic top 10, ASI06 |
| Audit trail | Every tool call, decision, approval and execution is an append-only record linked to the result | Unexplainable findings | CCP grants only insert on history tables ([first report](https://claude.ai/artifact/WRau5vs9uFjkgBqzbC1Arj), A.5) |

Model-based defenses still run, but as signals: evidence fences with datamarking, an injection classifier on chunks, and a reviewer model that sees only the request and the proposed action. A hit lowers confidence or routes to review; it never grants a PASS. The numbers explain why. Restricting tools before untrusted data arrives cut one model's attack success from 47.7% to 6.8% ([AgentDojo](https://agentdojo.spylab.ai/results/)). Adaptive attacks broke 12 published defenses at over 90% success ([Nasr et al.](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.09023)), and even Anthropic's best browser-agent setup still let 1.4% of attacks through ([Anthropic](https://www.anthropic.com/research/prompt-injection-defenses)).

## 6. Target architecture

The target keeps one rule above all others: code decides compliance status from verified facts, models read, extract, draft and investigate, and people approve anything that changes a customer's status or systems. Every result records who decided, by what method, and from which evidence.

> **Diagram:** Target architecture · 11 layers in two rows plus cross-cutting services (rendered in ccp-target-architecture.pdf).

The top row turns source data into facts with provenance; the bottom row reads those facts through one tool gateway and can only propose.
