Approved changes go back to the customer's systems as a pull request or a narrowly scoped role call, and the next sync re-checks them like any other change. 6.4 to 6.13 settle the agent side layer by layer: the agent stack, retrieval, evals and guardrails, context and long-running work, multi-agent work and the harness, judges and training, and the screens, with a coverage map, the coverage engine and the tools at the end.

### 6.1 Layer by layer

"Keep" cells describe code verified at `3386899`; "Change or add" cells are proposals, detailed in the roadmap (Section 8). External facts carry their links.

| Layer | Keep | Change or add | Technology and why | Alternatives rejected |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1. Integration and connector infrastructure | One `SourceEvent` contract for fixture and live sources, with provider ids, provider time and untouched payloads (`connectors/base.py:26-54`); a pinned GitHub API version (`connectors/github/client.py:45`) | Connections as data, checked daily. Sync runs of declared scopes that end complete, partial, failed or not visible; absence only from a complete scope. A per-endpoint error table and a conformance harness with negative controls. Content-hash snapshots; rate buckets per (tenant, account, region, service). Agent read tools on the same clients | An in-repo connector kit over httpx and boto3 in standard retry mode, which AWS recommends for clients serving many tenants ([AWS](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sdkref/latest/guide/feature-retry-behavior.html)). A GitHub App, whose tokens "expire after 1 hour" and can be limited to named repositories ([GitHub](https://docs.github.com/en/apps/creating-github-apps/authenticating-with-a-github-app/generating-an-installation-access-token-for-a-github-app)). CloudFormation and StackSets | Unified-API vendors on the core evidence path: they hold credentials and drop provider ids (inferred). Events as evidence: Config emits them "on a best effort basis" ([AWS](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/config/latest/developerguide/monitor-config-with-cloudwatchevents.html)). Third-party MCP servers: their annotations are untrusted ([MCP](https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/2026-07-28/server/tools)). Treating a 404 as empty |
| 2. Evidence ingestion and normalization | Land before indexing; dedup on provider ids (`connectors/base.py:44-46`); pure mappers; append-only observations; identity scoring with a review queue (`storage/migrations/0004_identity.sql:30-44`) | Identity and indexing on the change path (today full runs only, `compliance_state/run.py:66-67`). A poison record is dead-lettered and marks its sync partial instead of stalling the topic (`ingest/consumers.py:61-72`). Absence only from complete scopes. `mapper_version`, `ts_kind`, `sync_run_id` and `recorded_seq` on observations. Secrets dropped before landing. Upload tenant from the session (`connectors/manual/uploads.py:88`) | The Postgres `event_log` stays the transactional outbox | Kafka or change-data capture: a second log with no throughput need (inferred). Correcting observations in place |
| 3. Pointers carry the version id, a hash of the bytes received (today a re-serialization, `ingest/raw_zone.py:62`) and the provider's request id. A KMS key per tenant. Personal identifiers in erasable side tables, so the chain holds only commitments | Event holds, launched 8 Sep 2026 "at no additional charge" ([AWS](https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/09/amazon-s3-object-lock-variable-retention/)); SSE-KMS with Bucket Keys ([AWS](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/bucket-key.html)); checkpoints modelled on CloudTrail's signed digests ([AWS](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/awscloudtrail/latest/userguide/cloudtrail-log-file-validation-intro.html)); tenant-first keys because key checks "always bypass row security" ([PostgreSQL](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/ddl-rowsecurity.html)) | A managed ledger: QLDB ended on 31 Jul 2025 ([InfoQ, 26 Jul 2024](https://www.infoq.com/news/2024/07/aws-kill-qldb)). Compliance mode for raw data: irreversible short of closing the account ([AWS](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/object-lock.html)). A transparency-log service before buyers ask. Bitemporal entity tables for now |
| 4. Deterministic compliance evaluation engine | Tiers fixed at authoring; pure rules; extract-then-rule; one verdict per key (`storage/migrations/0005_state.sql:34`); `needs_review` as a fact; the clock producer; the rollup rule | Verdict and announcement in one transaction (`evaluators/runner.py:93-106`). Rule and parameter versions in the key, then pinned inputs. A required unknown input gives `needs_review`. Coverage and freshness in the rollup. Impact table as data. Tier 1 never parks on model budget. Rule changes with preview and notice. Long-tail checks as versioned YAML | Python rules and a small, total predicate interpreter, shipped in the release bundle behind the gates (proposed) | Model-returned statuses. Rego or CEL now: a second language to audit (inferred). Checks as rows edited at runtime (inferred). AWS conformance packs as the authority: AWS has no SOC 2 equivalent and points customers to partners ([AWS](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/audit-manager/latest/userguide/audit-manager-availability-change.html)) |
| 5. Agent execution and orchestration | The MIT LangGraph library; four graphs over one core with retry and timeout policies (`spec.md` A-52); budgets; checkpoints under row-level security; "graphs read, runners write" | Agent runs as queue jobs with deterministic thread ids (today random, `agent/tier3.py:157-158`) and synchronous checkpoints. Every model and tool call recorded, so resumes never re-sample. The Postgres run log as the record (today tool loops keep the first request and last response only, `agent/core.py:328-330`). Bundles pin runs. Runs end with a proposal. One investigation per evidence state. One writer | LangGraph on the hardened Postgres queue. LangGraph defaults to `async` checkpoints, which can lose the last step ([LangGraph](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/langchain-ai/langgraph/main/libs/langgraph/langgraph/pregel/main.py)). DBOS only after its spike and a trigger | DBOS as the default (Decision 4). A second reader before any AI-derived PASS. A refusal gives `needs_review` (today terminal, `llm/anthropic_client.py:255-256`). Code renders fixes from templates. Agent reads land as evidence. New workflows: evidence review, the AWS Investigator, questionnaires from live verdicts, mapping, audit prep, offline check drafting | Claude through `LLMClient` with structured outputs, generally available on every platform ([Anthropic](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/structured-outputs.md)); Haiku 5.5 with thinking off for extraction ([Anthropic](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/models/haiku-5-5/migration-guide.md)), Sonnet 5.5 for agents, Opus 5.5 where measured; Claude's PDF input for scans, zero-retention eligible except Covered Models ([Anthropic](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/pdf-support)); `voyage-4-large` at $0.12 per million tokens ([Voyage](https://docs.voyageai.com/docs/pricing)) | Native Citations with structured outputs: a 400 ([Anthropic](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/citations.md)). The single-flip gate (Section 5.2) |

Guardrails, human approval and audit trails | One approval stage; the payload hash (`approvals/store.py:22-24`); dry run by default; five live gates (`remediation/gates.py:53-84`); target validation; 21 guardrails, each with a test | Fail closed now (first report, Q2). Then signed-in reviewers with roles; one validated decision per item; payload and precondition hashes; expiry; separation of duties; single-use grants; an append-only journal; rollback as a new proposal; code-written risk summaries. The same service for identity merges, scope changes, break-glass, and exceptions with an expiry, shown as "fail (excepted until D)" and never as PASS. A cap on automated status changes. Passkey-signed approvals for the cloud write path | Cognito, which federates customers' SAML and OIDC providers and redirects "silently" given `idp_identifier` ([AWS](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cognito/latest/developerguide/cognito-user-pools-identity-federation.html)); later WebAuthn Level 3, whose signed client data carries the server's challenge ([W3C, 25 Aug 2026](https://www.w3.org/TR/webauthn-3/)) | Typed names (`storage/migrations/0011_remediation_approvals.sql:50`). Approvals held in engine state. Bulk approval. SAML built in-house (inferred). WorkOS or Auth0: a new subprocessor (inferred). Keycloak: a stateful system to run (inferred) |
| 10. Observability and production monitoring | One step logger with OpenTelemetry GenAI names; the record first, sinks second; replay without a vendor; cost per step | The Postgres run log as the record, with the dated model id, the reported model, API and SDK versions, platform and `usage.inference_geo` on every call. The price table refuses unknown models (today $0, `observability/cost.py:43-50`). Langfuse gets metadata only for production. A fixed failure taxonomy. Correctness objectives. `ccp explain` and `ccp verify` | OpenTelemetry with the conventions version pinned, since they are still in Development ([OpenTelemetry](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/open-telemetry/semantic-conventions-genai/main/docs/gen-ai/README.md)); Langfuse Cloud as the view, Pro at $199 a month ([Langfuse](https://langfuse.com/pricing)); CloudWatch | A tracing vendor as the evidence store. Self-hosted Langfuse, which means running ClickHouse ([Langfuse](https://langfuse.com/docs/open-source)). Another APM vendor (inferred) |
| 11. Customer-facing product workflows | The command line for operators; the Copilot's status contract | REST generated from the registry. UI v1: connect GitHub and AWS with the checks visible; the rollup with coverage and freshness; findings with evidence spans and a method card; an approvals inbox with the exact payload and hash; uploads with instant verifier feedback; an as-of export; the Copilot. Five status labels (pass, fail, partial, needs review, not assessed), each with a provenance badge (system-observed, customer-attested, AI-read and code-verified). Rule-change notices. Later: an auditor workspace, attestations, read-only MCP, an autonomy console, published quality results | FastAPI, built on Pydantic and OpenAPI ([FastAPI](https://fastapi.tiangolo.com/)), so CCP's models become the REST contract; Jinja and HTMX for server-rendered pages (proposed); Cognito | A React single-page app for v1: a second toolchain for about six screens (inferred). Django: its ORM and user model beside `ScopedDB` (inferred). Dashboards that compute status apart from the rollup. Auto-publishing every PASS |

### 6.2 The AWS example, end to end

A customer connects AWS, a deterministic check finds a bucket open to the public, the Investigator finds the cause, a person approves an exact fix, and the fix is re-checked like any other change. Every step leaves a record that links back to the evidence.

> **Diagram:** AWS example · 15 steps across six parties, one approval (rendered in ccp-target-architecture.pdf).

| Step | Who | What happens | What is recorded | Guard |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Customer | Launches CCP's CloudFormation stack, which creates a read role that trusts CCP under an external ID that CCP generated. An AWS Organization adds a stack in the management account and a StackSet for member accounts | Connection: tenant, account, external ID, stack version | One random external ID per account, generated by the vendor ([AWS](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/IAM/latest/UserGuide/id_roles_common-scenarios_third-party.html)); StackSets never deploy to the management account ([AWS](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AWSCloudFormation/latest/UserGuide/stacksets-orgs-associate-stackset-with-org.html)) |
| 2 | Connector | Assumes the role with the external ID, which must succeed, and without it, which must fail; lists the role's policies | Onboarding checks, as events | Refuse a role that works without the ID or that holds write actions. Use SecurityAudit plus explicit denies on reads that expose secrets, such as Lambda environment variables and EC2 user data ([Datadog](https://securitylabs.datadoghq.com/articles/securely-integrating-with-customers-aws-accounts/)) |
| 3 | Connector | Reads each bucket's public-access block and policy status, the account's public-access block, and, where the customer records AWS Config, the bucket's configuration items, paging through results with standard retries | Raw pages written once, with a hash, the provider's capture time or CCP's observation time, and a completeness record for the run | AWS API quotas are shared with the customer's own automation ([AWS](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/ec2/latest/devguide/ec2-api-throttling.html)), so a rate limit applies per tenant, account and region. A 404 `NoSuchPublicAccessBlockConfiguration` on the account means "not configured", a real fact ([AWS](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonS3/latest/API/API_control_GetPublicAccessBlock.html)); AccessDenied means "not visible", and the test returns `needs_review` |
| 4 | Connector | Maps pages to `Asset` observations, redacting secrets before anything is stored | Append-only observations with evidence ids | A missing resource becomes a deletion only from a complete run |
| 5 | Rules | The changed bucket re-runs the test "S3 buckets block public access"; the rule takes each flag from the bucket or the account, whichever blocks, reads the policy status, and returns FAIL | A verdict with its rule version, evidence ids and as-of times, and a bitemporal state row | Code decides; no model is involved |
| 6 | Rules | A new FAIL, or changed evidence behind a failing verdict, starts at most one bounded Investigator run per evidence state; when the rule's reasons already explain the failure, code writes the investigation instead | A run record with its harness version and budgets | Turn, token, cost and time budgets |
| 7 | Investigator | Calls read tools through the gateway: Config history in 7-day windows, a CloudTrail lookup of who changed the setting, and a search of the customer's infrastructure code for the bucket | Every provider response lands in the raw zone through the connector's client (rate limit, redaction, request id) before the model sees it, and every call is in the run log; code, not the agent, can then back-date the FAIL to when CloudTrail or Config shows the change | The policy check allows only the read class; the tenant comes from the session; CloudTrail lookups stay under its limit of 2 per second ([AWS](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/awscloudtrail/latest/APIReference/API_LookupEvents.html)); tags and names are treated as untrusted input |
| 8 | Investigator | Proposes a probable cause with cited evidence and picks a catalogue action with its parameters (repository, file, resource address); code renders the Terraform change from a versioned template, validates it offline and checks that it touches only this bucket | An append-only proposal with a payload hash | Code checks every citation; the agent cannot write anywhere and never writes the diff that is approved |
| 9 | Approvals | Shows a reviewer the failing evidence, the cause, the exact change and its hash | None | The reviewer is authenticated on the server and holds rights for this tenant and action; the request carries the payload hash, a precondition hash (the FAIL is still the head, the bucket's state, the base commit) and an expiry, and the approver is not the requester and owns the repository on the customer's side |
| 10 | Customer | Approves | A decision: reviewer, decision, payload hash, time | An unknown decision is rejected; a replayed or changed payload is refused; an expired request is refused, and a decision mints one single-use execution grant |
| 11 | Executor | Opens a pull request in the customer's repository with exactly the approved change, through CCP's GitHub App | A receipt: pull request link, commit, payload hash | The idempotency key is the proposal id plus the hash. The executor consumes the grant, re-checks the precondition and refuses on drift. It uses an installation token limited to this repository, with contents and pull-request write, which expires after 1 hour ([GitHub](https://docs.github.com/en/apps/creating-github-apps/authenticating-with-a-github-app/generating-an-installation-access-token-for-a-github-app)). CCP holds no AWS write credentials on this path, as with Drata's and Vanta's pull-request fixes ([Drata](https://help.drata.com/en/articles/9930785-drata-compliance-as-code), [Vanta](https://developer.vanta.com/docs/quickstart/remediate-with-mcp.md)) |
| 12 | Customer | Reviews and merges the pull request; their pipeline applies it | Nothing in CCP | The customer's own change process |
| 13 | Connector | Re-reads the bucket after the merge or at the next scheduled sync | A new raw page and observation | An event only triggers a re-read and is never evidence itself; AWS Config events are best-effort ([AWS](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/config/latest/developerguide/monitor-config-with-cloudwatchevents.html)) |
| 14 | Rules | The rule returns PASS | A new verdict and state row supersede the FAIL; history keeps both | As-of queries show what was true, and what CCP believed, at any time |
| 15 | Approvals | Links the receipt to the PASS verdict | One chain: evidence, FAIL, run, proposal, approval, pull request, PASS | A per-tenant hash chain over the raw objects and every database row in this chain (verdicts, state rows, the run, the proposal, the approval, the receipt), with an hourly signed checkpoint written to a compliance-mode bucket in a separate AWS account, modelled on CloudTrail's digest files ([AWS](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/awscloudtrail/latest/userguide/cloudtrail-log-file-validation-intro.html)), lets an auditor check the evidence without trusting CCP's database |

**When the bucket is not managed in code.** An opt-in remediator role, deployed by the customer as a separate stack, may run one allowlisted AWS-owned runbook, such as `AWSConfigRemediation-ConfigureS3BucketPublicAccessBlock`, which verifies its own result ([AWS](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/systems-manager-automation-runbooks/latest/userguide/automation-aws-block-public-s3-bucket.html)). CCP assumes it only after approval, for 15 minutes, with a session policy limited to the target resource and the approval id as `SourceIdentity`, so the customer's CloudTrail shows which approval each action served ([AWS](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/STS/latest/APIReference/API_AssumeRole.html)). The executor re-reads live state first and refuses if it no longer matches the state recorded in the approval. CCP never uses AWS Config auto-remediation, which can act on stale compliance data ([AWS](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/config/latest/developerguide/setup-autoremediation.html)). No competitor documents a vendor-held write role ([Drata](https://help.drata.com/en/articles/8981943-aws-organizational-units-connection), [Secureframe](https://support.secureframe.com/en/articles/15111264-comply-ai-cloud-remediation-test-guidance)), so this path ships only when a design partner asks for it, and its approvals are signed with a passkey (Decision 5).

**What not to build on.** AWS Audit Manager closed to new accounts on 30 Apr 2026 ([AWS](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/audit-manager/latest/userguide/audit-manager-availability-change.html)) and CloudTrail Lake on 31 May 2026 ([AWS](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/awscloudtrail/latest/userguide/cloudtrail-lake-service-availability-change.html)). Security Hub deletes findings that have not been updated for 90 days, and the IAM credential report keeps only its latest copy ([Security Hub](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/securityhub/latest/userguide/securityhub-findings.html), [IAM](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/IAM/latest/UserGuide/id_credentials_getting-report.html)), so CCP snapshots both into its own raw zone when it reads them.

### 6.3 Cross-cutting concerns

**Multi-tenancy.**

(proposed) One pooled cell per region by default (a database, a raw bucket, a deployment), isolated by forced row-level security, the raising tenant guard and a non-owner application role. Before real data lands: tenant-first composite keys and tenant-scoped cache keys, because unique and foreign-key checks bypass row security ([PostgreSQL](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/ddl-rowsecurity.html)) and today a state row can reference any tenant's verdict (`storage/migrations/0005_state.sql:51`) while both caches are keyed without the tenant (`storage/migrations/0008_chunks.sql:36`, `:44`); a catalogue lint with a deliberately permissive policy as its negative control; and the tenant set inside each transaction everywhere, including the checkpoint saver, which today passes it as a connection startup option (`storage/checkpoints.py:64-68`) that PgBouncer's transaction mode rejects or drops ([PgBouncer](https://www.pgbouncer.org/features.html)). Use PgBouncer, not RDS Proxy, which pins a session on every `set_config` ([AWS](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/rds-proxy-pinning.html)). Search each tenant's chunks exactly by default: pgvector filters "after the index is scanned", so small tenants get short results from a shared index ([pgvector](https://github.com/pgvector/pgvector)), and exact search returns the same order every time, which replay needs (inferred). Answer of 9 Nov 2026: each tenant gets its own HNSW partition from the start instead, and replay reads the stored candidate list rather than searching again (6.5, U13). Require pgvector 0.8.2 or later for CVE-2026-3172, a cross-relation leak ([EDB, 10 Mar 2026](https://www.enterprisedb.com/docs/security/assessments/cve-2026-3172)). The tenant is bound in code on every AI path: closures for graphs, the session for the policy check, the signed-in token for MCP. Tenant evidence sits after the prompt-cache breakpoint (`spec.md` A-62); caches are isolated per workspace on the Claude API but only per organization on Bedrock ([Anthropic](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/prompt-caching.md)). No standing staff access; break-glass goes through the approval service. A cross-tenant canary runs in production. Silo cells (own database, bucket and key) on contract, because a tenant-specific storage key is possible only in a silo ([AWS, Apr 2024](https://docs.aws.eu/prescriptive-guidance/latest/saas-multitenant-managed-postgresql/matrix.html)). Vanta's 2025 cross-customer exposure came from "a product code change" ([TechCrunch, 2 Jun 2025](https://techcrunch.com/2025/06/02/vanta-bug-exposed-customers-data-to-other-customers/)); CCP's raising guard turns that class of bug into an error.

**Scaling from 2 to thousands of tenants.**

| Stage | Tenants | Shape (proposed) | What changes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 0 | 2 to 50 | One RDS PostgreSQL 17 instance (Multi-AZ); two `api` and two `worker` tasks; no pooler | The near-term fixes; about 60 database connections (inferred) |
| 1 | 50 to 500 | A larger instance; four to eight workers scaled on queue age; `event_log` partitioned by time, with processed partitions dropped (it is operational, not evidence) | Fair claiming through a work-hint table and LISTEN/NOTIFY wake-ups, already in place from the medium term (today one loop visits every tenant every 2 seconds, `ingest/worker.py:67-103`) |
| 2 | 500 to 5,000 | PgBouncer in transaction mode; LIST partitions per tenant, each with its own HNSW index from the start (answer to U13), within the "few thousand partitions" Postgres handles well ([PostgreSQL](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/ddl-partitioning.html)); a read replica for exports | Very large tenants move to their own cell |
| 3 | 5,000 and up | Several pooled cells per region; a distributed Postgres only if one cell's database is outgrown, which tenant-first keys keep possible | Tooling to place and move tenants between cells |

(inferred) At 1,000 tenants with about 2,000 (test, subject) pairs each, a 30-day revalidation is about 67,000 evaluations a day plus changes: a few a second, mostly cheap tier-1 SQL. The first limits are model rate limits and spend caps (the Scale tier caps spend at $200,000 a month, and at the cap requests get a 429 with no retry-after, [Anthropic](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/api/rate-limits.md)) and customers' own AWS quotas, which CCP shares with their automation (CloudTrail `LookupEvents` allows 2 requests a second per account and region, [AWS](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/awscloudtrail/latest/APIReference/API_LookupEvents.html)). Content-hash snapshots keep the steady state cheap: an unchanged re-sync renews freshness and writes no new verdicts.

**Fault tolerance.**

| Failure | Behaviour (proposed) |
| --- | --- |
| Worker killed mid-job | The lease expires and another worker claims the job; idempotency keys make the repeat a no-op; an agent run resumes from its last synchronous checkpoint without sampling a model again |
| Crash between a verdict and its announcement | Impossible after the outbox fix (today it strands the verdict, `evaluators/runner.py:93-106`); a reconciler announces any verdict with no state row and reports the count |
| Model outage, 429 or 529 | Backoff with jitter through `available_at` (today a retry is claimable at once, `ingest/event_log.py:110-115`); then AI work parks and tier 1 continues |
| Anthropic spend cap | A 429 with no retry-after ([Anthropic](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/api/rate-limits.md)): park all model work and page; never retry |
| Refusal | `needs_review` with its category, never a verdict and never a dead letter |
| Prolonged Anthropic outage | A rehearsed manual switch to Bedrock for online calls, pre-agreed in each tenant's DPA because Amazon becomes the processor and Bedrock has no Message Batches ([Anthropic](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/claude-in-amazon-bedrock.md)); runs restart on the secondary, because Haiku 5.5 thinking blocks are bound to the account that produced them ([Anthropic](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/models/haiku-5-5/migration-guide.md)) |
| Connector throttling or lost permission | The scope ends partial; dependent tests return `needs_review`; the connection shows degraded; never a false absence |
| Bad mapper release | Dead-letter, mark the sync partial, alert; after the fix, replay from the immutable raw zone |
| Executor crash or drift | A single-use grant, an append-only journal and deterministic branch names make retries idempotent; drift from the approved precondition refuses and asks for a new approval |
| Deploy | Runs pinned to their bundle; stored proposals stay bound to their artifacts and hashes |
| S3 or KMS unavailable | Landing and decryption fail closed; evaluation waits rather than run on evidence it cannot store or verify |
| Altered database history or restore | The next `ccp audit verify` against checkpoints in the separate account fails and pages; attestations freeze; a quarterly restore drill ends with a verify |

**Schema evolution and backward compatibility.**

| Artifact | Versioned by | Compatibility rule (proposed) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Provider payloads | `SourceEvent.schema_version` (`connectors/base.py:35`); raw objects never change | A mapper change ships as a new `mapper_version`; replay from raw appends observations and never rewrites old ones, so old verdicts still point at what they read |
| Database schema | Additive migrations (the runner's existing rule, `spec.md` section 2) | Expand, migrate, contract for renames. Audit rows are hashed over a versioned canonical projection, so a new column never breaks an old hash |
| Rules and declarative checks | Rule version (later the inputs hash) in the evaluation key | A correction re-evaluates history and supersedes with reason `correction`; an amendment applies from its effective date. Preview and notice first; FAIL-to-PASS flips need a named sign-off. Add `rule_change` to the reason check (`storage/migrations/0005_state.sql:57`) |
| Extraction schemas and prompts | `schema_version` on verdicts; prompt files never edited and hash-checked (`spec.md` A-21) | A change makes a new bundle behind the gates; old artifacts stay re-verifiable because verifiers are versioned too |
| Models | Dated ids inside bundles; the API version pinned in code; the model each response reports | About one forced migration per tier per year, since each model's commitment runs about a year and retirement needs at least 60 days' notice ([Anthropic](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/about-claude/model-deprecations.md)). Each is a paired comparison gated on pass^k, refusal rate and under-escalation. Verdicts from a retired model stay reconstructible from stored outputs. An audit-period bundle freeze cannot outlast a retirement notice |
| Connectors and customer roles | Pinned provider API versions (`connectors/github/client.py:45`); recorded responses per version; a template version on each AWS connection | A test that needs a new AWS action returns `needs_review` on an older template instead of failing; daily checks catch AWS's edits to managed policies (SecurityAudit is at v94, edited 30 Sep 2026, [AWS](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/IAM/latest/UserGuide/access_policies_job-functions.html)) |
| Tools | `ToolSpec.version` | Output schemas only gain optional fields; a breaking change gets a new name |
| Agent checkpoints | A graph version on each thread | Threads of a retired version close and re-run; nothing waits inside a checkpoint |
| Embeddings | The model id on every chunk | Re-embed into new rows and switch the index version; keep old rows for replay |
| Framework mappings | A catalogue version with effective dates; control ids are already copied onto each state row at write time (`storage/migrations/0005_state.sql:49`) | Add the mapping version to state rows, so the mapping in force on any past date is known |
| Exports and APIs | A version on each API and export manifest | Readers accept the current and previous versions |

**Cost.**

Model list prices per million tokens, input and output, with the cache-read price in brackets ([Anthropic](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/about-claude/pricing.md)): Haiku 4.5 $1 and $5 ($0.10); Haiku 5.5 $0.10 and $0.50 ($0.01) for prompts up to 100,000 tokens; Sonnet 5.5 $2 and $10 ($0.10); Opus 5.5 $4 and $20 ($0.20). Models from Claude 4.7 on produce about 30% more tokens for the same text. Batches cost half, but keep results 29 days and so fall outside zero retention ([Anthropic](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/batch-processing.md)).

| Unit | Assumption | Estimate |
| --- | --- | --- |
| One tier-2 extraction today | The spec's logged call: 3,120 tokens in, 410 out (`spec.md:1783-1784`) | $0.0052 on Haiku 4.5 |
| The same on Haiku 5.5 | 30% more tokens, thinking off | About $0.0007; about $0.0005 with a cached prefix (inferred) |
| Second reader on an AI-derived PASS | One more extraction, on candidate passes only | About $0.0007 per pass (inferred) |
| One Investigator run | Sonnet 5.5, about 8 calls, about 100,000 cumulative input and 4,000 output tokens | About $0.24 uncached, about $0.10 with most input cached; capped at $0.50 a run (`config.py:91`) (inferred) |
| A 200-question questionnaire | Per question: four Haiku 5.5 extractions, one Sonnet 5.5 draft, one entailment check | About $3 to $5 (inferred) |
| Embeddings | `voyage-4-large` | $0.12 per million tokens ([Voyage](https://docs.voyageai.com/docs/pricing)) |

(inferred) A tenant with 600 tier-2 calls (about $0.40), 30 investigations once they are deduplicated by evidence state ($3 to $7; without that, a 30-day revalidation of every persistent failure buys a new run), 100 Copilot answers ($5 to $15) and 50 document reviews (about $0.50) costs roughly $10 to $25 a month in model spend: about $10,000 to $25,000 a month at 1,000 tenants. Investigations and the Copilot dominate, so investigating once per evidence state, writing rule-explained investigations in code, and caching stable prefixes (possible from 512 tokens on the 5.5 models, against 4,096 on Haiku 4.5, [Anthropic](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/prompt-caching.md)) are the main levers. Infrastructure: KMS keys cost $1 a month each plus up to $2 for rotations ([AWS](https://aws.amazon.com/kms/pricing/)), so about $1,000 to $3,000 a month at 1,000 tenants (inferred); Object Lock adds no fee beyond storage; Langfuse Pro is $199 a month ([Langfuse](https://langfuse.com/pricing)). RDS and Fargate prices are not in the notes, so this report gives no figure for them; they grow with instance size and task count, not tenant count (inferred). Avoided: DBOS Conductor at $99 a month for one million checkpoints, then $50 per million ([DBOS](https://www.dbos.dev/dbos-pricing)); Temporal Cloud from $50 per million actions ([Temporal](https://temporal.io/pricing)); a sandbox (E2B Pro is $150 a month plus usage, [E2B](https://e2b.dev/pricing)); a ClickHouse cluster. Customers pay for AWS Config if they enable it: $0.003 per continuously recorded item and $0.012 per periodic item ([AWS](https://aws.amazon.com/config/pricing/)), which is why event-driven sync is an opt-in add-on.

**Operational complexity and on-call.**

- **Inventory (proposed).** One RDS PostgreSQL 17 (Multi-AZ); S3 buckets in two AWS accounts; KMS keys; one container image run as two ECS services (`api`, `worker`); a load balancer; a Cognito user pool; CloudWatch. Five outside services: Anthropic, Bedrock (standby), Voyage, Langfuse Cloud and GitHub (the App and Actions).
- **Deliberately not added.** Kafka, Redis, Temporal, DBOS (until triggered), Kubernetes, a vector database, self-hosted observability, an MCP gateway, a code sandbox.
- **Pages.** The database is down or failing over; the oldest pending evaluation is older than 30 minutes; dead letters on evaluations or apply jobs pass a threshold; chain verification fails; a replay or executed-versus-approved mismatch; AI work parked for over 30 minutes or the spend cap hit; connection checks failing for many tenants at once; the cross-tenant canary fails. Everything else becomes a ticket.
- **Rotation.** One weekly rotation across the three to five engineers, business hours first; after-hours paging only for database, integrity and apply alerts until a contract requires 24-hour cover. A separate weekly quality review reads 50 to 100 traces, the coverage backlog and monitor alarms.
- **Runbooks.** Replay dead letters; unpark AI work; switch to Bedrock and back; rotate the GitHub App and signing keys; re-verify or revoke a connection; re-map after a mapper bug; roll out a rule correction; migrate a model; offboard a tenant by destroying its key; seal an auditor export; break-glass access.
- **Game days, monthly.** Kill a worker mid-run; fail over the database; simulate an Anthropic outage; corrupt a chain entry in staging and watch the verifier page.
- **Kill switches.** Per workflow to suggestion-only or off; per tool; per tenant AI off; one switch that parks all model work.

**Hosting and product stack.** The options were AWS (ECS Fargate, RDS, S3, KMS, Cognito), GCP (Cloud Run, Cloud SQL), Kubernetes or a platform-as-a-service, and for the UI FastAPI with server-rendered pages, a React single-page app or Django. The choice is AWS on ECS Fargate with RDS PostgreSQL 17, and FastAPI with server-rendered pages for version 1. The AWS connector needs an AWS principal that customers' roles trust, which on AWS needs no stored keys (inferred); S3 event holds, KMS and Bedrock sit in one account (6.1); Fargate runs containers "without having to manage servers or clusters of Amazon EC2 instances" ([AWS](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonECS/latest/developerguide/AWS_Fargate.html)); and FastAPI is built on Pydantic and OpenAPI ([FastAPI](https://fastapi.tiangolo.com/)). The costs are lock-in to AWS, a less rich UI than a single-page app, and an older pgvector: RDS and Aurora ship 0.8.2 ([AWS](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonRDS/latest/AuroraPostgreSQLReleaseNotes/AuroraPostgreSQL.Extensions.html)) while Cloud SQL ships 0.8.5 ([Google](https://docs.cloud.google.com/sql/docs/postgres/extensions)), and CCP indexes every tenant with HNSW from the start (answer to U13), so it keeps chunks insert-only, rebuilds a tenant's index after any erasure, and moves to 0.8.3 or later, which fixed an HNSW vacuum bug, as soon as RDS ships it (6.5). Early customers mostly on GCP or needing EU processing first (Vertex EU), or a UI that needs heavy client-side interaction, would change it.

### 6.4 The agent stack: decisions by layer

In our reading, frontier teams now build agents the same way: one writer runs a short loop inside code, with a few stable tools, a prompt rebuilt from a durable record, and harness changes shipped like releases. CCP's design already has this shape (3.1 to 3.5), so the table settles the remaining choices for layers 5 to 7 of 6.1, plus models and context, and names what waits for a trigger. None of the decisions adds a service.

> **Diagram:** Agent stack · six code parts around two model steps (rendered in ccp-target-architecture.pdf).

Only the two blue boxes call a model. Everything that builds context, checks, decides or acts is code, and each model step starts from a context rebuilt from the run log.

| Component | Decision for CCP | Frontier precedent | Not now, and the trigger |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Orchestration | Each graph is a fixed workflow with at most one agent loop, placed where the path is unknown: the Investigator loops only in `gather`, and four of its six nodes are code (`agent/graphs/investigator.py:291-312`). Code is the one writer; a sub-agent, if added, is read-only, gets its own context and an output schema, and returns ids. Breadth work, such as vendor reviews or audit-prep gap lists, fans out from code, one fresh run per item, with a deterministic check before results merge | A Google Research, Google DeepMind and MIT study of 180 configurations found that a central coordinator gained 80.8% on a finance task that splits cleanly, while every multi-agent design lost 39% to 70% on sequential planning. Independent agents amplified errors 17.2 times, against 4.4 with a coordinator ([Kim et al., Dec 2025](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.08296v2)). Microsoft's Agent Framework 1.0 ships graph workflows "for composing agents and functions into deterministic, repeatable processes", with checkpoints and human approvals ([Microsoft, Apr 2026](https://devblogs.microsoft.com/agent-framework/microsoft-agent-framework-version-1-0/)) | Agents that negotiate with each other or write in parallel; Claude Code's agent teams are still "experimental and disabled by default" ([Claude Code docs, undated](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/agent-teams)). Trigger: one agent scores below about 45% on a read task that splits into independent parts; above that score, the study found coordination stops paying |
| Harness releases | Every run and verdict names its bundle (3.5; 6.1, rows 5 and 8), which pins prompts, graph, tool descriptions, budgets, effort and thinking settings, and the context-building code. Today effort is fixed at medium in code (`llm/anthropic_client.py:69-71`). A change ships after per-model evals, a line-by-line ablation of any multi-line prompt edit, and a canary tenant before a staged rollout; a model migration is planned to finish within six weeks of a deprecation notice | Anthropic traced three quality drops that users noticed to harness changes, not the model: default effort cut from high to medium ("the wrong tradeoff"), old thinking cleared on every turn, and a verbosity line that, with other changes, cost 3% on an ablation eval. It now uses per-model evals for every system-prompt change, line-by-line ablations, soak periods and gradual rollouts ([Anthropic, Apr 2026](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/april-23-postmortem)). Its 2026 deprecations gave about two months' notice, as with Sonnet 4.5, announced 30 Sep and retiring 30 Nov ([Anthropic release notes, Sep 2026](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/release-notes/overview.md)) | Never: a vendor-hosted prompt store. OpenAI shuts its reusable-prompts API on 30 Nov 2026 and advises "Move prompt content into application code" ([OpenAI, undated](https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/deprecations)). Prompts stay in git, hash-checked (`spec.md` A-21) |
| Context assembly | The prompt view (3.4) keeps one byte-stable order: tool definitions (already sorted, `agent/core.py:292`), then system prompt and schema, then the run's data; nothing that varies per run goes before the cache point. Tool loops turn on automatic caching, so each turn reads the earlier turns from cache; today only system blocks carry a cache point (`llm/anthropic_client.py:48-55`, `:303-312`). A result too large for the context arrives as a handle with a preview, and a read tool returns the rest in pages | Claude Code orders prompts "static content first, dynamic content last" and puts updates in the next message, not the system prompt ([Claude blog, Apr 2026](https://claude.dev/blog/lessons-from-building-claude-code-prompt-caching-is-everything/)). Anthropic's automatic caching moves the cache point forward as a conversation grows ([Anthropic release notes, Feb 2026](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/release-notes/overview.md)). Cursor writes long tool outputs to files that the agent reads in parts ([Cursor, Jan 2026](https://cursor.com/blog/dynamic-context-discovery)) | Skill-style procedure files per framework, listed by name and description and loaded in full only when relevant, as Agent Skills are ([Anthropic, Oct 2025](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/equipping-agents-for-the-real-world-with-agent-skills)). Trigger: a third framework, or guidance too large for the cached prefix; each file is versioned and hashed and holds no pass or fail logic |
| Long runs and compaction | Runs stay short: by default a run stops at 8 tool calls, 60,000 tokens, $0.50 or 120 seconds (`llm/budget.py:27`, `config.py:91-93`), and each Copilot turn starts a fresh context (`agent/graphs/copilot.py:256`). Longer work becomes a new run seeded from typed state. If a run must shed context, the view swaps old tool results for handles, never a result that a draft cites, and no model-written summary sits between evidence and a proposal | Anthropic's context editing "automatically clears stale tool calls and results"; Anthropic reports a 29% gain from it alone on an internal eval run before the 5.x models ([Anthropic, Sep 2025](https://claude.com/blog/context-management)). Amp replaced compaction with hand-offs to fresh threads because compaction is "lossy" ([Amp, Oct 2025](https://ampcode.com/news/handoff)). Factory reports that every compression method it tested was weakest at tracking which files changed ([Factory, Dec 2025](https://factory.com/news/evaluating-compression)) | Server-side compaction, still in beta ([Anthropic docs, undated](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/compaction)). Trigger: the Copilot starts carrying earlier turns; then on-demand compaction with CCP's own summary prompt, stored in the run log, for the Copilot only |
| Memory | Facts stay in the versioned store, and past investigations are read through tools such as `get_investigation` (`agent/tools.py:170-174`), never copied into memory. When memory is added, its only new layer is short per-tenant notes on how a tenant works (owners, where evidence lives), each with source ids, and a person confirms each note before any agent sees it. No rule, extraction or verifier reads memory | Anthropic's memory tool "operates entirely client-side through tool calls", so the builder owns the store ([Anthropic, Sep 2025](https://claude.com/blog/context-management)). A June 2026 study found that agents which write and retrieve memory more aggressively are "more exploitable", and that "existing prompt injection defenses fail to cover memory poisoning attacks" ([Dash et al., Jun 2026](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.04329)) | Copilot memory, absent today (`spec.md:113-116`), and any memory hosted by a model provider or shared across tenants. Trigger: users keep restating context, and a poisoning suite (notes planted through uploads, connector text and chat) passes before the first write path ships |
| Tool set | Each graph keeps a small, fixed tool list per bundle, in sorted order and without overlaps: today 4 to 13 of CCP's 13 tools (`agent/allowlists.py:19-30`). A blocked or failed call returns a typed error that says what to try next; tools are never added or removed mid-run. Three consecutive errors end the run in `needs_review`, as Claude Code's auto mode escalates after three consecutive denials (3.6) | GitHub cut "the default 40 built-in tools down to 13 core ones" and reports 2 to 5 points more on SWE-bench Verified and SWE-Lancer, and 400 ms lower latency ([GitHub, Nov 2025](https://github.blog/ai-and-ml/github-copilot/how-were-making-github-copilot-smarter-with-fewer-tools/)). Cursor reports 46.9% fewer agent tokens, in runs that used MCP tools, from loading only tool names up front ([Cursor, Jan 2026](https://cursor.com/blog/dynamic-context-discovery)). Claude Code: "adding or removing a tool invalidates the cache for the entire conversation" ([Claude blog, Apr 2026](https://claude.dev/blog/lessons-from-building-claude-code-prompt-caching-is-everything/)) | Tool search with deferred loading, which no longer needs a beta header ([Anthropic release notes, Feb 2026](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/release-notes/overview.md)); see 3.3. Trigger: one graph needs more than about 20 tools. The Copilot gets every read tool (`agent/allowlists.py:29`), so it gets there first as connectors add read tools |
| Code execution | No model-written code runs in CCP, and none will run on a path that feeds a status. Typed tools cover today's workflows, and code renders remediation diffs from templates (6.2) | Replit's testing sub-agent writes Playwright code in a REPL instead of calling fixed browser tools, and Replit reports a median cost of $0.20 per testing session ([Replit, Dec 2025](https://frontier.replit.com/automated-self-testing/)). Anthropic's containment rule: "The weakest layer is the one you built yourself"; standard hypervisors held where its own proxies failed ([Anthropic, May 2026](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/how-we-contain-claude)) | Anthropic's code execution, which falls outside zero retention (3.7), and programmatic tool calling, which runs inside code execution and so falls outside it too (inferred). Trigger: an offline analysis that typed tools cannot express, such as diffing a large access-review export; then a standard microVM sandbox with no network or credentials, run by CCP, with the generated code kept as a record of what ran |
| AI workflows (tiers 2 and 3) | Tier 2 stays one tool-less call per chunk or item, and a rule decides from its typed fields; a large batch is a code loop of such calls, never one long context. Tier 3 ends a run by storing a proposal whose actions come from the closed catalogue, with reversibility read from the table, never from the model (`knowledge/actions.py:11`) | Manus found that uniform context makes agents brittle: "don't few-shot yourself into a rut" ([Manus, Jul 2025](https://manus.im/blog/Context-Engineering-for-AI-Agents-Lessons-from-Building-Manus)). Stripe's compliance agents split each review into scoped sub-tasks; the reviewer answers every one, and agent output is "supplementary information to the human reviewer" ([Stripe on the AWS ML blog, Jun 2026](https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/production-grade-ai-agents-for-financial-compliance-lessons-from-stripe/)) | Agents that act. Trigger: a reversible, low-impact action earns a measured level under 4.4 (LT8) |
| Verification before approval | A run reaches "propose" only through code gates: schema, citations with typed values (5.4), catalogue bounds and, where an action changes an observed setting, a dry run that applies its declared effect (a new catalogue field) to a copy of the observations, re-runs the tier-1 rule and shows the reviewer both results. A failure gives `needs_review` with its reason; high-value outputs then get the clean-context reviewer of 3.2, as a signal only. Today the Investigator checks only that quotes exist (`agent/graphs/investigator.py:187-213`), and the rule re-runs only after approval, on fixtures (`remediation/runner.py:205-231`) | Anthropic ranks rule-based checks first and an LLM judge last, as "generally not a very robust method" ([Anthropic, Sep 2025](https://claude.com/blog/building-agents-with-the-claude-agent-sdk)). Replit verifies in a separate testing sub-agent, to catch features that look finished but do not work ([Replit, Dec 2025](https://frontier.replit.com/automated-self-testing/)). Anthropic found its own adversarial reviewer can be overly defensive and add needless complexity ([Anthropic, Oct 2026](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/redesigning-source-control)) | A model reviewer on every output, or judge-driven retry loops. Trigger: the reviewer's flags agree with expert labels at 85% or better on a labelled sample (7.1) |
| Model routing and budgets | Route by step and never switch models inside a run: Haiku 5.5 for extraction, Sonnet 5.5 for agent loops, and Opus 5.5 only as a fresh run on cases where evals show it helps (Decision 9). Hard caps stay in code (`llm/budget.py:73-100`); an API task budget is added only at a measured p99 and set once per run | Claude Code notes that switching from Opus to Haiku 100,000 tokens into a session costs more than letting Opus answer, because Haiku must rebuild the cache, so it routes cheaper work through sub-agents ([Claude blog, Apr 2026](https://claude.dev/blog/lessons-from-building-claude-code-prompt-caching-is-everything/)). Task budgets are "a soft hint, not a hard cap", have a 20,000-token minimum, can cause "refusal-like behavior" when too small, and break the cache when changed ([Anthropic docs, undated](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/task-budgets.md)) | The advisor tool. On the 5.5 models its advice comes back as "opaque `encrypted_content`" ([Anthropic docs, undated](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/agents-and-tools/tool-use/advisor-tool.md)), so the run log could not record it, and its zero-retention status is unconfirmed; Anthropic's results, such as Haiku with an Opus advisor at 41.2% against 19.7% alone on BrowseComp, are self-reported ([Anthropic, Apr 2026](https://claude.com/blog/the-advisor-strategy)). Trigger: readable advice and confirmed zero retention, then a gain on CCP's evals |
| Retrieval | Agents search through typed, as-of-aware tools that return ids, versions and hashes; hybrid search is one of those tools. Detail in 6.5 | Anthropic advises starting with agentic search and adding semantic search only for speed or varied phrasing ([Anthropic, Sep 2025](https://claude.com/blog/building-agents-with-the-claude-agent-sdk)); Cursor reports 12.5% higher answer accuracy on average from adding semantic search to grep ([Cursor, Nov 2025](https://cursor.com/blog/semsearch)) | See 6.5 |
| Evals, guardrails and operations | Every bundle clears the gates in 5.3 and the guardrails in 5.5. The run log adds four agent metrics per graph: cache-hit rate, tokens per completed task, consecutive tool errors and refusals. Detail in 6.6 | Factory: "The right optimization target is not tokens per request. It is tokens per task." ([Factory, Dec 2025](https://factory.com/news/evaluating-compression)). After infrastructure bugs that its evals missed, Anthropic added "Continuous quality evaluations run on production systems" ([Anthropic, Sep 2025](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/a-postmortem-of-three-recent-issues)) | See 6.6 |

**Not adopted yet**

- **A hosted agent runtime.** Every Claude Managed Agents endpoint still needs a beta header ([Anthropic release notes, Apr 2026](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/release-notes/overview.md)), Anthropic hosts the harness, and the service sits outside zero retention (3.7), which conflicts with replay from CCP's own run log (inferred). Revisit for an isolated, sandboxed analysis task if it becomes eligible.
- **Fine-tuned small models.** Fin reports 12.5% lower cost per transaction, with satisfaction unchanged, after replacing a GPT-4.1 step with a fine-tuned 14B model, and it trained one classifier on a million examples ([Fin, Sep 2025](https://fin.ai/research/david-vs-goliath-are-small-llms-any-good)). Haiku 5.5 is already 20 times cheaper than Sonnet 5.5 with no training pipeline (6.3, Cost). Revisit for one narrow step with that volume and stable labels.

### 6.5 Retrieval, layer by layer

Today one SQL statement fuses Postgres full-text and pgvector HNSW results by reciprocal rank fusion, and Voyage `rerank-2.5` reranks the top 20 and passes 6 on (`retrieval/hybrid.py:112-151`, `retrieval/config.py:34-37`, `retrieval/rerank.py:50`). Chunks are 700-character windows, PDFs give only their text layer, and agents get one search tool capped at 8 results plus chunk reads (`retrieval/chunker.py:19-21`, `connectors/manual/pdf_text.py:91-100`, `agent/tools.py:32`). The target keeps this core, reads scans and screenshots, serves each tenant from its own rows as of any date, and gives agents exact tools beside semantic search; retrieval only finds spans, code checks them (5.4), and a rule decides.

> **Diagram:** Retrieval pipeline · ingest once per version, query per search, code checks every citation (rendered in ccp-target-architecture.pdf).

Ingest runs once per evidence version; queries run on every search. Whatever finds a span, code checks the citation before any rule uses it.

| Layer | Decision for CCP | Frontier precedent | Not now, and the trigger |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Parse and transcribe (PDFs, scans, screenshots) | Keep pypdf for pages with a text layer. Transcribe scans, image-only pages and screenshots page by page with Claude in the zero-retention organization, choosing the model on CCP's fixtures; record each transcript like an extraction and show the page image beside every cited span. Today images are skipped and a PDF is rejected only if every page is empty, so a mixed PDF's scanned pages drop out with no event (`connectors/manual/uploads.py:125-132`, `connectors/manual/pdf_text.py:86-88`, `normalize/manual_mapper.py:28-31`) | Claude reads each PDF page as text plus an image ([Anthropic](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/pdf-support)), but a scan without a text layer cannot be cited ([Anthropic](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/citations)). Vendor-built benchmarks put Claude Opus 4.8 at 93.0% field accuracy as a parser (Extend reports) and Haiku 4.5 near the bottom (LlamaIndex reports) | A third-party parser such as Reducto or LlamaParse, which adds a processor of evidence. Trigger: Claude misses the field-accuracy target on CCP's fixtures, such as SOC 2 exception tables |
| Chunk and add context | Split on structure (headings, policy and report sections, whole table rows) within pages, store code-point offsets into the canonical text, which chunks lack today (`storage/migrations/0008_chunks.sql:10-29`), and tune size per evidence type. Keep contextual enrichment, on Haiku 5.5 with thinking off and cached by content hash, and give it the whole document within the 100,000-token price band instead of a 60,000-character cut (`retrieval/contextual.py:17`, `:43`): about $0.003 for an 8,000-token policy and under $0.20 for a report near the limit, once per version (inferred) | Prepended context cut top-20 retrieval failures by 35%, and by 49% with the context also in the lexical index, for $1.02 per million document tokens at 2024 prices ([Anthropic, Sep 2024](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/contextual-retrieval)). Vanta measured +16% recall from context-aware chunking on its compliance content ([Vanta, Feb 2025](https://www.vanta.com/resources/building-a-smarter-retrieval-system-lessons-from-vanta-ai)). Chunk size is "a hyperparameter to tune" ([Husain, Sep 2026](https://hamel.dev/blog/posts/evals-faq/)) | `voyage-context-4`, a contextualized-chunk embedder at $0.12 per million tokens with no model calls, which gives the lexical arm nothing ([MongoDB](https://www.mongodb.com/docs/voyageai/models/)). Trigger: enrichment cost or ingest time becomes material |
| Index | LIST partitions per tenant, each with its own HNSW index from the start (answer to U13), indexed as `embedding::halfvec(1024)` to roughly halve index memory (inferred). RDS offers pgvector 0.8.2 at most, and 0.8.3 fixed an HNSW vacuum bug, so chunks stay insert-only (vacuum rarely touches the index), a tenant's index is rebuilt after any erasure, and CCP moves to 0.8.3 or later when RDS ships it. Past (as-of) dates still search exactly. Keep `ts_rank_cd`: its GIN index goes unused under row-level security (Decision 8), so the lexical arm scans the tenant's rows, which suits small tenants (inferred). Check hot plans as the app role | pgvector supports half-precision expression indexes and recommends list partitioning for multitenancy ([pgvector](https://github.com/pgvector/pgvector)); 0.8.3 fixed index corruption during HNSW vacuuming ([pgvector, Jun 2026](https://github.com/pgvector/pgvector/blob/master/CHANGELOG.md)). pg\_textsearch warns that "BM25 corpus statistics include all indexed rows, including rows hidden by RLS", while "BM25 statistics are local to each partition" ([pg\_textsearch](https://github.com/timescale/pg_textsearch)) | BM25 with `pg_textsearch`, only ever on a tenant's own partition: on a shared table one tenant's vocabulary would shift every tenant's scores (inferred). Trigger: identifier recall or a large tenant's lexical latency misses target, and RDS allows the extension, which needs `shared_preload_libraries`; RDS listed no BM25 extension on 9 Nov 2026. Binary quantization waits for a halfvec index that outgrows memory |
| Query understanding | Tier 2 queries stay templates rendered from the test definition (`evaluators/tier2.py:99-116`): deterministic, stored, never rewritten by a model. Evidence type, subject, period and as-of are typed filters, not query words. Agents rewrite and split questions themselves with several narrow searches. Which tests apply is decided by code from scoping facts, never by retrieval | OpenAI's file search offers query rewriting and attribute filters ([OpenAI](https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/guides/retrieval)). Multi-hop and open-ended queries are the hardest for retrievers ([ViDoRe V3, Nov 2025](https://huggingface.co/blog/QuentinJG/introducing-vidore-v3)). Fin warns that "a missed rule can alter the response" ([Fin, Sep 2026](https://fin.ai/research/how-fin-scales-customer-defined-behaviour)) | A query-rewriting model in tier 2. Trigger: the golden set's paraphrase slice misses its recall target after reranking |
| Hybrid search and fusion | Keep one statement, both arms on every query, RRF at k = 60. Add per-call arm weights only where the golden set shows a gap, such as identifier queries that need the lexical arm, fitted from the ranks the step log already keeps (`evaluators/tier2.py:178-185`) | Linear runs full-text and vector queries in parallel, then rank fusion and reranking ([turbopuffer, Jun 2025](https://turbopuffer.com/customers/linear)); OpenAI exposes fusion weights per arm ([OpenAI](https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/guides/retrieval)). Vanta measured +9% recall from BM25 plus embeddings with RRF ([Vanta, Feb 2025](https://www.vanta.com/resources/building-a-smarter-retrieval-system-lessons-from-vanta-ai)) | Learned fusion. Trigger: fitted weights still leave a measured gap on a query slice |
| Reranking | Move to `rerank-3`, a cross-encoder at the same price, and keep its top score as the input to the distractor-calibrated confidence cap (`evaluators/tier2.py:305-308`). Rerank 50 candidates instead of 20 only if Recall@20 is the gap; that costs under a tenth of a cent a query (inferred) | Reranking cut Anthropic's top-20 failures from 2.9% to 1.9% ([Anthropic, Sep 2024](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/contextual-retrieval)), and on 50 candidates added 21% MRR at Vanta ([Vanta, Feb 2025](https://www.vanta.com/resources/building-a-smarter-retrieval-system-lessons-from-vanta-ai)). LLM rerankers lead on familiar queries but generalize unevenly and take minutes where cross-encoders take seconds ([arXiv, Aug 2025](https://arxiv.org/html/2508.16757v1)) | An LLM or fine-tuned reranker; Intercom reports that one distilled from an LLM beat Cohere Rerank 3.5 on MAP (0.612 against 0.521) at 80% lower cost. Trigger: thousands of consented labels (last row) and a way to calibrate its score |
| Agentic search tools | The Investigator, Policy Agent and Copilot get, through the registry: `search_evidence` with the subject, period and as-of filters it lacks (`agent/tools.py:39-42`); `find_exact` for control ids, CVEs, ARNs, emails and dates; `outline` and `read_span` to navigate a document version, then read it by page or offsets; and typed fact tools. Results carry version ids and offsets; every call is logged and budgeted. Agents get both kinds of search because the measured gains come from combining them (inferred) | Anthropic calls semantic search faster but "less accurate" than agentic search and suggests starting agentic ([Anthropic, Sep 2025](https://claude.com/blog/building-agents-with-the-claude-agent-sdk)). Cursor reports that adding semantic search to its agent's grep raised answer accuracy by 12.5% on average. A stronger retriever lifted a GPT-5 research agent from 55.9% to 70.1% with fewer searches ([BrowseComp-Plus, Aug 2025](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.06600)) | A trained retrieval sub-agent or embeddings trained on agent traces, as Cognition and Cursor built. Trigger: searches per verified citation stay high after these tools ship, and enough consented labels exist |
| Structured facts instead of a document graph | Build no graph from documents. The links already exist as typed, versioned facts (person, account, grant, asset, vendor, report period), so a multi-hop question such as "which leavers still hold GitHub admin?" is a join inside a typed tool, with as-of dates and explicit truncation (7.1). Chunks keep their subject entity for entity-scoped search (`storage/migrations/0008_chunks.sql:15`) | Building a knowledge graph kept only about two thirds of answer entities, graph-triplet retrieval reached 39.2% accuracy against 88.6% for plain retrieval, and graph methods won only on multi-hop questions, by about 3 points ([Han et al., Nov 2026](https://arxiv.org/html/2502.11371)) | A document-derived graph or model-written SQL: see **Not now** |
| Long-context reads | Read one whole document when the task is exhaustive: a vendor SOC 2 report's exceptions, complementary user entity controls, carve-outs and opinion, or a policy against a clause list. Send canonical text rather than the PDF where a text layer exists, extract verbatim quotes first and check them (5.4). Keep retrieval for questions across a tenant's documents | Claude takes up to 600 PDF pages per request, but dense PDFs can fill the window first ([Anthropic](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/pdf-support)). Accuracy fell with input length across 18 models ([Chroma, Jul 2025](https://www.trychroma.com/research/context-rot)), and 11 of 13 models fell below half their short-context score at 32K tokens ([NoLiMa, Feb 2025](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.05167)). Anthropic advises extracting word-for-word quotes first beyond 20K tokens ([Anthropic](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/test-and-evaluate/strengthen-guardrails/reduce-hallucinations)) | Long context as the default across documents: 400K tokens cost about $0.80 per uncached Sonnet 5.5 question, against half a cent for six chunks (inferred). Trigger: a current model shows no loss at 200K tokens on CCP's own set |
| Citations | CCP's pointer citations stay the contract: document version, offsets and exact text, checked by code (5.4). Agent and Copilot tools return `search_result` blocks whose `source` is a stable `ccp://` URI for the version and chunk and whose content is one text block per sentence, so code maps each cited block range to offsets and runs the same checks. Tier 2 keeps structured outputs with quoted spans (3.7) | Anthropic guarantees only that citations "contain valid pointers" ([Anthropic](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/citations)). `search_result` blocks hold text only, citations are all-or-nothing per request, a tool result holding one may hold nothing else, and JSON-only prompts may get few citations ([Anthropic](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/search-results)). Harvey cites sentence indices instead of fuzzy-matching model text ([Harvey, Apr 2026](https://www.harvey.ai/blog/rebuilding-harveys-review-algorithm)) | Native citations in tier 2, which return a 400 alongside structured outputs. Trigger: the API accepts both together |
| Permissions, as-of filters and erasure | Tenant scope stays in SQL (forced row-level security, later the tenant's partition), never a filter after search; when user roles arrive, a visibility class on sensitive evidence such as HR files joins the same policy. Chunks become insert-only rows per evidence version (6.1, row 3) with validity dates, so search and replay can ask "as of" any date; HNSW covers current rows only and past dates search exactly (inferred). Today the index keeps current evidence only and deletes the rest (`retrieval/index.py:76`, `:188-192`). Erasure removes a version's chunks, cache rows and index entries in one job (U10) | Harvey enforces access "at the database layer so that unauthorized vectors are never retrieved", and its embeddings follow the source document's encryption and retention ([Harvey, Apr 2026](https://www.harvey.ai/blog/how-harvey-secures-embeddings-at-scale)). Glean checks source-derived permissions as it serves each result and removes content the source no longer returns ([Glean](https://docs.glean.com/connectors/key-terms)) | Live per-user permission checks against each source, as Glean does. Trigger: customers expect per-user source permissions mirrored inside a tenant |
| Embedding and reranker models | Move to `voyage-4-large` at 1,024 dimensions and `rerank-3`: embed into new rows under a new index version, keep old rows for replay (6.3), recalibrate the thresholds kept per model pair (`retrieval/config.py:38-45`), and switch after a paired golden-set comparison. Today a model change overwrites vectors in place (`retrieval/index.py:160-169`). Keep an open-weight embedder as a tested fallback, not a running service | `voyage-3-large` has been Legacy since 15 Jan 2026 and `rerank-2.5` since 29 Sep 2026 ([MongoDB, Oct 2026](https://www.mongodb.com/docs/voyageai/models/lifecycle/)). All 4-series embeddings "are compatible with each other" ([Voyage, Aug 2026](https://docs.voyageai.com/docs/embeddings)), so a cheaper query model could share the index (inferred). Public scores "can become inflated", so RTEB keeps private sets ([Hugging Face, Oct 2025](https://huggingface.co/blog/rteb)) | Running an open model such as Qwen3-Embedding-8B, the retriever behind the BrowseComp-Plus gain. Trigger: a contract that excludes MongoDB as a processor, or a Voyage outage longer than the lexical-only fallback can carry (`retrieval/hybrid.py:176-180`) |
| Retrieval evals and gates | Grow the 30 golden queries over six evidence types (`spec.md:1645`) to 25 to 50 per type, sliced by query kind (identifier, paraphrase, multi-hop, negation) and tenant size. Report Recall@20 before reranking, Recall@6 after it, MRR, and the share of queries with a distractor in the top 6; a model, parser, chunker or prompt change ships only if recall holds and that share does not rise (5.3). Add free positives from spans that passed the 5.4 checks and a reviewer accepted, keep a private holdout, and calibrate, so the flat 0.60 retrieval factor every tier-2 verdict gets today becomes measured (`evaluators/confidence.py:23`, `:58-60`; `config/retrieval.yaml:8`) | Evaluate retrieval separately and first, with IR metrics ([Husain, Sep 2026](https://hamel.dev/blog/posts/evals-faq/)). RTEB reads a large gap between public and private results as overfitting ([Hugging Face, Oct 2025](https://huggingface.co/blog/rteb)). Cursor reports pairing an offline benchmark with an online test of code retention, and labelling embedding training data from agent traces | Online tests per retrieval setting. Trigger: enough volume per workflow to detect a difference |

**Tier by tier.**

| Path | Retrieval it uses | Pinned for replay |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Tier 2 extraction | One templated hybrid search per test and subject, filtered by type, subject and as-of, reranked to six; no tools and no loop | The query, the index version (parser, chunker, embedding and rerank models), candidate ids with ranks, chunk ids with text hashes, and the fenced request and response. Replay rebuilds from stored text and never searches again; today candidates sit only in the step log (`evaluators/tier2.py:178-185`) |
| Tier 3 agents (Investigator, Policy Agent) | A budgeted loop over search, exact match, outline, read span and typed fact tools, after any fixed fetches the graph declares; the Policy Agent reads policy documents only (`agent/graphs/policy.py:4-5`) | Every tool call and result in the run log (3.4), with the version ids and offsets each returned |
| Copilot | The same read tools, as of the date asked; status answers come from rule outputs and the rollup, never from documents | The turn's tool calls, results and cited spans |
| Questionnaires | Code first maps each question to tested controls and their current verdicts; search runs only for supporting prose and unmapped questions (LT3) | The mapping version, chunk ids per answer and the verdicts relied on; an answer goes stale when any of them changes |
| Evidence review | No search: the document under review is read whole, or by outline and span when long, quotes first | The document version and text hash, the transcription method if any, and the extraction request and response |

**Not now.**

- **GraphRAG.** Typed facts already hold the links, and document-built graphs lose entities and single-hop accuracy (see the structured-facts row). Trigger: a corpus-wide question that summaries over typed facts cannot answer, such as recurring exceptions across every vendor SOC 2 report; then trial a LazyGraphRAG-style index, whose indexing costs the same as vector search ([Microsoft, Nov 2024](https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/research/blog/lazygraphrag-setting-a-new-standard-for-quality-and-cost/)), in a drafting feature only.
- **Visual (ColPali-style) retrieval.** The best visual retrievers reach about 0.65 NDCG@10 on enterprise pages ([ViDoRe V3, Nov 2025](https://huggingface.co/blog/QuentinJG/introducing-vidore-v3)), page-image vectors cannot carry text citations that code checks, and screenshots are mostly found by control, system and date. Trigger: transcription measurably loses what retrieval needs on CCP's fixtures, such as architecture diagrams.
- **Text-to-SQL for any status.** Never: a status is a versioned rule over facts (Decision 1), so no trigger applies. Read-only model-written SQL may later serve Copilot exploration over curated views under row-level security, with timeouts and row caps, labelled exploratory, once Copilot logs show questions the typed tools cannot answer.
- **A dedicated vector engine.** Linear left Elasticsearch and pgvector at 2B+ documents in 4M+ namespaces to cut operations and keep tail latency flat ([turbopuffer, Jun 2025](https://turbopuffer.com/customers/linear)); Harvey's dual writes show the cost: "In a dual write system drifts are inevitable" ([Harvey, Oct 2026](https://www.harvey.ai/blog/making-review-tables-faster-and-more-reliable)). Trigger: p99 latency that partitions cannot hold flat, per-tenant keys required on the index itself, or millions of chunk writes a day for one tenant.

### 6.6 Evals, guardrails and operations: decisions by layer

Section 5 fixes the graders, metrics, gates and guardrails, and rows 8 to 10 of 6.1 name what to build; this subsection records only what frontier practice in 2026 changes or adds. The labs' own postmortems and studies show quality and safety slipping at the seams around the model: a harness or prompt edit, drift in serving, where untrusted text sits in a prompt, which model actually answered, and what a trace carries out. Each row gives the decision, the precedent behind it, and what waits for a trigger.

| Layer and topic | Decision for CCP | Frontier precedent | Not now, and the trigger |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 8. Prompt and harness changes are releases | A change to any prompt line, tool description, effort setting, model id or graph edge makes a new bundle; today the effort level is fixed in client code (`llm/anthropic_client.py:69-71`). The bundle runs the nightly suite on every model that uses the prompt, and a change of several prompt lines that moves a metric is retested one line at a time. A bundle change demotes a workflow's autonomy level (4.4) until a shadow soak passes | In April 2026 Anthropic traced a Claude Code quality drop to three product changes, among them a lower default reasoning effort and a system-prompt line that had passed weeks of internal evals; a broader test later showed that line cost 3%. Anthropic now runs a per-model suite on every system-prompt change, tests prompt lines one at a time, and uses soak periods and gradual rollout ([Anthropic, Apr 2026](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/april-23-postmortem)) | A/B tests on customer traffic. Trigger: a drafting surface where a person approves every output and volume supports a 95% interval, read as Intercom does: significant only when the whole interval is above zero ([Intercom Help](https://www.intercom.com/help/en/articles/18227993-how-to-roll-out-a-release-in-fin)). Status-deciding steps keep shadow (5.3) |
| 8. Drift and infrastructure noise | A fixed canary set of synthetic evidence with known answers runs daily through the production path (same organization, platform and bundle), and an alert fires when the lower bound of its accuracy drops below baseline. Timeouts, rate limits and connector errors are logged as an infrastructure class: they still fail the gate (5.2), but a run with many of them is re-run, not read as a quality change. No bundle or autonomy level is promoted on a gain under about 3 points unless it repeats across days (inferred) | Anthropic's 2025 quality bugs came from serving infrastructure under unchanged model ids, and its evals "didn't capture" them; it now runs quality evals continuously on production systems ([Anthropic, Sep 2025](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/a-postmortem-of-three-recent-issues)). Resource settings alone moved one coding benchmark by 6 points, and Anthropic treats gaps under 3 points with skepticism ([Anthropic, Feb 2026](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/infrastructure-noise)) | Canaries per cell and platform. Trigger: a second cell, or an EU cell on Bedrock (LT9) |
| 8. Gold sets sized to the false-PASS bound | Weight each workflow's gold set towards FAIL cases, since only they measure false PASS: about 300 expert-labelled FAIL cases with none passed show a rate of at most 1% at 95% confidence, and one false pass raises the need to about 475 (inferred arithmetic). Today's labelled verdict set has four FAIL cases (repository root `evals/labeled/README.md:23`). A FAIL case counts as a false PASS if any of its five runs passes it; labels come from named experts, never from approvals given in CCP; retrieval keeps its own labelled queries (6.5) | HealthBench reports worst-at-k, the worst score across k samples ([HealthBench, May 2025](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.08775)). Ramp does not take users' past decisions as ground truth, because finance teams are lenient ([Ramp](https://engineering.ramp.com/post/how-to-build-agents-users-can-trust)). "Evaluate the two components separately, starting with retrieval" ([Husain, Sep 2026](https://hamel.dev/blog/posts/evals-faq/)) | Certifying a workflow before its FAIL set reaches the size its bound needs; until then it keeps per-item review. Trigger: U3 sets the bound per test |
| 8. What judges see | Judges get the source spans that code verified, the tool outputs and the final artifact. The agent's reasoning is graded only as prose, never taken as proof of what it did; today the judge reads the agent's whole output, reasoning included, and only the case input besides (`evals/runner.py:252-257`, `evals/judge.py:128-133`). Empty, truncated, punctuation-only and "Thought process:" outputs join the negative controls (5.3), and the judge must fail them all | Rewriting an agent's reasoning while keeping its actions fixed raised web-agent judges' false-positive rates by up to 90% ([Gaming the Judge, Jan 2026](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.14691)). Content-free replies such as ":" or "Thought process:" won false positives from judges including Claude-4 ([One Token to Fool, Jul 2025](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.08794)) | Several judges voting. Anthropic found one judge prompt more consistent than several ([Anthropic, Jun 2025](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/multi-agent-research-system)). Trigger: a judge misses the 85% expert-agreement bar (7.1) |
| 8. Drafts: pairwise comparison and simulated users | Choose drafting models and prompts (questionnaire answers, policy drafts, Copilot answers) by blinded side-by-side comparison, three experts per pair. Simulated auditors and customers, scripted to be vague and uncooperative, serve only as regression tests for the Copilot and questionnaires, never for quality or autonomy claims. Neither gates a status-deciding step | Pairwise expert judgments recovered legal quality rankings far better than rubrics (per-task Spearman 0.908 against 0.150) in under half the annotation time ([JudgmentBench, May 2026](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25240)); Harvey has 3 to 10 lawyers review each pair ([Harvey, Nov 2025](https://www.harvey.ai/blog/introducing-biglaw-bench-arena)). Simulated users are "excessively cooperative": with them, agent success reached up to 77.8%, against 63.6% with real people ([Sim2Real, Mar 2026](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.11245)) | Checking simulators against real sessions each quarter. Trigger: the Copilot reaches customers (UI v1, MT4) |
| 9. Untrusted text in the prompt | Evidence reaches the model only as tool results or document blocks, never pasted into the instruction text, and the attack suite below tests each placement before it ships. Strip invisible Unicode from evidence and from pasted Copilot questions, and mark pasted text as quoted. Today tier 2, the structured agent nodes and the Copilot's answer step paste fenced evidence or tool output into the text of the user message (`evaluators/tier2.py:257-260`, `agent/core.py:233-236`, `agent/graphs/copilot.py:291-296`) | Anthropic reports that early Opus 5.5 snapshots followed instructions planted in pasted user text in 52% of attempts but never in tool results (0 of 105); the released model still did about 2% of the time at default effort, and never once invisible characters were stripped and pasted text marked (Opus 5.5 system card, Sep 2026, self-reported) | Scans and screenshots as model input (LT2).
