Trigger: the suite covers instructions hidden in images |
| 9. Which model answers | Never opt in to API safety fallbacks on a tool-using graph; a refusal still becomes needs\_review (3.7). Check on every turn that the model the response reports is the bundle's model, and route the run to review if not. CCP sends no fallback setting today and records the model each response names (`llm/anthropic_client.py:202-208`, `:232`) | On the API, automatic fallbacks are opt-in; on a cyber-classifier block, Sonnet 5.5 falls back to Sonnet 5 ([Anthropic, Sep 2026](https://www.anthropic.com/claude-sonnet-5-5-system-card)). Anthropic reports that none of the 2,872 adaptive-attack requests Opus 5.5 answered itself were compromised; its attack success came from turns its fallback, Opus 4.8, served (Opus 5.5 system card, Sep 2026, self-reported) | Fallbacks on tool-less extraction calls. Trigger: U5 finds refusals too frequent for review to absorb, and the fallback model passes the same gates |
| 9. An attack suite built from CCP's evidence | Plant instructions in every evidence type CCP reads (vendor PDFs, tickets, cloud tags, GitHub text, pasted questions) and in the approved-answer library, aimed at changing an extracted field. Count a success when a field changes and nothing surfaces it, per attempt and per scenario at k tries, and refresh the attacks each quarter and on every model change. Today's suite plants text in one policy chunk and counts only status flips through tier 2 (`evals/adversarial.py:1-5`) | In a contest of 272,000 attack attempts by 464 people on 13 frontier models, every model was vulnerable (attack success 0.5% to 8.5%), and an attack counted only if the agent hid it from the user ([IPI Arena, Mar 2026](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15714)). Existing injection defenses "fail to cover" memory poisoning ([Dash et al., Jun 2026](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.04329)). Anthropic's system cards report Opus 5's adaptive attack success rising from 0.56% (Jul 2026) to 88.92% (Sep 2026) once the attacker improved (self-reported; fallback routing also changed) | A third-party adaptive red team, and a bug bounty that pays for a false PASS or a cross-tenant read. Trigger: the first production customer (Gate 2) for the red team; open sign-up for the bounty |
| 9. Authorization outside the model | Make `policy.check` default-deny: new permit rules first log what they would allow, denied tools leave the graph's tool list, and three denials in a row stop the run for review, as in Claude Code's auto mode (3.6). Each graph type runs as its own principal, recorded with tenant, user and run on every registry call, and never exceeds the rights of the person who started it; scheduled runs use a read-only tenant principal. Only the executor fetches a credential from the broker (5.5), one per approved operation | AWS's agent policy service blocks every tool call by default, opens calls only on conditions over arguments and identity claims the model cannot alter, and has a log-only mode ([AWS, May 2026](https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/security/why-policy-in-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-chose-cedar-for-securing-agentic-workflows/), [AWS, Mar 2026](https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/aws/amazon-bedrock-agentcore-adds-quality-evaluations-and-policy-controls-for-deploying-trusted-ai-agents/)). MCP's enterprise-managed authorization became stable in June 2026, so a company's identity provider can grant agent access centrally ([MCP, Jun 2026](https://blog.modelcontextprotocol.io/posts/enterprise-managed-auth/)); Auth0's Token Vault keeps third-party tokens out of agent code ([Auth0, Nov 2025](https://auth0.com/blog/auth0-for-ai-agents-generally-available/)) | Cedar or a managed policy service, enterprise-managed authorization on CCP's MCP server, and agent principals in customers' own directories. Trigger: customers' agents call CCP's tools (MT3), or the rule table outgrows a page (6.1, row 6) |
| 9. Review that catches errors | Reviewers first mark each AI-read field right or wrong; correcting is a separate step. Seed known-wrong items into CCP's internal expert review at a low rate, report each reviewer's catch rate and accept-all streaks, and show rule-aware hints for a wrong scope, period or entity | In a preregistered study of 2,784 people checking AI-extracted figures from corporate reports, errors that needed a grasp of the rules were corrected only 31% of the time, and time spent did not predict accuracy; the authors advise separating judging from correcting and flagging reviewers who accept or reject everything ([Beck et al., Sep 2025](https://arxiv.org/html/2509.08514v1)) | Seeded items in customers' approval queues. Trigger: a customer opts in; a seeded item can never execute |
| 9. Standards buyers will ask about | Map CCP's agent controls to the OWASP Agentic Top 10 and AIUC-1's six domains, and refresh the map each quarter. Keep every AI output labelled as such (the provenance badges, 6.1 row 11), and ask counsel, before the first EU customer, whether the AI Act's Article 50(2) duties reach CCP's drafts | The AI Act omnibus moved Annex III high-risk duties to 2 Dec 2027 and Annex I to 2 Aug 2028, while some Article 50(2) transparency duties apply from 2 Dec 2026 ([FPF, Jul 2026](https://fpf.org/blog/the-ai-act-implementation-timeline-what-changes-under-the-ai-omnibus)). AIUC-1 certifies agents after typically 1,000 to 5,000 red-team scenarios with quarterly retests, and crosswalks to ISO/IEC 42001 ([AIUC-1, Oct 2026](https://www.aiuc-1.com/)). NIST's planned agent control overlays have no public draft ([NIST, Oct 2026](https://csrc.nist.gov/projects/cosais)) | AIUC-1 or ISO/IEC 42001 certification. Trigger: a buyer's procurement requires one |
| 10. Trace export and replay | A deterministic hook replaces evidence text, quotes and free text with chunk ids and hashes before any span leaves CCP's account, and a CI test fails if one carries evidence text. Engineers see content only through an access-controlled, audit-logged replay from the run log. Today the tier-2 step hands the trace sink its extracted fields, verbatim quotes included, masked only for secret patterns (`evaluators/tier2.py:293-295`, `evaluators/extraction/base.py:8-14`, `security/secrets_patterns.py:65-69`) | Langfuse's masking hook runs in the application at export time, and Langfuse does no masking on its servers ([Langfuse docs](https://langfuse.com/docs/observability/features/masking)). Anthropic's limits on viewing user data slowed diagnosis of its 2025 quality bugs; it responded with privacy-preserving debugging tools ([Anthropic, Sep 2025](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/a-postmortem-of-three-recent-issues)) | Anthropic's OpenTelemetry export, in beta since October 2026 ([Anthropic, Oct 2026](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/release-notes/overview.md)). Trigger: its documentation shows it can carry metadata only |
| 10. Cost and capacity | Alert when the cached share of input tokens drops after a deploy, and use the API's cache diagnostics to find the break; cache tokens are already logged per step (`observability/steps.py:148-153`). Report cost per accepted result, counting failed, retried and refused runs, with reviewer minutes beside it. CCP's token buckets keep about half the rate limit for interactive work, and background jobs yield first when 429s rise (inferred) | Claude Code alerts on its cache hit rate and declares incidents when it falls ([Claude blog, Apr 2026](https://claude.dev/blog/lessons-from-building-claude-code-prompt-caching-is-everything/)); the API's `cache_miss_reason` left beta in September 2026 ([Anthropic, Sep 2026](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/release-notes/overview.md)). In Datadog's 2026 data only 28% of calls to caching models read any cached tokens, and in February rate limits caused 60% of call errors ([Datadog](https://www.datadoghq.com/state-of-ai-engineering/)). Counting review and rework cut GPT-5's naive 474-fold cost saving to 1.63-fold ([GDPval, Oct 2025](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.04374)) | Cache alerts before the Haiku 5.5 move, since tier-2 prompts never reach Haiku 4.5's 4,096-token minimum (3.7). Trigger: MT7's migration |
| 10. Model lifecycle and the Bedrock path | Run each new Claude release in a tier against the incumbent on the frozen gold set and a hard-case suite within a month, even when no switch is planned, so a deprecation notice never starts the work. Keep the migration runbook short enough to finish in six weeks, and re-certify every autonomy level after any model or platform change. Run the gold suite on Bedrock each quarter and before a failover, and track Bedrock's retirement dates apart from Anthropic's | Anthropic shipped about a dozen models between February and November 2026, and three 2026 deprecation notices set retirement about two months out ([Anthropic, Nov 2026](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/release-notes/overview.md)). In its 2025 incidents, misrouting hit 16% of first-party Sonnet 4 requests at the worst hour against 0.18% on Bedrock, so one model id can behave differently by platform ([Anthropic, Sep 2025](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/a-postmortem-of-three-recent-issues)) | Automatic failover to Bedrock, or sending it load. Trigger: an availability commitment that a manual switch cannot meet, or sustained 429s at the top tier, once Bedrock passes the same gates |
| 10. Publishing the false-PASS rate | Publish per workflow and release: the false-PASS upper bound with its count of FAIL-labelled cases, pass^5, the bundle's model and prompt versions, the date of the last re-certification, and the method (dataset version, labeller qualifications, run settings). Publish only bounds that rest on gold sets of the size above (LT6) | Intercom lets customers test its agent inside the product on simulations built from their own conversations ([Intercom, Aug 2026](https://www.intercom.com/blog/announcing-evals-and-releases/)). Anthropic asks that eval results name their resource settings and generator seeds ([Anthropic, Feb 2026](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/infrastructure-noise), [Anthropic, Dec 2025](https://alignment.anthropic.com/2025/bloom-auto-evals/)) | Customers or auditors running CCP's suites themselves. Trigger: an auditor or enterprise buyer asks (LT4's auditor workspace) |

### 6.7 Context engineering and long-running work

Sections 3.4 and 6.4 already rebuild every prompt from the run log and keep runs short, and 6.5 decides when to read a whole document. This subsection sets the numbers and rules inside that frame: how much each call may hold and in what order, how thinking is set and carried, when a 1M-token window pays, and how work that spans days or waits on a person becomes short runs that code can resume and check. Where labs and agent companies differ, CCP takes the option that leaves a typed record a reviewer can replay.

| Topic | Decision for CCP | Frontier precedent | Not now, and the trigger |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| A context budget per call type | Each call type declares an input ceiling in its bundle; the view builder counts tokens before sending and drops the lowest-priority items first, never instructions or a span a draft cites. Starting ceilings, tuned on evals: tier-2 chunk extraction 20K tokens; whole-document extraction 90K on Haiku 5.5, else Sonnet 5.5 up to 200K or split by section (6.5); an agent's first call 15K and any later call 50K; a Copilot turn 30K (inferred) | Anthropic says models have an "attention budget" that they draw on as context grows, since n tokens form "n² pairwise relationships" ([Anthropic, Sep 2025](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/effective-context-engineering-for-ai-agents)). Manus reports an input-to-output ratio "around 100:1" and about 50 tool calls per task, so input is where cost sits ([Manus, Jul 2025](https://manus.im/blog/Context-Engineering-for-AI-Agents-Lessons-from-Building-Manus)) | A model that chooses what enters its own context. Trigger: evals show the fixed priority order dropping items that answers needed |
| Prompt composition and order | After the cached prefix of 6.4, the variable part runs: typed task and item state, then documents and tool results, then the step's question. Code appends a one-line reminder of the item's question and open checks after the newest tool results on every turn. When several questions go to one document, the document joins the cached prefix and only the question changes (inferred) | Anthropic advises placing long documents "near the top of your prompt, above your query", and reports that "Queries at the end can improve response quality by up to 30 percent in tests" ([Anthropic docs, undated](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/prompt-engineering/claude-prompting-best-practices)). Manus has its agent rewrite a todo.md as it works, to avoid "lost-in-the-middle" issues ([Manus, Jul 2025](https://manus.im/blog/Context-Engineering-for-AI-Agents-Lessons-from-Building-Manus)) | A model that edits its own plan, as Manus's agent does. Never for status work: code renders the checklist from the item row |
| Preload or fetch just in time | Preload only what every step needs and is small: the test or question definition, the item's typed facts and prior verdict ids. Fetch evidence text, past investigations and long results by handle (6.4); a tool result over about 5,000 tokens is stored and replaced by a preview, tighter than LangChain's 20,000 because CCP's runs are short (inferred). Tier 2 stays fully preloaded, with no tools | Anthropic recommends a "hybrid strategy, retrieving some data up front for speed", as Claude Code loads CLAUDE.md up front and uses glob and grep for the rest ([Anthropic, Sep 2025](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/effective-context-engineering-for-ai-agents)). Deep Agents swaps results over 20,000 tokens for "a file path reference and a preview of the first 10 lines" ([LangChain, Jan 2026](https://www.langchain.com/blog/context-management-for-deepagents)) | Anthropic's server-side tool-result clearing, which by default fires at 100,000 input tokens and keeps the last three tool uses ([Anthropic docs, undated](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/context-editing)). CCP's view does the swap itself, so the run log shows exactly what the model saw (inferred). Trigger: an agent loop whose ceiling must pass 100K |
| Progress records for multi-run tasks | Each long task keeps its progress as typed Postgres rows: a task row and one row per item (status, attempts, evidence ids, open question, last run id). Only the runner changes an item's status, after the 5.4 gates pass. A run may leave a short hand-off note in the run log, which the next run reads as a hint, never as evidence (inferred) | Anthropic's long-running harness tracks over 200 features in a JSON list whose pass flag is all a coding session may change, since the model "is less likely to inappropriately change or overwrite JSON files compared to Markdown files" ([Anthropic, Nov 2025](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/effective-harnesses-for-long-running-agents)). Anthropic's docs advise "structured formats for state data" and git as the log ([Anthropic docs, undated](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/prompt-engineering/claude-prompting-best-practices)) | Free-form notes, such as the NOTES.md file in Anthropic's post ([Anthropic, Sep 2025](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/effective-context-engineering-for-ai-agents)), as the record. Never; per-tenant notes wait for the memory trigger in 6.4 |
| Sub-agent context isolation | A fanned-out item (6.4, Orchestration) runs in a fresh context holding only its question, typed facts and handles, never the parent's history. It returns a typed result of at most about 1,500 tokens (ids, offsets, fields, a one-line reason); the parent sees only that, and the full sub-run stays in the run log (inferred) | Anthropic's sub-agents may each use "tens of thousands of tokens or more" yet hand back a short summary (3.4) ([Anthropic, Sep 2025](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/effective-context-engineering-for-ai-agents)). Its long-running app harness linked planner, generator and evaluator through files, not shared context: "Communication was handled via files" ([Anthropic, Mar 2026](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/harness-design-long-running-apps)) | Giving a sub-agent the parent's whole context. Trigger: an item that cannot be stated without the parent's reasoning, which usually means the item list is wrong (inferred) |
| Thinking and effort settings | Each bundle sets thinking and effort per call type; nothing relies on defaults, which differ by model. Tier 2 on Haiku 5.5 runs with thinking off, checked on fixtures against low effort; tier 3 on Sonnet 5.5 stays at medium, with high only where a sweep shows a gain. Today only Sonnet, Opus, Fable and Mythos ids get thinking settings, so tier 2 moved to Haiku 5.5 unchanged would think by default inside a 2,048-token cap and could stop mid-JSON (`llm/anthropic_client.py:58-79`, `:198`; `evaluators/tier2.py:278`; inferred) | Defaults are medium on Opus 5.5 and Haiku 5.5 and high on Sonnet 5.5, whose levels "are recalibrated", so Anthropic says to run "a fresh effort sweep" and, for multistep tool use, "start with `medium` for well-specified tasks". In long agent prompts at `low`, Haiku 5.5 "is more likely to skip a search, stop early, or skip a check" ([Anthropic docs, undated](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/effort)). Thinking is "On by default" and counts toward `max_tokens` ([Anthropic docs, undated](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/thinking)) | `xhigh` and `max`, aimed at runs "over 30 minutes" with "token budgets in the millions" ([Anthropic docs, undated](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/effort)). Trigger: an Opus 5.5 fresh-run tier (6.4, Model routing) measures a gain at them |
| Thinking blocks across tool turns | Within a run, the view passes every thinking block back byte for byte, as CCP's loop already does (`llm/anthropic_client.py:121-122`, `:328-329`; `agent/core.py:333`), and the run log keeps them so a resumed step rebuilds the same turn. Thinking and effort never change mid-run. With `display` left at "omitted", CCP holds only signed, encrypted blocks, and no reviewer or judge sees reasoning as justification (6.6) | Anthropic: "within a tool-use turn, pass thinking blocks back"; "Modified thinking blocks are rejected with a 400 error"; on the 5.5 models earlier thinking stays and counts "as input like any other conversation history". A thinking or effort change "starts a new cache prefix", and visible thinking "is never the raw chain of thought" ([Anthropic docs, undated](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/thinking)) | Thinking-block clearing, which invalidates the cache "at the point where clearing occurs" ([Anthropic docs, undated](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/context-editing)). Trigger: Copilot sessions that carry turns (6.4) and whose kept thinking fills over about a quarter of the turn's ceiling (inferred) |
| 1M-token windows | All four current models take 1M tokens, but CCP keeps the ceilings of the first row. The whole-document ceiling rises past 200K only after a position test: CCP documents padded to 100K, 200K and 400K tokens, with known facts placed early, middle and late, scored by checked citations (inferred). Above the ceiling, code splits by report section and merges, as 6.5 does across documents | Fable 5.2, Opus 5.5, Sonnet 5.5 and Haiku 5.5 each list a 1M window ([Anthropic docs, undated](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/models/overview)). There "1M is the default: you don't need a beta header", yet "As token count grows, accuracy and recall degrade" ([Anthropic docs, undated](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/context-windows)); Anthropic saw models "lose coherence on lengthy tasks as the context window fills" ([Anthropic, Mar 2026](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/harness-design-long-running-apps)) | Fable 5.2 at $8/$40 per million tokens, which Anthropic suggests "when your evals on Claude Opus 5.5 at higher effort still fall short" ([Anthropic docs, undated](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/models/overview)). Trigger: exactly that, on CCP's evals |
| Long-context cost and caching | Haiku 5.5 prompts stay under 100,000 tokens, cache reads included, hence the 90K ceiling. A document asked many questions sits in the cached prefix: 20 questions over a 150,000-token report on Sonnet 5.5 cost about $0.66 of input with a 5-minute cache, against $6.00 uncached (inferred arithmetic). The 1-hour cache, at twice base input to write, serves only read sessions with gaps over 5 minutes (inferred) | From Claude 4.6 on, models other than Haiku 5.5 bill the 1M window at standard rates: "A 900k-token request is billed at the same per-token rate as a 9k-token request". A Haiku 5.5 request over 100,000 tokens pays five times the rate "even when part of its prompt is a cache hit"; Sonnet 5.5 cache hits cost 0.05 times base input ([Anthropic docs, undated](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/about-claude/pricing)) | The Batches API at 50% off ([Anthropic docs, undated](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/models/overview)) for overnight audit-prep extraction. Trigger: its zero-retention eligibility is confirmed (not checked here) and audit prep can wait for batch results |
| Multi-day tasks as resumable runs | A long task starts with a code step, not a model, that writes the item list from scoping facts: tests in scope for audit prep, questions mapped to controls for a questionnaire, requested documents and checklist items for a vendor review. Each item runs as short queued runs, each in a fresh context seeded from the task and item rows; resuming means taking the next open item. A model may propose new items, which code adds after checks (inferred) | Anthropic's harness has an initializer session write the feature list, an `init.sh` and a progress file; each later session reads "the git logs and progress files", picks "the highest-priority feature that's not yet done" and first runs a basic test ([Anthropic, Nov 2025](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/effective-harnesses-for-long-running-agents)). Cursor's week-long runs still need "periodic fresh starts to combat drift and tunnel vision" ([Cursor, Jan 2026](https://cursor.com/blog/scaling-agents)) | One agent working for hours across an audit. Anthropic dropped context resets from one harness once Opus 4.5 "largely removed" context anxiety, and reports a run of about 4 hours and $124 ([Anthropic, Mar 2026](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/harness-design-long-running-apps)); CCP keeps short runs for replay and budgets, not model limits (inferred). Trigger: a task whose items code cannot list; a planning run then proposes the list and code stores it |
| Drift, loops and premature completion | Code watches each run: a repeated identical tool call gets the stored result with a note, and a third repeat ends the run in `needs_review`. A task is done only when code finds every item final with its gates passed; no model marks anything done. Per task, code tracks items done, retries per item and runs without progress, and parks the task for a person after three runs that change nothing (inferred) | Anthropic saw later sessions "see that progress had been made, and declare the job done", and Claude "mark a feature as complete without proper testing" ([Anthropic, Nov 2025](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/effective-harnesses-for-long-running-agents)). Cursor reports that Opus 4.5 "tends to stop earlier and take shortcuts when convenient" ([Cursor, Jan 2026](https://cursor.com/blog/scaling-agents)). LangChain calls goal drift "the most insidious failure mode" ([LangChain, Jan 2026](https://www.langchain.com/blog/context-management-for-deepagents)) | A model judge that decides whether work continues, as Cursor's does after each cycle ([Cursor, Jan 2026](https://cursor.com/blog/scaling-agents)). Trigger: a task whose completion code cannot check, such as a readiness narrative; the judge then flags and a person decides |
| Resuming after a human wait | A wait holds no context (3.5). When the person answers, code refreshes facts and re-runs the rule, which may settle the item with no model call. Otherwise a new run starts from the stored question, the answer as quoted, untrusted text (6.6), the typed state and handles to the evidence cited before, re-read as of now; old messages, thinking and cache are not reused (inferred) | Anthropic's docs: "design your state artifacts so that context recovery is fast when a new session starts" ([Anthropic docs, undated](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/context-windows)). Cache entries last 5 minutes or 1 hour ([Anthropic docs, undated](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/about-claude/pricing)), so after a wait of hours nothing cached survives anyway (inferred) | A model-written hand-off summary as the seed, in the style of Amp's Handoff (6.4). Trigger: answers that only make sense with the earlier conversation, such as multi-turn auditor clarifications; the summary is then stored in the run log and a person can edit it before use |
| Per-task token and time budgets | Keep per-run caps, but count tokens as the peak context of any one call plus dollars, not a running sum. Today cache reads count in full against the 60,000-token cap and cache writes not at all (`llm/anthropic_client.py:316`, `llm/budget.py:74`, `config.py:91-93`), so an eight-turn loop can trip the cap while costing about ten cents (inferred). Each task row adds caps: dollars forecast from item count times the p95 cost per item, a deadline, at most three runs per item, and an alert at 1.5 times the forecast (inferred) | Anthropic: "Effort is soft guidance. `max_tokens` is a strict limit." ([Anthropic docs, undated](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/thinking)). The 5.5 models get no injected token-budget tags; Anthropic points to task budgets, in beta, instead ([Anthropic docs, undated](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/context-windows)). Anthropic reports a 6-hour, $200 harness run against 20 minutes and $9 for a solo agent ([Anthropic, Mar 2026](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/harness-design-long-running-apps)) | Telling the model its remaining budget through task budgets; 6.4 (Model routing) sets that trigger |

**How CCP's long tasks run.** The plans below are inferred from the rows above; only the cited facts are sourced.

- **Audit preparation, over weeks.** Items are the in-scope tests for the audit period, written from scoping facts. Most settle in tier 1 with no model; the gap list is a query over item rows, not a model summary.
- **Vendor review, over days.** Items are the requested documents and the review checklist. Each document that arrives gets one whole-document read, quotes first (6.5), cached for its checklist questions; a missing document is a wait row.
- **An investigation that waits on a person.** The run ends by storing its question as a row, and the answer starts a new run; after three runs a person takes the item.
- **A questionnaire of hundreds of questions.** Code maps questions to controls and verdicts first (6.5, Questionnaires). Each remaining question runs in its own call, calls on one topic share a cached prefix of the same policies, and an answer goes stale when a verdict it relied on changes.
- **Why item runs stay minutes long.** METR's horizon is the task length at which an agent "is predicted to succeed half the time", on tasks "designed to be self-contained and well-specified"; METR calls measurements "above 16 hrs" unreliable and noted in September 2026 that the page is no longer actively updated ([METR, Sep 2026](https://metr.org/time-horizons/)). Its 2025 paper found the horizon doubling about every seven months since 2019 ([METR, Mar 2025](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.14499)). A coin flip at hours is no basis for audit work, so items stay short and code checks each one (inferred).
- **Context probes join the eval suite.** LangChain plants a fact early, forces summarization and checks that the agent recovers it, and stress-tests by compressing at 10 to 20% of the window ([LangChain, Jan 2026](https://www.langchain.com/blog/context-management-for-deepagents)). CCP adds four probes per graph: a fact planted early in a long run, re-reading a swapped-out result by handle, resuming an item without redoing finished work, and a cited span placed early, middle and late in a long document.

### 6.8 Multi-agent systems and harness engineering

3.2 and the Orchestration row of 6.4 set the rule: one writer, and fan-out only from code. This subsection decides how CCP fans out, which multi-agent failures it guards against, where A2A fits, and how it builds, tests and measures the harness around each agent loop. In the 2025-2026 evidence, quality comes more from the harness (prompts, tool descriptions, stop rules and checks) than from adding agents, so that is where a team of three to five should spend its effort (inferred).

| Topic | Decision for CCP | Frontier precedent | Not now, and the trigger |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| When to fan out | Fan out only reading work that splits into independent items: vendor reviews (one run per vendor), the questionnaire questions that code could not answer from mapped verdicts (6.5), and audit-prep gap lists (one run per control family). Investigating one failure, reviewing one policy, Copilot turns, control mapping and remediation stay single runs, because each step depends on the last. Code lists the items; no model decides the split | Anthropic's research system scales effort to the query: "Simple fact-finding requires just 1 agent with 3-10 tool calls", while complex research may use more than 10 subagents ([Anthropic, Jun 2025](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/multi-agent-research-system)). Claude Code's docs say parallel subagents work best "when the research paths don't depend on each other", and keep work whose phases "share significant context" in the main conversation ([Claude Code docs, undated](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/sub-agents)) | A model that decides how many workers to start. Trigger: a reading task whose items code cannot list, on which one run scores below the 45% mark in 6.4 |
| The fan-out contract | Each item is its own queued run, keyed by parent run, item id and bundle (3.5), with typed inputs (tenant, item, as-of date), a fixed read-only tool list, its own budget, and a Pydantic output whose claims carry pointer citations. Code checks each result (schema, 5.4) and merges by key; a failed item becomes `needs_review` alone and never blocks the rest. Only code starts workers: no model gets a spawn tool | Anthropic's subagent briefs need "an objective, an output format, guidance on the tools and sources to use, and clear task boundaries"; vaguer ones made subagents run "the exact same searches". Its subagents store their outputs and "pass lightweight references back to the coordinator" ([Anthropic, Jun 2025](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/multi-agent-research-system)). Claude Code, by contrast, lets subagents spawn their own, "up to three layers below the main conversation" ([Claude Code docs, undated](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/sub-agents)) | Workers that message each other or share a scratchpad, and fan-out as branches inside one graph thread. Trigger: none; items that need each other's results are one item (inferred) |
| Planner, worker and judge | The planner is code: scoping facts and the control catalogue produce one checklist row per item, and only code marks a row done. The worker is a model run. The judge is code first (schema, citations, catalogue bounds, the dry run of 6.4), then, for high-value drafts only, a clean-context model reviewer whose verdict is a signal (3.2) | Cursor reports that its weeks-long runs worked only with planners, workers and a judge after each cycle: twenty equal agents with locks ran at "the effective throughput of two or three", and without a hierarchy "agents became risk-averse" ([Cursor, Jan 2026](https://cursor.com/blog/scaling-agents)). Anthropic's long-running harness starts from a JSON list of over 200 features marked failing, and agents may change only each feature's `passes` field ([Anthropic, Nov 2025](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/effective-harnesses-for-long-running-agents)). Cognition's reviewer shares no context with the coder and, Cognition reports, finds "an average of 2 bugs per PR" ([Cognition, Apr 2026](https://cognition.com/blog/multi-agents-working)) | A model planner. Trigger: an open-ended sweep, such as readiness across every framework, whose items code cannot list; the planner then proposes items, and code checks them against the catalogue before any worker starts (inferred) |
| Hand-offs between agents | No conversational hand-offs. Work moves between graphs as stored, typed records read by id: a Copilot request for an investigation starts a fresh Investigator run with typed inputs, and its draft is a row that a person or the Policy Agent reads. No run inherits another's transcript or rights | In the OpenAI Agents SDK a hand-off is a tool call, after which the new agent by default "gets to see the entire previous conversation history"; nested history "does not redact sensitive data", and input guardrails run only on the first agent ([OpenAI Agents SDK docs, undated](https://openai.github.io/openai-agents-python/handoffs/)). Claude Code subagents get "only this system prompt plus basic environment details" ([Claude Code docs, undated](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/sub-agents)) | Routing Copilot questions to specialist agents. Trigger: Copilot logs show intents that one tool list cannot serve (the 20-tool trigger in 6.4); then code routes each intent to a fresh run |
| Multi-agent failures and their guards | Guard each MAST class in code: specification failures with typed briefs, fixed tools, caps and loop detection (rows below); misalignment between agents by giving them no channel to each other; verification failures with code checks before merge and before approval, never a model reviewer alone | MAST labelled over 1,600 traces from 7 multi-agent frameworks into 14 failure modes in 3 classes, with inter-annotator kappa of 0.88 ([Cemri et al., Oct 2025](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.13657)). The commonest were step repetition (15.7%), reasoning-action mismatch (13.2%) and unawareness of termination conditions (12.4%). The authors found that "many existing verifiers perform only superficial checks"; a task-level verification step raised ChatDev's success by 15.6%, yet "not all failure modes are resolved" ([Cemri et al., Oct 2025](https://arxiv.org/html/2503.13657v3)) | A model that watches other agents' runs. Trigger: labelled traces show a failure class that code checks miss |
| A2A beside MCP | Neither expose nor consume A2A now. CCP's outside surface stays REST, and later the MCP server generated from the registry (3.3): read tools for customers' and auditors' agents, under their own credentials. A message from an outside agent is never an approval or evidence; an A2A endpoint, if added, wraps the same tools and stored proposals with no new rights (inferred) | A2A 1.0.0 shipped on 12 Mar 2026 with breaking changes, including OAuth flows that "remove implicit/password, add device code / pkce"; 1.0.1 followed in May ([A2A, May 2026](https://github.com/a2aproject/A2A/releases)). A steering committee from AWS, Cisco, Google, IBM Research, Microsoft, Salesforce, SAP and ServiceNow maintains it, and the project calls the protocols complementary: "MCP is for agent-to-tool communication", A2A "for agent-to-agent communication" ([A2A, undated](https://a2a-protocol.org/latest/)). Adoption counts appear only secondhand, so none is given | An A2A endpoint. Trigger: two or more enterprise buyers or audit firms ask to exchange evidence requests agent to agent; then map A2A tasks onto registry tools, using OAuth and the signed Agent Cards added in 0.3.0 |
| System prompt and starting brief | One prompt file per graph role, in fixed sections: role and scope, what the run must not decide, tool guidance, stop and abstain rules, and the output contract, with a few examples from the labelled set. Code builds the starting brief, as the Investigator's `load_context` node does (`agent/graphs/investigator.py:291-312`), so the model spends no calls finding its bearings. Start small, and add a line only for a failure seen in evals, naming that case (inferred) | Anthropic aims for the "right altitude", between "hardcoding complex, brittle logic" and "vague, high-level guidance", in sections such as `<instructions>`, `## Tool guidance` and `## Output description`, starting from "a minimal prompt" ([Anthropic, Sep 2025](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/effective-context-engineering-for-ai-agents)). Factory layers tool descriptions, system prompts and mid-run "System Notifications", and reports that giving start-up system information as shell output cut redundant commands ([Factory, Sep 2025](https://factory.com/news/terminal-bench)). Cursor: "the prompts matter more" ([Cursor, Jan 2026](https://cursor.com/blog/scaling-agents)) | Mid-run reminders, such as Factory's notifications. Trigger: evals show runs drifting from the brief in late turns, which 8-call caps make unlikely (inferred) |
| Tool descriptions are harness code | Tool descriptions, parameter notes and error texts belong to the bundle: versioned, hashed and gated like prompts (6.6, row 8). Each tool gets a contract test that calls it with good, wrong and missing arguments and checks that the typed result says what to try next. A model may draft better descriptions offline from failed traces; a person accepts them, and the eval gate decides | Anthropic's tool-testing agent "rewrites the tool description to avoid failures", which, Anthropic reports, cut task completion time for later agents by 40% ([Anthropic, Jun 2025](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/multi-agent-research-system)). Factory treats tool descriptions as one of its three prompt layers and limits tools "to essential operations" ([Factory, Sep 2025](https://factory.com/news/terminal-bench)) | Description rewrites applied without review. Trigger: none on any path that feeds a status |
| Verification and self-correction | Allow one correction round, driven by code: when a check fails (a citation that does not resolve, a schema error, an action outside the catalogue), the run gets one turn with the typed failure, the check runs again, and a second failure gives `needs_review`. Today a schema mismatch is only logged (`agent/core.py:256-259`), and the verify node drops an item whose citation fails, with no chance to repair it (repository root `tests/unit/test_agent_core.py:33`) | Anthropic found that "letting the agent know when a tool is failing and letting it adapt works surprisingly well" ([Anthropic, Jun 2025](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/multi-agent-research-system)). In its long-running harness, agents marked features done after unit tests or curl calls missed end-to-end failures, until prompted to test end to end ([Anthropic, Nov 2025](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/effective-harnesses-for-long-running-agents)). Claude Code's agent teams can block a task from completing with a `TaskCompleted` hook ([Claude Code docs, undated](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/agent-teams)) | Critique or debate between models, and judge-driven retries (6.4). Trigger: a drafting workflow where the critic meets the 85% expert-agreement bar (7.1) and one round shows a measured gain |
| Stopping conditions and loop detection | One stop function returns a typed reason: normal end, cap (6.4), three tool errors in a row (6.4), refusal or truncation, or a loop (the same tool and arguments three times, or the same result three times running). Any stop but a normal end gives `needs_review` with its reason. Today the loop only counts calls, a refusal or truncation is only logged, and the budget enforces its cap on a separate path (`agent/core.py:311`, `:331-338`; `llm/budget.py:88-90`) | Step repetition and unawareness of termination conditions are two of MAST's three commonest failures (row above). Pydantic AI flags the same call 5 times within 10, the same result 3 times in a row, or A-B-A-B alternation, and warns "A nudge doesn't stop a model that ignores it" ([Pydantic AI docs, undated](https://pydantic.dev/docs/ai/harness/detect-loops/)) | Nudging a looping run and letting it go on. Trigger: evals show nudged runs end correctly more often than stopped ones |
| Error recovery and retries | One policy per error class. Transient errors (429, 529, timeouts) retry in code with backoff and never reach the model; tool errors return to the model as typed results and count toward the three-error stop; bad model output gets the one correction round, then `needs_review`. Today a node retries up to 3 times with jittered backoff (`agent/core.py:52-63`, `:154`), and since the whole tool loop is one node (`:311`), a retry restarts it and samples earlier turns again (inferred); record each model call as its own step (3.5) | Anthropic: "Agents are stateful and errors compound", so it built systems that "resume from where the agent was when the errors occurred" ([Anthropic, Jun 2025](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/multi-agent-research-system)). Claude Code's agent-team docs warn that "Teammates may stop after encountering errors instead of recovering" ([Claude Code docs, undated](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/agent-teams)) | Retrying a failed call on another model. Trigger: as for fallbacks in 6.6 (row 9, which model answers) |
| Testing the harness | Four test layers run in CI on the stub model. Structural tests (graph shape, allowlists, no write imports) exist (`agent/core.py:89-107`; repository root `tests/unit/test_layering.py:1-10`); add single-step tests on a fixed history, fuzzed tool results fed through the stub's chat handler (`llm/client.py:242-244`), and replay of recorded run-log trajectories against each candidate bundle. A fuzzed result (empty, truncated, malformed, oversized, an error, a planted instruction) must end in `needs_review` or a cited proposal, never a status | LangChain says "about half of our test cases looked like single step evals", stopped before the tools node; it records HTTP requests and replays them in tests, and gives each run "a fresh, clean environment" ([LangChain, Dec 2025](https://www.langchain.com/blog/evaluating-deep-agents-our-learnings)). Anthropic began its research evals with "about 20 queries representing real usage patterns" and grades end states rather than each turn ([Anthropic, Jun 2025](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/multi-agent-research-system)) | Fuzzing against the live model in CI. Trigger: fan-out ships; then a nightly fuzz run on the live model in the eval organization (3.7) |
| Harness metrics | Per graph and bundle, from the run log: stops by reason, tool-error and repeated-call rates per tool, correction rounds and how often they succeed, the share of runs that pass code checks first time, and tokens per accepted result (6.4). For fan-out, add items per parent run, failed items and merge rejections. A bundle that raises loop or cap stops does not ship (inferred) | On BrowseComp, Anthropic reports that "token usage by itself explains 80% of the variance", with tool calls and model choice the other two factors ([Anthropic, Jun 2025](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/multi-agent-research-system)). AWS's AgentCore Evaluations ship built-in "tool selection accuracy" and "tool parameter accuracy" evaluators ([AWS, Mar 2026](https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/aws/amazon-bedrock-agentcore-adds-quality-evaluations-and-policy-controls-for-deploying-trusted-ai-agents/)) | Per-tenant harness dashboards. Trigger: a second bundle runs in production beside the first, as in a canary (6.6, row 8) |

**How CCP's graphs should be built and tested**

- **Shape.** One graph per role, at most one loop node, and every other node code, as in the Investigator's six nodes (`agent/graphs/investigator.py:291-312`). The stop function and the correction round become nodes or edges, so they show in the node path.
- **Declared paths.** Each graph declares its node paths, as the Investigator's `TRAJECTORY` does (`agent/graphs/investigator.py:41-45`; repository root `tests/unit/test_agent_core.py:156`), and the run log flags any run whose path was not declared (inferred).
- **Fan-out outside the graph.** The launcher, the per-item runs and the merge live in the queue and the runners (row 2), so no graph holds another run's state.
- **Not checked.** OpenAI's harness-engineering post still returned HTTP 403 on 9 Nov 2026, so its figures are not used. There are still no public multi-agent results on compliance work (3.8).

### 6.9 Judges, feedback loops and training

Judges, reviewer feedback and training form one loop: experts label, judges stretch those labels across more runs, and the labels decide whether training ever pays. Section 5 fixed the graders and 6.6 fixed what judges see; this table settles how judges are calibrated, chosen and re-checked, how reviewer decisions become labelled data within consent, and when to train rather than prompt. In our reading the answer on training is "not yet", and the work that matters now is recording labels that CCP does not keep today.

| Topic | Decision for CCP | Frontier precedent | Not now, and the trigger |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Judge verdict format | Keep one yes-or-no verdict per criterion, with an overall pass only when every criterion is met (`evals/judge.py:74-86`, `:156`). Mark each criterion as a blocker or not; a failed blocker scores the case zero. The reference that 5.1 requires arrives as facts (code-verified spans, the expert's key points), never as a model answer to copy (`evals/judge.py:1-3`) | Husain: "Binary evaluations force clearer thinking and more consistent labeling" ([Husain, Sep 2026](https://hamel.dev/blog/posts/evals-faq/)). Cognition grades each criterion as a blocker, where a fail scores zero, or a non-blocker ([Cognition, Jun 2026](https://cognition.com/blog/frontier-code)). In Intercom's simulations, "if any single criterion fails, the Simulation fails" ([Intercom, Aug 2026](https://www.intercom.com/blog/announcing-evals-and-releases/)) | Scores from 1 to 5 or 0 to 1. Never for anything that gates; drafts are ranked pairwise instead (6.6) |
| Calibration protocol | Split each failure mode's 100 to 200 expert labels (5.1) into a dev half for prompt work and a held-out test half, which alone reports TPR and TNR with intervals. Read the result against the expert ceiling from the double-labelled 20% (5.3), and rewrite any criterion two experts cannot agree on before tuning a judge. Today calibration runs on one 20-case set whose intervals its own header calls wide (`evals/calibration.py:1-8`; repository root `spec.md:2236`) | OpenAI's GDPval grader agreed with experts 65.7% of the time, against 70.8% between experts ([GDPval, Oct 2025](https://arxiv.org/html/2510.04374v1)). In HealthBench, physician-physician and model-physician agreement both ran "from 55% to 75%" ([HealthBench, May 2025](https://arxiv.org/html/2505.08775v1)). Husain asks for "at least 50 passing and 50 failing examples" when aligning a judge ([Husain, Sep 2026](https://hamel.dev/blog/posts/evals-faq/)) | Stretching scarce labels with judge scores, as prediction-powered inference does. Trigger: a quality rate is reported from judged production samples. Never for false-PASS bounds, which rest on expert labels (6.6) |
| Judge bias guards | Keep the order, length and paraphrase checks (`evals/judge.py:179-216`) as a gate on every judge, prompt or rubric change, and run pairwise draft comparisons in both orders (inferred). Add a self-preference guard in code: a judged case refuses to run when the judge model wrote the output. Today both default to `claude-haiku-4-5`, so tier-2 reasoning is graded by its author (`config.py:73-75`; repository root `spec.md:1688`) | Evaluators' preference for their own text showed a "linear correlation" with their ability to recognize it ([Panickssery et al., Apr 2024](https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.13076)). Cognition tested its graders with deliberately bad "hack reports" and with calibration solutions spanning 0 to 100% quality ([Cognition, Jun 2026](https://cognition.com/blog/frontier-code)) | Several judges voting (6.6) or a trained debiasing model. Trigger: a bias check still fails after two prompt fixes |
| Judge model and family | Choose each judge per rubric by its test-split TPR and TNR, starting with the cheapest Claude model that differs from the producer: Sonnet 5.5 for Haiku 5.5 extractions, Haiku 5.5 or Opus 5.5 for Sonnet 5.5 agents (inferred). Judges on customer data run in the zero-retention organization. A judge from another family runs only on fixture or synthetic suites, to catch blind spots that Claude models share | Husain finds one model for task and judge "usually fine" ([Husain, Sep 2026](https://hamel.dev/blog/posts/evals-faq/)); CCP keeps its stricter rule because self-preference is measurable (row 3) (inferred). On 931 labelled agent trajectories, "the LLM backbone matters most", and two backbones erred in opposite directions, one conservative and one permissive ([MobileJudgeBench, Aug 2026](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11434)). OpenAI picked GPT-4.1 over reasoning models as HealthBench's grader for "physician-level agreement without the costs and latency" ([HealthBench, May 2025](https://arxiv.org/html/2505.08775v1)) | A fine-tuned judge. Trigger: judge volume makes cost material and no Claude judge clears the bar; it then follows the last row |
| Judge drift and re-calibration | Version the judge like a bundle (model id, `judge_v1` prompt, rubric hashes); a change to it, a rubric or the judged model re-runs the test split and bias checks as a pre-migration gate (5.3). Move it off `claude-haiku-4-5` soon: it relies on temperature 0 (`llm/anthropic_client.py:73`; repository root `spec.md:2233`), which newer models reject, so self-consistency over three runs becomes a real measure, not "near 1.0 by construction" (repository root `spec.md:1714`). Each month an expert relabels 20 judged production items, and agreement outside the test interval triggers re-calibration (inferred) | Anthropic lists Haiku 4.5 as retiring "Not sooner than October 15, 2026", a date now passed, with at least 60 days' notice. API version 2026-10-21 removes `temperature`, and earlier versions return a 400 for a non-default value on Claude 4.7 and later ([Anthropic deprecations, undated](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/about-claude/model-deprecations.md)). Husain: when gold goes stale, "Use regular error analysis to find new problems and update your examples or reference answers" ([Husain, Sep 2026](https://hamel.dev/blog/posts/evals-faq/)) | Automatic judge-drift alarms. Trigger: the monthly audit misses a drift that error analysis later finds |
| Trajectory judges | Grade agent paths in code first: tools, arguments, tenant scope, budgets and no write before approval (5.2; repository root `spec.md:2273`). A model judge reads the run log's typed calls and results, never the reasoning (6.6), and answers narrow questions such as "was a required evidence type never searched?". It sorts failures for error analysis and gates nothing until calibrated like any judge | Agent-as-a-Judge, an agent that evaluates other agents' work, "dramatically outperforms LLM-as-a-Judge" and was "as reliable as our human evaluation baseline" on 55 tasks with 365 requirements ([Zhuge et al., Oct 2024](https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.10934)). Sierra runs a separate agent as "an independent judge" ([Sierra, Aug 2025](https://sierra.ai/blog/simulations-the-secret-behind-every-great-agent)). NIST's CAISI designs and validates transcript scanners step by step to find agents cheating on evals ([NIST, Feb 2026](https://www.nist.gov/blogs/caisi-research-blog/analyzing-transcripts-ai-agent-evaluations)) | A tool-using agent judge on customer data. Trigger: a path question that code cannot answer and a tool-less judge misses the bar; it then gets read-only tools under the same tenant binding (5.5) |
| Sampling, cadence and cost | Judges score a nightly sample per workflow, stratified by verdict, confidence band, tenant and evidence type, with AI-derived PASS results and near-threshold items oversampled. People label first where code and judge disagree, where the judge answers "unknown", and where an expert finds a needs\_review decidable; beside 5.3's weekly traces, a deeper pass of 100 or more fresh traces every two to four weeks may rename failure modes. Cost comes from the usage each judge call records (`evals/judge.py:161-167`): 200 items of about 4,000 tokens a night is roughly $50 to $100 a month per workflow at 6.5's Sonnet 5.5 price (inferred); only synthetic suites use batches | Husain: review "at least 100+ fresh traces each review cycle", with cycles of "2-4 weeks" and 10 to 20 traces weekly "focusing on outliers" ([Husain, Sep 2026](https://hamel.dev/blog/posts/evals-faq/)). Decagon stratifies judge samples by issue type and customer segment, and "Human labelers audit a subset" ([Decagon, Jul 2025](https://decagon.ai/blog/evaluation-engine-ai-agents)) | Judging every production run. Trigger: a calibrated detector for a failure mode that sampling misses, such as unsupported claims in sent questionnaire answers |
| Capturing reviewer signal | Today an approval stores an event, the reviewer and a free-text note (`storage/migrations/0011_remediation_approvals.sql:44-53`). Add an insert-only label row per AI-read field or draft: right or wrong, the corrected value and span, a reason from a short closed list (wrong value, period or entity; unsupported claim; missing evidence), the bundle, the seconds spent and a consent scope. A plain approval is stored but counts as nothing; only a wrong mark, an edit or a rejection with a reason becomes a candidate label | Cursor trains its Tab model on accepts and rejects, needing "1.5 to 2 hours to roll out a checkpoint and collect the data for the next step"; it reports 21% fewer suggestions and a 28% higher accept rate ([Cursor, Sep 2025](https://cursor.com/blog/tab-rl)). There acceptance is the goal; in CCP it is not proof of correctness (6.6) (inferred) | Rewards or quality metrics built from acceptance. Never for status-deciding steps; drafts may track edit distance beside expert labels |
| Consent and data rights | Keep three uses apart in contracts and in code. A tenant's labels and captured cases replay only for that tenant; pooling them into shared gold or judge calibration needs a written opt-in, strips identifiers and follows the evidence's erasure (6.5, U10), and training needs a second opt-in. Customer evidence never enters the eval organization, and every suite build checks each label's consent scope (inferred) | Intercom trains Fin's custom retrieval and ranking models on resolved conversations, removes identifying data with Microsoft Presidio, and lets customers opt out, who may then miss the gains ([Fin, undated](https://fin.ai/training)). Decagon lets customers "opt out of experimentation" ([Decagon, Jul 2025](https://decagon.ai/blog/evaluation-engine-ai-agents)) | Never: an opt-out default like Fin's. Compliance evidence is more sensitive than support chats (inferred) |
| Failures into eval cases | Each production failure opens a candidate case: a wrong mark, an auditor rejection, a Copilot thumbs-down, or a needs\_review an expert found decidable. `ccp evals capture` stores the observed output as the expectation (`evals/capture.py:5-6`); a failure case takes its expectation from an expert label instead, and joins gold only after review. Each failure pattern also gets a templated synthetic twin, so batches, other-family judges and prompt optimizers can use it in the eval organization | Intercom's Monitors check "every live conversation", and flagged ones become new simulations, a loop it calls "Eval-driven delivery" ([Intercom, Aug 2026](https://www.intercom.com/blog/announcing-evals-and-releases/)). Ramp turns user-flagged errors into test cases ([Ramp, undated](https://engineering.ramp.com/post/how-to-build-agents-users-can-trust)). Husain: "Add confirmed failures as you find them" ([Husain, Sep 2026](https://hamel.dev/blog/posts/evals-faq/)) | Model-generated twins at scale. Trigger: templates cannot express a pattern; then a generator in the eval organization, with an expert checking each twin |
| Prompt optimization | Allowed for any prompt, offline in the eval organization on synthetic suites, once two rounds of manual error analysis stop helping. The optimizer sees only the dev split; its output is an ordinary prompt change, measured on real gold (5.3) and ablated line by line (6.6) | Across six tasks, GEPA, which rewrites prompts by reflecting on traces, beat reinforcement learning (GRPO) by 6% on average and up to 20% with up to 35 times fewer rollouts, and beat the MIPROv2 optimizer by over 10% ([Agrawal et al., Feb 2026](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.19457)). Husain: "Inspect a few disagreements manually before trying automated prompt tuning", which can need "hundreds or thousands of evaluations" ([Husain, Sep 2026](https://hamel.dev/blog/posts/evals-faq/)) | Optimizers on customer data. Trigger: synthetic twins stop predicting production error rates for a prompt, and the tenants involved have opted in |
| Fine-tuning, reinforcement fine-tuning and distillation | Train and distil nothing for now, as 6.4 decided. If CCP trains, it uses LoRA (small adapter weights) on an open-weight model in its own AWS account, for one tool-less step whose output code still checks; the trained model is a new bundle with its own retirement date to track (inferred) | Bedrock's fine-tuning list holds one Claude model, Claude 3 Haiku ([AWS docs, undated](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/custom-model-fine-tuning.html)), which Anthropic retired from its API on 20 Apr 2026 ([Anthropic deprecations, undated](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/about-claude/model-deprecations.md)); Bedrock's reinforcement fine-tuning covers Nova 2 Lite, gpt-oss-20B and Qwen3 32B ([AWS docs, undated](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/reinforcement-fine-tuning.html)). OpenAI says that from 6 Jan 2027 "Active existing customers will no longer be able to create new fine-tuning jobs", and fine-tuned models shut down with their base ([OpenAI, May 2026](https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/deprecations)). LoRA matched full fine-tuning for reinforcement learning "even with small ranks" ([Thinking Machines, Sep 2025](https://thinkingmachines.ai/blog/lora/)) | Reinforcement fine-tuning with code-computed rewards. Trigger: the last row's test passes for a step whose reward code can compute, such as the span and value checks of 5.4 (inferred) |
| Embedding and reranker training | Log training pairs now, at no model cost: each query and its candidates, positives from spans that passed 5.4 and a reviewer marked right (6.6), and hard negatives from spans marked wrong, each with its consent scope. Train nothing until 6.5's triggers fire; the first candidate is a reranker, since it sits behind today's search and can be recalibrated on the golden set (inferred) | Fin's first custom models target retrieval and ranking, not reply writing ([Fin, undated](https://fin.ai/training)). 6.5 records Intercom's distilled reranker and Cursor's embeddings labelled from agent traces | A trained embedder, which forces a full re-embed and a new index version per release (6.5). Trigger: a trained reranker still leaves a recall gap on a query slice |
| When to train at all | Train only when all of these hold: one narrow, tool-less step; label definitions unchanged for two quarters, with experts at their ceiling; volume where cost or latency matters; a gap left after prompting, model choice, retrieval fixes and prompt optimization; consented data and hosting inside zero retention; and an owner for retraining when the base model retires. A trained model re-earns its autonomy level from the start (4.4) | AWS advises starting reinforcement fine-tuning with "100-200 examples" and says it "might be unnecessary" once rewards pass 95%; its claim of accuracy gains "up to 66% on average" is its own ([AWS docs, undated](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/reinforcement-fine-tuning.html)). Fin's fine-tuned 14B model and million-example classifier (6.4) rest on support-chat volume that CCP's steps do not have (inferred) | A training pipeline or model registry. Trigger: the first step passes every test in this row |

**Start now, because later choices rest on it.**

- **The label row and its consent scope** (rows 8 and 9). Every later judge, eval case and training decision depends on them, and labels cannot be collected after the fact.
- **A judge that differs from its author, off Haiku 4.5** (rows 3 to 5). It is the cheapest fix on this list, and it removes a known bias before more cases are judged.

### 6.10 Human-agent interaction and computer use

Sections 3.6, 4.4, 5.5 and 6.6 set who approves what and how reviews are checked; this subsection covers the screens around them (6.1, row 11) and computer-use agents. Decision 6 already bars agents from changing customer systems through a browser, so the last three rows cover only reading evidence from consoles that have no API. Across frontier products the pattern is the same: show records rather than narration, let people stop a run at any time, be honest about what undo cannot undo, and keep models away from browsers and passwords while an API, an export or an upload will do.

| Topic | Decision for CCP | Frontier precedent | Not now, and the trigger |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Showing a plan before an agent acts | Show a plan only when a person starts a long run and waits for it, such as a Copilot investigation or audit prep: the questions, the read tools and the budget, which the person can edit or cancel. The plan is advice, not permission; the registry still enforces tools and budgets (inferred) | In Claude Code's plan mode, Claude "reads files and proposes a plan but makes no edits until you approve" ([Claude Code docs](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/common-workflows)). When Claude Code stops itself, the top reason is to offer a choice between approaches, 35% of stops ([Anthropic, Feb 2026](https://www.anthropic.com/research/measuring-agent-autonomy), self-reported) | Plans for scheduled verdict runs, whose path is the fixed graph (3.1). Trigger: a graph that chooses its own steps |
| Progress and streaming | Progress comes from the run log, not the model's narration: each recorded step (tool called, evidence read, check passed) reaches the page as it lands, over server-sent events (inferred). Stream prose for drafts and Copilot replies, never a status, and show a citation as a link only once code has checked it: the Copilot already holds back status answers (`agent/graphs/copilot.py:314-315`), and a stream that has started cannot be retried (`llm/anthropic_client.py:168-170`) | Anthropic asks product teams for "trustworthy visibility into what agents are doing" ([Anthropic, Feb 2026](https://www.anthropic.com/research/measuring-agent-autonomy)). Claude "sometimes acts as if its actions succeeded without explicitly checking their results" ([Claude docs](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/agents-and-tools/tool-use/computer-use-tool)), so narration is not proof | WebSockets or a separate streaming service. Trigger: two people working live in one space, such as the auditor workspace (LT4) |
| Interrupt and steer | Every run has a Stop button. Stop sets a flag that the runner checks at the next node boundary, where the run budget already stops runs (`agent/core.py:151-153`); the run ends cancelled, keeps its record and changes no status. Steering a status-deciding run means restarting it with the person's note stored as an input, so replay sees it | Experienced Claude Code users interrupt in about 9% of turns, new users in 5% ([Anthropic, Feb 2026](https://www.anthropic.com/research/measuring-agent-autonomy), self-reported). A message typed while Claude Code works can have no checkpoint of its own ([Claude Code docs](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/checkpointing)). An OpenClaw agent ignored stop commands sent from a phone ([OWASP, Apr 2026](https://genai.owasp.org/2026/04/14/owasp-genai-exploit-round-up-report-q1-2026/)), so Stop must not depend on the agent | Steering inside a running investigation. Trigger: restarts waste more than a few minutes of work per run (inferred) |
| The approvals inbox | One inbox for CCP's four item kinds (`approvals/store.py:16`), each showing the code-written payload, its hash, its expiry and whether it can be undone. The choices are approve, reject, or change and resubmit, which makes a new proposal with a new hash for someone else to approve. No decision, or an expired one, means no action; today a tier-3 resume without an action approves, as reviewer "unknown" (`agent/graphs/tier3.py:231-232`) | LangChain's middleware lets a reviewer approve, edit, reject or respond, and warns that "Significant modifications to the original arguments may cause the model to re-evaluate its approach" ([LangChain docs](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langchain/human-in-the-loop)). Intercom Fin's human-in-the-loop beta sends Fin's draft "as written, without review" when no teammate answers in time, after 24 hours by default ([Intercom Help, Nov 2026](https://www.intercom.com/help/en/articles/16198358-ask-a-teammate-for-help-with-fin-s-replies-human-in-the-loop)); CCP rejects that default | Approving from email, Slack or a phone; until then they carry a link, never an approve button or evidence text. Trigger: passkey-signed approvals (Decision 5). Auth0's agent approvals show the authorization details before a token issues ([Auth0, Nov 2025](https://auth0.com/blog/auth0-for-ai-agents-generally-available/)) |
| Explanations and citations in the UI | Code writes the "why" of every status from the rule version and the facts it used, each fact linked to its checked pointer citation and shown as a highlighted span on the page image or JSON field (6.5). Model prose sits under a "summary" label and is never the reason. A citation that fails its check shows as unverified text, not a link | Ramp says "Every decision needs a 'why'", and its policy agent links to the policy sections its reasoning uses ([Ramp](https://engineering.ramp.com/post/how-to-build-agents-users-can-trust)). In a contest of 272,000 attacks, successful ones often left "no clue of compromise in the final user facing response" ([IPI Arena, Mar 2026](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15714)), so a fluent summary proves nothing | Model-written plain-English rule text. Trigger: auditors ask for it; then write it once per rule version and review it like code |
| Showing uncertainty and abstention | Needs review and not assessed always show a reason code and the one action that clears it, such as reconnecting AWS or uploading the policy for the period, styled as normal states, not errors. Show the factors behind computed confidence (coverage, citation rate), never a bare score; today a threshold on that score decides which drafts a person sees (`agent/graphs/tier3.py:214`) | Ramp let its models say "I'm not sure", sends those cases to people, says it matters that "unsure doesn't look like an error state", and replaced numeric confidence with Approve, Reject and Needs review ([Ramp](https://engineering.ramp.com/post/how-to-build-agents-users-can-trust)). Fin says when it found no answer, and when unsure may "express uncertainty" and ask for clarification ([Intercom Help, Nov 2026](https://www.intercom.com/help/en/articles/7837535-fin-ai-agent-faqs)) | Calibrated probabilities on screen. Trigger: per-test calibration on gold sets of the size 6.6 sets |
| Undo and reversal | Code labels each proposal by how it can be undone: one click for internal changes (assignments, snoozes, drafts), close or revert for pull requests, and for external sends (trust page, questionnaire) only a retraction, which is a new approved action. External sends wait ten minutes with a cancel button before they leave CCP (inferred). Undo is never a model action | Claude Code's rewind restores only its own file edits; a file that only shell commands changed "stays as it is", and checkpoints are "Not a replacement for version control" ([Claude Code docs](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/checkpointing)). OWASP recommends hard confirmation, an emergency stop and easy rollback for delete, send, pay and publish actions ([OWASP, Apr 2026](https://genai.owasp.org/2026/04/14/owasp-genai-exploit-round-up-report-q1-2026/)) | Undo in a customer's cloud. Trigger: the runbook role (Decision 6, LT10), with runbooks that check their own result |
| Copilot conversation scope and refusals | The Copilot answers only about this tenant's controls, evidence, verdicts and findings, from read tools, and says plainly when it found nothing. It gives no audit opinion, legal advice or status of its own; it points to the rollup with its as-of date or to a named owner, and logs its own refusals apart from the model's, which U5 counts. What it suggests becomes a proposal in the inbox | Fin answers "based solely on your support content", says when it found no answer, and hands sensitive topics such as refunds to people through escalation rules ([Intercom Help, Nov 2026](https://www.intercom.com/help/en/articles/7837535-fin-ai-agent-faqs)) | A Copilot for buyers on the trust center. Trigger: buyers ask for one (LT4); it would answer only from published, approved content |
| Disclosure that content is AI-made | Keep the provenance badge (6.1, row 11) on every AI-read field and draft. Anything that leaves CCP names who approved it and when, and the export and audit trail keep the AI-drafted flag; 6.6 already sends the AI Act Article 50 question to counsel | After a teammate's input, Fin's reply names the reviewing teammate in web chat and in the email footer ([Intercom Help, Nov 2026](https://www.intercom.com/help/en/articles/16198358-ask-a-teammate-for-help-with-fin-s-replies-human-in-the-loop)) | Machine-readable marking of AI text. Trigger: counsel finds Article 50(2) reaches CCP's drafts (6.6) |
| Reviewer ergonomics and trust calibration | Facts and source spans come first; the agent's reasoning and tool log sit behind a toggle, since people, like judges, can be swayed by a story (inferred from 6.6). New tenants and workflows get per-item review. A workflow that graduates under 4.4 swaps prompts for a daily digest of what ran, with one-click reopen, and keeps its seeded checks | As Claude Code users gain experience, full auto-approve rises from about 20% to over 40% of sessions while interrupts also rise (self-reported); Anthropic warns that rules that "prescribe specific interaction patterns" can "create friction without necessarily producing safety benefits" ([Anthropic, Feb 2026](https://www.anthropic.com/research/measuring-agent-autonomy)). Fin's teammate view shows the draft and its confidence, with reasoning behind a "Show reasoning" option ([Intercom Help, Nov 2026](https://www.intercom.com/help/en/articles/16198358-ask-a-teammate-for-help-with-fin-s-replies-human-in-the-loop)) | A minimum time per item, since time spent did not predict accuracy in the study 6.6 cites. Trigger: seeded catch rates fall |
| Computer-use agents for evidence collection | None in v1 or v2. Evidence comes from APIs and exports, then from people's uploads, and no model drives a browser or desktop with a customer's sign-in. A console with no API gets the next two rows, not an agent | GUI computer use is the weakest surface in Anthropic's tests: the chance an attacker succeeds within 15 attempts was 12.5% for Sonnet 5.5 there against 1.0% in tool use, and 2.8% against 0.4% for Opus 5.5 ([Anthropic, Sep 2026](https://www.anthropic.com/claude-sonnet-5-5-system-card), [Sep 2026](https://www.anthropic.com/claude-opus-5-5-system-card), self-reported). Claude's docs advise a dedicated VM, no login details and a domain allowlist, and warn that instructions "contained in images might override your instructions" ([Claude docs](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/agents-and-tools/tool-use/computer-use-tool)); Google's say its tool "may contain errors and security vulnerabilities" ([Google docs](https://aistudio.google.com/docs/computer-use)). Vanta's browser evidence agent doubled its completion rate once a verifier checked its work, from a baseline Vanta does not give ([Vanta, Jun 2026](https://www.vanta.com/resources/vantas-agent-development-principles), self-reported) | A computer-use agent in a sandbox with a read-only account. Trigger: three customers need the same API-less system, uploads keep failing the verifier, and the attack suite (6.6) covers screenshots. Claude's tool is zero-retention eligible, except Covered Models ([Claude docs](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/agents-and-tools/tool-use/computer-use-tool)); OSWorld scores, verified under one fixed setup ([OSWorld](https://osworld-v1.xlang.ai/)), do not show that a console was read completely (inferred) |
| Browser automation without a model | For a console with no API or export, CCP writes a reviewed Playwright script per page and runs it with no model: a disposable browser limited to that console's domain, signed in with a customer-created read-only account held by the credential broker (5.5). A changed page ends the run as not assessed instead of calling a model to repair it. A model may draft scripts offline for an engineer to review | Stagehand records actions on a first run and replays them "with no LLM calls", but its hosted cache calls the model again when a page changes ([Stagehand docs](https://docs.stagehand.dev/v3/best-practices/caching)); CCP turns that repair off. 1Password injects credentials at run time, only after a person approves, so "the AI agent and underlying LLM never need to see nor handle" them ([1Password, Oct 2025](https://1password.com/blog/closing-the-credential-risk-gap-for-browser-use-ai-agents)) | A collector that customers run, or credentials drawn from their own vault. Trigger: a customer will not give CCP even a read-only console account |
| Screenshots as evidence | A screenshot is raw evidence: stored write-once with its hash, transcribed by Claude in the zero-retention organization (6.5, LT2), and counted only after code checks its date and subject. An upload is badged customer-attested; only a capture by CCP's own script, stored with its URL, account, capture time and page HTML, is system-observed (inferred). One screenshot shows one moment, so it never covers a Type II period alone | Oneleet's evidence guide asks for when a screenshot was taken, says to "Add URLs, report names, system IDs", and wants Type II evidence to cover the whole period ([Oneleet docs](https://docs.oneleet.com/guides/what-makes-good-evidence/)) | Recorded sessions as evidence. Trigger: an auditor asks to watch a collection run; then keep the script's trace beside the capture (inferred) |

Where this lands in the roadmap (Section 8):

- **With MT4 and UI v1:** the inbox rules above (a missing or expired decision never acts; change and resubmit; links, not approve buttons, in messages), run pages fed from the run log with a Stop button, reason codes with a next action, and code-written explanations over checked spans.
- **With LT2:** screenshots as raw, transcribed, badged evidence, with uploads as the only path for consoles that have no API.
- **Off the roadmap until a trigger fires:** computer-use agents and model-free browser scripts. Neither needs work now beyond keeping the credential broker (5.5) able to hold a read-only console account.

### 6.11 Coverage map: every agentic concern and where it is decided

This is the checklist for the agent side of CCP: each concern an agentic system has to settle, the decision in one line, and where the report makes it. Review it whenever a new workflow, model or tool arrives; a concern with no row is a gap.

| Concern | Decision in one line | Where |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Workflow or agent | Code fixes the path; an agent loops only where the path is unknown, and no agent sets a status | 3.1, 4.2, 4.3, 6.4 |
| Multi-agent work | One writer; fan out only independent reading items, each a queued run with a schema that code checks and merges; no agent-to-agent channel and no A2A for now | 3.2, 6.4, 6.8 |
| Harness engineering | Prompts, tool descriptions, graph, budgets and effort ship as versioned bundles behind per-model evals; one stop function, one code-driven correction round, and harness tests on every bundle | 6.4, 6.6, 6.8 |
| Context engineering | Each prompt is rebuilt from the run log within a budget for its call type: stable content first for caching, evidence by handle, old tool results swapped for handles | 3.4, 6.4, 6.7 |
| Reasoning settings | Effort and thinking settings are pinned per step in the bundle and changed only through the eval gates | 6.4, 6.6, 6.7 |
| Long-running work | Long tasks split into short, resumable runs linked by typed state and progress records; human waits are rows; drift, loops and early "done" end in needs\_review | 3.5, 6.4, 6.7, 6.8 |
| Long context | Used only to read one document end to end, quotes first; questions across documents go through retrieval | 6.5, 6.7 |
| Memory | Per-tenant notes confirmed by a person, never counted as evidence, with poisoning tests before any write path | 3.4, 6.4 |
| Tools and MCP | One typed registry behind a default-deny policy check; a small, fixed tool set per graph; REST now and a read-only MCP server later, as outputs | 3.3, 6.4, 6.6, Decision 7 |
| Retrieval | Layered, per-tenant hybrid search with exact-match, outline and read-span tools for agents, evaluated on its own | 6.5, Decision 10 |
| Structured output and citations | Typed fields with pointer citations that code checks before any rule uses them | 5.4, 6.5 |
| Models and routing | Haiku 5.5 for extraction, Sonnet 5.5 for agents, Opus 5.5 where measured; no model switch inside a run; no safety fallbacks on tool-using graphs | 3.7, 6.4, 6.6, Decision 9 |
| Verification before action | Code gates and a dry-run re-check of the rule; a clean-context model reviewer is a signal only | 5.4, 6.4, 6.8 |
| Evals and release gates | Four gates plus production sampling and a daily canary; gold sets weighted towards FAIL cases and sized to the false-PASS bound | 5.1 to 5.3, 6.6 |
| Judges | One yes-or-no verdict per criterion, calibrated on a held-out expert split, never the model that wrote the output, re-checked monthly | 5.1, 6.6, 6.9 |
| Feedback and training | Insert-only review labels with a consent scope start now; prompt optimization only offline on synthetic suites; no fine-tuning until every test in 6.9's last row passes | 6.9 |
| Prompt injection | Evidence never enters the user turn; an attack suite built from CCP's own evidence types; policy enforced outside the model | 5.5, 6.6 |
| Authorization and agent identity | A default-deny policy check, one principal per graph type, and credentials only in the executor | 5.5, 6.6 |
