| Human oversight | People approve every consequential action, framework mapping, new service class and drafted test; approvals are payload-bound and single-use; reviews are designed to catch errors; autonomy is earned per workflow and demoted on any bundle change | 3.6, 4.4, 6.6, 6.10, 6.12, Decision 5 |
| Human-agent interaction | Progress from the run log, a Stop that does not rely on the agent, code-written explanations, and every abstention with a reason and a next step | 6.10 |
| Computer use | No computer-use agents in v1 or v2: APIs, exports and uploads first; a reviewed, model-free browser script only when its trigger fires | 6.10, Decision 6 |
| Durable execution | CCP's hardened Postgres queue with idempotent steps and each model call recorded as its own step; DBOS only on a trigger | 3.5, 6.8, Decision 4 |
| Observability | The run log is the record; traces carry ids and hashes, not evidence; engineers read content only through logged replay | 3.7, 6.6 |
| Cost and latency | Cache-hit alerts, cost per accepted result, and rate-limit headroom kept for interactive work | 6.3, 6.6 |
| Model lifecycle | Each new release compared with the incumbent within a month; migrations finish within six weeks; the Bedrock path tested each quarter | 3.7, 6.6 |
| Tenancy and data | Forced row-level security, per-tenant retrieval, memory and labels, and zero-retention model calls | 6.3, 6.5, 6.9, Decision 8 |
| Standards and governance | A quarterly map to the OWASP Agentic Top 10 and AIUC-1; AI Act questions go to counsel | 6.6 |
| Compliance catalogue | Frameworks, controls, tests and mappings as versioned data; every mapping reviewed by a person; each verdict records the catalogue version | 6.12, MT8, LT5 |
| Test coverage and new services | An inventory-based coverage map; class playbooks fill gaps with FAIL or needs\_review only; offline drafting promotes reviewed mappings to deterministic tests | 6.12, LT7, LT11 |
| Eval and guardrail tooling | Gates live in CCP's repository; Langfuse as the viewer; Promptfoo for attack suites; Cedar when policy outgrows a page; Presidio-style redaction; classifiers only as flags | 6.13 |

### 6.12 Coverage engine: the catalogue, deterministic tests, service playbooks and test drafting

CCP measures its coverage of a customer's systems from an inventory; it never assumes it. Deterministic tests decide wherever they exist. Where they do not yet cover a resource, a fixed playbook for the resource's service class reads it under the guardrails of 6.6 and may raise a FAIL or needs\_review, never a PASS, and an offline pipeline turns reviewed mappings into deterministic tests, so coverage converges on code. Writing a test is the judgement step, so the model works when tests are written, not when they run.

> **Diagram:** Coverage engine · deterministic path, bounded playbooks, people and escalation, offline test drafting (`docs/diagrams/workflow-ccp-coverage-engine/ccp-coverage-engine.html`).

**The catalogue.**

| Part | Decision | Where |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Frameworks and requirements | Versioned files in the repository with effective dates; SOC 2 and ISO 27001 first | MT8 |
| Controls | One common control catalogue. Each control maps to many framework requirements and states its intents per service class: encrypted, not public, logged, backed up, least privilege | MT8, this section |
| Tests | Each test declares its evaluation (deterministic or interpretive), evidence types, subject kind, rule, tenant parameters, freshness, the sources that must be complete and its expected subjects. Today seven of CCP's ten tests are deterministic and three read documents (`knowledge/tests.yaml`, `knowledge/graph.py:111-115`) | MT8, 5.4 |
| Framework-to-control mappings | A model may propose a mapping with a confidence score and the option to abstain; a named reviewer accepts each one; every acceptance makes a new catalogue version; mappings never earn autonomy | LT5, 3.6, 4.3 |
| Policies | Policies are evidence. An interpretive test reads a policy version against the control's clause list, quotes first; code checks the quotes and a rule decides. The Policy Agent only proposes edits | 6.5, 5.4 |
| Tenant custom controls and frameworks | Tenant-scoped catalogue versions on top of the common catalogue, in the same declarative format and through the same review and eval gates (inferred) | LT11 |
| Applicability and provenance | Code decides which tests apply from scoping facts; every verdict and state row records the catalogue and mapping versions in force | 6.3, 6.5 |

**Covering a cloud account, step by step.**

| Step | Decision | Guardrail |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1. Inventory | The AWS connector lists every resource type in every account and region, from AWS Config where the customer records it and from listing APIs otherwise. Complete scopes give each test its expected subjects (MT8) | A read-only role with explicit denies on fields that can hold secrets (6.2); a partial listing makes the run incomplete, never a pass |
| 2. Service classes | About ten to twelve classes: data store, compute, network edge, identity, keys and secrets, logging, messaging, AI and ML, and so on. A reviewed lookup assigns known AWS types. For an unknown type a model proposes a class from the type's schema and a person approves it | Until a class is approved, the type's resources show not assessed, because a wrong class would hide a gap |
| 3. Coverage map | For each resource: which intents of its class apply, and which deterministic test covers each | Gaps are listed, never assumed covered |
| 4. Deterministic tests | Where a test exists, its rule decides, as for any test | Rules only |
| 5. Service playbook for a gap | One fixed LangGraph subgraph per class, versioned in the release bundle: read the resource's configuration (responses land in the raw zone first), map known fields in code and unfamiliar fields with one tool-less model call that cites JSON paths, check each cited path and value in code, and let the class rule decide | Code picks the playbook; fixed node path, read-only tool list and budgets; the result can be FAIL or needs\_review, never PASS |
| 6. Escalation | A field the playbook cannot map or check goes to the Investigator, which can only propose | 6.4, 6.8 |
| 7. Test drafting, offline | Reviewed playbook mappings and the schemas of new resource types go to a drafting pipeline. A model drafts a declarative check from the schema and the class intents; the check must compile, with up to three retries, pass recorded and synthetic good and bad configurations, match in a shadow diff against the playbook and AWS Security Hub findings, and win a reviewer's approval for audit relevance | Public schemas and synthetic fixtures only, so no customer evidence reaches the pipeline (LT7) |
| 8. Promotion | The approved check ships in a release bundle and becomes a deterministic test; the playbook path retires for that type | Tracked: days from first sighting to a deterministic test |
| 9. New AWS services | A weekly diff of AWS's resource-type registry queues drafts before customers adopt a service. A type first seen in a customer's account shows not assessed at once and joins the queue | AWS keeps its job-function read policies "up to date to include support for new services" ([AWS](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/IAM/latest/UserGuide/access_policies_job-functions.html)), so reads usually work from day one; CCP's explicit denies still apply |

**Keeping playbooks from going rogue.**

- **Read-only end to end.** No write tool exists on this path, and the AWS role cannot write.
- **Asymmetric authority.** A playbook result can raise FAIL or needs\_review. PASS needs a deterministic test, or a playbook mapping that has earned the 1% false-PASS bound for that resource type under 4.4.
- **Bounded runs.** Code lists the items and picks the playbook; tools are fixed; each run has budgets, and each tenant and AWS account a daily cap, because AWS rate limits are shared with the customer's own automation. One stop function with loop detection ends a stuck run (6.8), and kill switches work per playbook, class and tenant.
- **Untrusted input.** Tags, names and descriptions reach the model only as data inside tool results, with invisible Unicode stripped, and the attack suite plants instructions in them (6.6).
- **Release discipline.** Playbooks, prompts and class tables ship in versioned bundles behind per-model evals; any change re-runs shadow and demotes autonomy until it passes (6.6).

**Evals.**

- Synthetic AWS accounts with seeded resources and misconfigurations: discovery recall (no type missed), class accuracy, mapping accuracy (right path and value), recall of seeded FAILs, and zero false PASS in shadow.
- Golden configurations per playbook: secure and insecure variants generated from each resource schema.
- The drafting pipeline: compile rate, fixture pass rate, reviewer acceptance, audit relevance and defects in the first 30 days (LT7).
- Production: coverage per tenant, unknown types, the share of AI-mapped results, reviewer overturns, and time to a deterministic test.

**Precedent.** Drata generated over 1,000 executable JSON tests for 118 resources and 165 controls across AWS, Azure and GCP with Claude Sonnet, from resource schemas, controls and existing tests. Its validator fed errors back for up to three attempts, auditors reviewed every test, and the model ran only when tests were written, at about $20 to $30 per resource. It generated from each resource to the controls, because the other direction left gaps, and most rejected tests worked but did not matter to an audit ([Drata, Dec 2025](https://drata.com/blog/building-ai-compliance-tests)). Vanta's custom tests work on one resource type at a time ([Vanta](https://help.vanta.com/en/articles/11345522-creating-custom-tests)); Prowler's open catalogue holds 662 AWS checks ([Prowler](https://github.com/prowler-cloud/prowler)); and Stripe's compliance agents answer only question types whose quality has been measured ([Stripe on the AWS ML blog, Jun 2026](https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/production-grade-ai-agents-for-financial-compliance-lessons-from-stripe/)), the same idea as one measured playbook per class.

### 6.13 Tools for evals and guardrails

The rule for tools follows the rule for decisions: the gates CCP relies on live in CCP's repository and code, and outside tools serve as viewers, generators of attacks or extra flags. Hosted eval products are consolidating quickly, so nothing that decides a release depends on one vendor staying the same.

| Need | CCP's pick | What teams also use, and why not here | Source |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Eval harness and release gates | CCP's own pytest-based runner (`evals/` in the repository), with datasets, graders, rubrics and thresholds as files, and the statistics of 5.2 in code | Hosted eval platforms churn: OpenAI's hosted Evals shuts down on 30 Nov 2026 (5.3), and OpenAI agreed in March 2026 to acquire Promptfoo | [Promptfoo, Mar 2026](https://www.promptfoo.dev/blog/promptfoo-joining-openai/) |
| Traces, experiments and labelling views | Langfuse, already in use (MIT licence): production traces carry metadata only (6.6), while datasets, experiments and annotation queues serve synthetic and eval-organization data; labels on customer data stay in CCP's own label table (6.9) | Braintrust has the most polished eval workflow, and Notion uses it, but it is proprietary; LangSmith fits LangGraph best, but self-hosting is an Enterprise add-on (in our reading of the earlier research) | [Langfuse, Jan 2026](https://langfuse.com/blog/joining-clickhouse), [Braintrust/Notion](https://braintrust.dev/customers/notion) |
| LLM judges | CCP's own judge module, extended as 6.9 decides: one yes-or-no verdict per criterion, calibration on a held-out expert split, and a block on self-grading (`evals/judge.py`, `evals/calibration.py`) | Judge frameworks add little over code CCP already has and must keep in the repository anyway (inferred) | 6.9 |
| Agent transcript analysis | Scanners over the run log that look for runs gaming an eval or skipping a required step, each validated like any judge | US CAISI uses Inspect Scout, from UK AISI and Meridian Labs, to find agents cheating on agentic evals | [NIST CAISI, Feb 2026](https://www.nist.gov/blogs/caisi-research-blog/analyzing-transcripts-ai-agent-evaluations) |
| Red-team and injection suites | Promptfoo, pinned to a version, for scheduled red-team and injection runs in CI and nightly, fed from CCP's own attack corpus in the repository (6.6); a third-party adaptive red team before the first production customer (Gate 2) | Promptfoo says it will remain open source and support many providers after joining OpenAI; pinning and an in-repo corpus keep CCP independent of that promise | [Promptfoo CI/CD docs](https://www.promptfoo.dev/docs/integrations/ci-cd/) |
| Authorization at the tool boundary | `policy.check`: Python and a YAML rule table, default-deny, with a log-only phase for new rules (6.6); Cedar once the table outgrows a page | AWS chose Cedar for agent policy because it is "readable by humans, and analyzable by machines" | [AWS, May 2026](https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/security/why-policy-in-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-chose-cedar-for-securing-agentic-workflows/) |
| Output and schema validation | Pydantic v2 models for every model output and tool result, structured outputs for tier 2, and code checks on every pointer citation (5.4) | Already CCP's stack; no extra library needed (inferred) | 5.4 |
| Prompt-injection flags | Structural defences first: evidence only in tool or document content, no write tools on agent paths, code decides. A small injection classifier may add a flag, never a gate | Meta reports that on AgentDojo its 86M-parameter Prompt Guard 2 cut attack success from 17.63% to 7.53%, and to 1.75% combined with an alignment check, while utility fell from 47.73% to 42.68%; adaptive attacks on the guardrails were not tested | [Chennabasappa et al., May 2025](https://arxiv.org/html/2505.03574) |
| PII and secret redaction | A Presidio-style PII detector plus CCP's secret patterns, in the trace export hook (6.6) and before any export, measured on CCP's own evidence types, since no public accuracy figures were found | Intercom removes identifying data with Microsoft Presidio before training Fin's models | [Fin, undated](https://fin.ai/training) |
| Content-safety classifiers and guardrail frameworks | Not adopted: Llama Guard, NeMo Guardrails, Guardrails AI and Bedrock Guardrails target harmful content, not whether a compliance claim is true, and add latency (inferred). Meta reports an English F1 of 61% at an 11% false-positive rate for Llama Guard 4 | Revisit if a Copilot opens to people outside the customer's team | 5.5 |
| Sandboxes | None, because no model-written code runs (6.4); a standard microVM sandbox when the 6.4 trigger fires | Anthropic's containment lesson in 6.4 | 6.4 |

## 7. Competitor-inspired improvements

The strongest ideas share one theme: keep the decision in code and make every AI contribution checkable. The approach and evidence columns are verified from the linked source; "Why it matters" is our reading; the last column is our recommendation, starting with adopt, adapt or reject.

### 7.1 From compliance platforms

| Company | Interesting approach | Evidence | Why it matters | What we should adopt or adapt |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Vanta | Every product action is one authorized service method, exposed to REST, CLI, the agent and MCP | [Vanta, Aug 2026](https://www.vanta.com/resources/give-your-agents-capabilities) | One permission check and one schema per action, so an agent cannot do what its user cannot | **Adopt.** CCP's tool registry becomes this capability layer; REST, agent tools and MCP are generated from it (3.3) |
| Vanta | An evidence verifier inside the agent loop doubled the browser agent's task completion | [Vanta, Jun 2026](https://www.vanta.com/resources/vantas-agent-development-principles), [Aug 2026](https://www.vanta.com/resources/build-verifiers-for-messy-problems) | Agents improve most from a checker's feedback, not longer prompts | **Adopt.** Code verifiers first (citations, dates, scope), then a calibrated model verifier; agents call them before proposing |
| Vanta | Tools shaped around information, with explicit truncation and a recovery path: about 27% fewer tokens and over 45% higher task completion | [Vanta, Sep 2026](https://www.vanta.com/resources/design-tools-around-information-not-apis) | A partial result shown as complete is a silent false-pass risk | **Adopt** in the tool registry: truncation is always explicit, and missing data is an error |
| Vanta | Auto Mode pre-approves eligible actions but never destructive ones; the event log records which agent acted, for whom | [product updates](https://help.vanta.com/en/articles/11345422-product-updates) | Cuts approval fatigue without giving up control of irreversible actions | **Adopt** as the approval tiers in 4.4, with an agent actor type in the audit log |
| Vanta | Published eval results: mapping precision from below 35% to 78%; judge agreement with experts from about 65-70% to 85-90% | [Vanta, Jun 2026](https://www.vanta.com/resources/vanta-ai-quality-evaluation-maturity-model) | The only published calibration bar in the category | **Adopt** 85% agreement with experts as the minimum before a judge gates anything |
| Drata | An offline LLM pipeline wrote 1,000+ deterministic tests, validated against Drata's test API and reviewed by auditors | [Drata, Dec 2025](https://drata.com/blog/AI-compliance-tests) | AI speeds up authoring while runtime verdicts stay deterministic | **Adopt** for connector checks: AI drafts offline; the conformance harness and a reviewer gate each check |
| Drata | The vendor agent asks before every write and withholds a recommendation when too many criteria are inconclusive; the mapping agent proposes with confidence | [TPRM Agent](https://help.drata.com/en/articles/16880143-getting-started-with-the-tprm-agent), [autonomy](https://help.drata.com/en/articles/16880162-tprm-agent-autonomy-settings), [mapping](https://help.drata.com/en/articles/16630433-framework-mapping-agent-ai-assisted-control-mapping-for-custom-frameworks) | Abstention and proposals are built into the product, not left to prompts | **Adopt** the withholding rule ("any step may abstain") and propose-then-map; **adapt** autonomy presets only once 4.4's metrics exist |
| Sprinto | Questionnaires run as verbatim fact extraction, answer direction, then writing, with a grounding check | [Aug 2026](https://docs.sprinto.com/product-updates/august-2026.md) | Separating facts from prose is extract-then-rule for documents | **Adapt.** Keep the passes; replace the model grounding check with CCP's code citation and support checks |
| Secureframe | Changes to built-in tests ship as versioned updates after a countdown | [test updates](https://support.secureframe.com/en/articles/15111262-test-activity-dashboard) | Customers see a rule change before it alters their status | **Adopt.** Rule versions with a notice period and a preview of the verdicts they would change |
| Scrut | A validation agent reproduces each finding before publishing it; every REST write needs an `Idempotency-Key` | [Sep 2026](https://help.scrut.io/docs/september-2026.md), [API](https://developer.scrut.io/) | An AI assertion becomes a fact only after it is shown again; retries cannot double-apply | **Adopt** both: code re-checks an agent's finding before it surfaces, and every write carries an idempotency key |
| Thoropass | Evidence frozen per audit; LLMs draft first-pass test results for its auditors | [help](https://help.thoropass.com/en/articles/9293724-why-can-t-i-reuse-evidence-from-previous-audits), [blog](https://www.thoropass.com/blog/mcp-for-audit-ai-auditor-judgment) | Auditors will increasingly consume structured evidence through AI | **Adapt.** Export hashed, as-of audit packages from bitemporal state, with each result's method |

### 7.2 From open-source products

| Project | Interesting approach | Evidence | Why it matters | What we should adopt or adapt |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Comp AI | Long-tail checks as a versioned JSON DSL in the database, snapshotted before every edit and hot-reloaded | [schema](https://github.com/trycompai/comp/blob/150fed5ade35be5c37f142b126aa10b1859aa26e/packages/db/prisma/schema/dynamic-integration.prisma#L1-L135) | Breadth without a deploy per check | **Adapt.** Declarative checks as versioned data files in the repository, shipped in the release bundle behind the same review and eval gates, not rows edited at runtime; without its unsandboxed code step; with explicit error results instead of empty ones |
| Comp AI | Findings reconcile only from complete, successful scans; unscanned scope stays unknown | [reconciliation](https://github.com/trycompai/comp/blob/150fed5ade35be5c37f142b126aa10b1859aa26e/apps/api/src/cloud-security/reconciliation.service.ts#L15-L71) | Prevents a false "resolved" after a partial sync | **Adopt** as CCP's sync completeness contract |
| Probo | Durable runs in Postgres: leased claims, a checkpoint per tool turn, and approval decisions that must cover exactly the pending tool calls | [service](https://github.com/getprobo/probo/blob/7bbe06ab6180d7099f4c4dd20e662520f792f348/pkg/agentexecution/service.go#L100-L157) | A tested human-approval design on the database CCP already uses | **Adopt** the semantics, and make approval mandatory per tool class (Probo enables it in no production agent) |
| Probo | Field-level provenance for AI-written data (source, confidence, status); human edits are never overwritten | [enrichment](https://github.com/getprobo/probo/blob/7bbe06ab6180d7099f4c4dd20e662520f792f348/pkg/thirdparty/common_third_party_enrichment.go#L132-L192) | Keeps AI-written values apart from curated ones | **Adapt.** Add a code check that the source supports the value before it counts as found |
| CISO Assistant | A read-only MCP profile that does not register write tools; an action ledger; an eval of tool selection against tool-surface size | [MCP server](https://github.com/intuitem/ciso-assistant-community/blob/a86708675840cc1628a927a3132a119afd4cf645/cli/ca_mcp/server.py#L312-L370) | The boundary lives in code, not in annotations | **Adopt** all three |

### 7.3 From AI-native entrants and adjacent agent companies

| Company | Interesting approach | Evidence | Why it matters | What we should adopt or adapt |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Harvey | A deterministic policy engine between agents and tools: permit, deny or ask; fails closed; tenant settings only tighten; tool definitions pinned | [Oct 2026](https://www.harvey.ai/blog/building-harveys-mcp-policy-engine) | Turns CCP's per-graph allowlists and approval stage into one policy point | **Adopt** in the tool gateway: a small Python policy module first, a policy language only if the rules multiply |
| Harvey | Citations point to sentence indices and are rendered from the source | [Apr 2026](https://www.harvey.ai/blog/rebuilding-harveys-review-algorithm) | Fuzzy-matched quotes are exactly where CCP's checker fails | **Adopt:** cite chunk and sentence ids, render quotes from the source, check support in code (5.4) |
| Harvey | A benchmark of atomic, binary criteria in which a task passes only if every criterion passes; the harness is MIT-licensed | [May 2026](https://harvey.ai/blog/introducing-harveys-legal-agent-benchmark) | Mirrors how auditors test a control: every attribute must hold | **Adopt** for CCP's golden sets |
| Sierra | Immutable release bundles of code, prompts, model versions and knowledge, with rollback; production conversations become regression tests | [Sierra](https://sierra.ai/blog/agent-development-life-cycle) | A verdict can name exactly what produced it | **Adopt:** each verdict records its bundle (model, prompt, schema, rule and index versions) |
| Sierra | Supervisors that intercept only on high-risk topics; calibrated monitors on everything else | [Sierra](https://sierra.ai/blog/agent-monitoring) | Blocking checks cost latency, so they belong where output is irreversible | **Adopt** the two tiers: block on customer-visible and external writes; monitor the rest |
| Ramp | Approve, reject or needs review instead of model confidence; an exposure budget per agent | [Ramp](https://builders.ramp.com/post/agentic-risk-operations) | Users' acceptance is a lenient ground truth, and caps bound the damage of a bad release | **Adopt** per-tenant caps on automated status changes; keep computed confidence internal |
| Anterior | A risk-ordered human review queue and continuous judge-versus-human agreement on live cases | [talk](https://ai.engineer/talks/mission-critical-evals-at-scale-learnings-from-100-000-medical-decisions) | Offline calibration drifts once real traffic arrives | **Adopt** for production monitoring (Section 5) |
| Anecdotes | Models draft deterministic analysis rules; the rule re-runs after remediation | [Agent Studio](https://www.anecdotes.ai/agent-studio) | Coverage grows without model verdicts | **Adopt** together with Drata's offline generation (7.1) |
| Decagon | Natural-language procedures backed by code, with tests generated and retired automatically | [Decagon](https://decagon.ai/resources/from-sops-to-agent-operating-procedures) | Lets compliance staff author procedures, but auto-updated tests can hide regressions | **Adapt:** authored procedures are welcome; any change to a test needs human approval |
| Trustero | A repeatability metric published without a method | [Trustero](https://www.trustero.com/trustero-ai) (vendor claim) | Stability is visible to auditors and buyers | **Adapt:** publish pass^k with its dataset and method |
| AIUC-1 | Agent certification with 1,000 to 5,000 red-team scenarios and quarterly retests | [AIUC-1](https://www.aiuc-1.com/) | An emerging buyer requirement for AI agents | **Adapt** as a checklist for CCP's own agents; decide on certification once buyers ask |

### 7.4 From agent engineering teams

| Source | Interesting approach | Evidence | Why it matters | What we should adopt or adapt |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Anthropic | pass^k for consistency; grade outcomes; start from 20-50 real failures | [Jan 2026](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/demystifying-evals-for-ai-agents) | A verdict must repeat on the same evidence | **Adopt** as the gate metric (Section 5) |
| Anthropic | A stateless harness with an append-only session log; credentials fetched from a vault through a proxy | [Apr 2026](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/managed-agents) | Recovery and the audit trail become one record; the model never holds secrets | **Adopt:** the run log is the record (3.4) and a credential broker serves the tools (3.3) |
| Anthropic | Tool search and usage examples raised accuracy from 49% to 74% and from 72% to 90% | [Nov 2025](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/advanced-tool-use) | CCP runs on Claude, so these figures apply more directly than to other models | **Adopt** when a workflow's tool count grows; measure on CCP's tasks first |
| Anthropic | A separate, sceptical evaluator agent beat self-grading, at about 20 times the cost | [Mar 2026](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/harness-design-long-running-apps) | Generators rate their own work too highly | **Adapt:** use it only on high-value outputs, after code checks |
| OpenAI | Rate each tool's risk; approval does not guarantee exactly-once execution; a run id is not authorization | [guide](https://cdn.openai.com/business-guides-and-resources/a-practical-guide-to-building-agents.pdf), [SDK](https://openai.github.io/openai-agents-python/human_in_the_loop/) | Approval security and idempotency are the application's job | **Adopt** in the approval stage (3.5) |
| Cognition | Multi-agent designs work when "writes stay single-threaded" | [Apr 2026](https://cognition.com/blog/multi-agents-working) | Parallel writers make conflicting decisions | **Adopt** as "graphs read, runners write" |
| Meta, Simon Willison | An agent may hold at most two of: untrusted input, sensitive data, and the ability to act externally | [Meta, Oct 2025](https://ai.meta.com/blog/practical-ai-agent-security/), [Willison, Jun 2025](https://simonwillison.net/2025/Jun/16/the-lethal-trifecta/) | A structural rule that does not depend on detecting injections | **Adopt** as a static check on every graph's tool allowlist (5.5) |

### 7.5 What we reject, and why

- **Model-chosen commands against customer infrastructure** (Comp AI's denylisted AWS executor) and **console changes through a browser extension** (Sprinto). In both, what a person approved and what ran can differ.
- **Full-write MCP with no read-only mode** (Secureframe, Comp AI). Writes arrive only as approval requests.
- **AI deciding whether a customer sees a failure** (Comp AI's self-heal hold). Store the raw outcome and the AI's triage as separate facts, and show needs\_review.
- **Tenant ids chosen by the model** (Probo's MCP tools). The tenant comes from the session.
- **Grounding by prompt alone** (Comp AI's questionnaires) and **input classifiers that fail open** (Probo). Use structural defences and code checks.
- **Parallel writer agents, and a general agent with hundreds of tools as the first agent.** Vanta needed domain tool loading once its agent passed 280 tools (2.1). Start with narrow workflows backed by verifiers.

## 8. Prioritized engineering roadmap

Fix correctness before breadth, change one architectural thing at a time, and land every item with a test that reproduces its defect or a metric that shows its effect. Ids NT, MT and LT belong to this report; the "Matches" column gives the ids in the [first report](https://claude.ai/artifact/WRau5vs9uFjkgBqzbC1Arj)'s roadmap (its Section F). Where a near-term row lists Q ids, its scope is the first report's scope, so the two documents agree. Sizes: S is up to about one engineer-week, M two to six, L more than six.

> **Diagram:** Roadmap · 5 workstreams across 3 horizons, 3 gates (rendered in ccp-target-architecture.pdf).

### 8.1 Near term: weeks 0 to 10, inside the current architecture

Order, following the first report: NT1 on day 1; NT2, NT3's upload fix and NT4's outbox fix in weeks 1 and 2; NT5 and the rest of NT4 in weeks 2 to 6, with a first NT8 baseline run in week 2 to measure NT5's effect; NT9 in weeks 3 to 6; NT10 in weeks 3 to 8, its judge fix before the nightly NT8 runs; NT3's secrets work and NT6 in weeks 5 to 8; NT7 and the rest of NT8 in weeks 6 to 10.

| # | Matches | Limitation | Change | Benefit | Why preferable | Size | Dependencies and migration | Risks and tradeoffs | Success metric |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |

Capacity (inferred): the first report estimates Q1 to Q13 at 17 to 24 engineer-weeks, NT9 adds 2 to 3 and NT10 another 2 to 3, so ten weeks need about 2.1 to 3 engineer-weeks a week: three engineers at about three-quarters to full time, or about eleven to fifteen weeks at the first report's 2 engineer-weeks a week.

**Amendments to the first report's quick wins.** The table keeps the first report's scopes. This design adds five things to them, which both documents should take together:

1. **Q11:** put the rule version and a hash of tenant parameters into the evaluation key, not only on the verdict, or a corrected rule over unchanged data is still dropped as a duplicate (Decision 3).
2. **Q12:** state that a 404 on any listing means "not visible" and makes the pull incomplete. GitHub answers 404 rather than 403 to a request not authorized for a private resource ([GitHub](https://docs.github.com/en/rest/using-the-rest-api/troubleshooting-the-rest-api), read 9 Nov 2026), and today that path ends in "no GitHub grants on record" and a GH-1 PASS (`evaluators/deterministic/gh1.py:42-49`).
3. **Q8:** mark truncated tool results; today they are cut at 20,000 characters, possibly mid-JSON, without the model being told (`llm/client.py:136`).
4. **Q13:** the different-family judge applies to fixture suites only (Decision 9); move tier 2 to Haiku 5.5 with thinking off as soon as NT10 lands, without waiting for a refusal measurement: every refusal becomes needs\_review, and the refusal rate is watched in production (answer to U5).
5. **New:** add NT9 to the quick wins and its lint to Gate 1. From 6.6, three small additions ride on existing items: NT5 moves evidence and tool output out of the user turn and strips invisible Unicode; NT3's redaction also covers traces, with a CI test that fails on any evidence text; and NT8's judges get the code-verified spans, not the agent's reasoning, with empty and punctuation-only outputs added as negative controls.

Two medium-term items extend the first report: MT2 adds Decision 4's triggers to M2's harden-first order, and MT6 accepts M7's HR aggregator only behind the sync contract (U8 in Section 9).

**Gate 1, week 10: before CCP takes real customer data or relies on a real model's output.** NT2 to NT7, NT9 and NT10 are merged, each with a test that reproduces its defect; CI is green on the private remote; a keyed eval run has measured the false-PASS rate on labelled cases and it is under the agreed limit; production calls go through a zero-retention organization and the Voyage training opt-out is on.

### 8.2 Medium term: months 3 to 8

Order: MT2 and MT3 start first, because MT4 and MT5 build on them; MT5 lands before any real tenant data; MT1 comes before MT6; MT7 runs throughout; MT8 follows MT1; MT9 follows MT3 and MT7; MT10 follows MT2, MT3 and NT10.

| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| MT1 | M1 | `pull()` is the whole contract (`connectors/base.py:49-54`): no connection record, cursor, completeness, rate limit or test harness; what an error means differs by endpoint | Connections as data with daily checks; sync runs of scopes that end complete, partial, failed or not visible; absence only from complete authoritative scopes; a per-endpoint error table; content-hash snapshots; backoff and rate buckets; a GitHub App; a conformance harness with recorded responses and negative controls | Every connector inherits completeness; absence becomes a proven fact | One contract beats each connector solving completeness alone; a unified-API vendor would drop provider ids and request-level receipts (inferred) | L | NT7; MT2 for schedules; fixture readers emit sync runs too | Harness upkeep; GitHub permissions differ by plan | The harness is green, negative controls included, for every live connector; at least 99% of syncs complete; an unchanged re-pull lands zero rows |

Then the DBOS spike: kill a worker mid-step, overlap two deploys, fail over Postgres, recover with three processes and no Conductor | Several workers become safe; resumes never sample a model again; every AI output can be rebuilt end to end | One queue model with transactional enqueue; after these fixes an engine adds little for minute-long jobs (Decision 4) (inferred) | L | NT4; additive columns and tables; `event_log` stays the outbox | CCP owns about a thousand lines of concurrency code (inferred); the engine triggers are reviewed each quarter | A chaos test with three workers and overlapping deploys loses or duplicates no verdict; no model call is sampled twice on resume; 100% of agent runs replay with an empty request diff |

Capacity (inferred): the first report estimates its M1 to M9 at 38 to 52 engineer-weeks; the run log (MT2), the chain (MT5) and pinned inputs with coverage (MT8) add about 8 to 12, MT9 adds about 4 to 6 and MT10 another 4 to 6, so about 54 to 76 over about 22 weeks. That needs four engineers at three-quarters to nearly full time; with fewer, MT6's identity-provider connector, MT9's model switch and MT10's fan-out launcher move to month 9.

**Gate 2, month 8: before long-term items and before a first production customer.** Every live connector passes the harness and records completeness (MT1); the crash and overlapping-deploy tests pass with three workers (MT2); only signed-in reviewers with the right role can approve, and every execution consumes a grant (MT4); the raw zone is in S3, `ccp audit verify` is green hourly, and a restore drill has caught a deliberately altered row (MT5); nightly real-model evals gate every release bundle (MT7); no requirement passes with an unassessed expected subject (MT8); the retrieval golden set meets its recall and distractor targets on the new models (MT9); and a third-party adaptive red team has tested the evidence paths (6.6).

### 8.3 Long term: months 8 to 18

| # | Matches | Limitation or opportunity | Change | Benefit | Why preferable | Size | Dependencies and migration | Risks and tradeoffs | Success metric |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| LT1 | L3 | One live action, a collaborator removal behind five gates (`remediation/gates.py:53-84`); cloud findings have no reviewable fix path | Investigator read tools on the connector clients (Config history in 7-day windows, CloudTrail lookups under 2 requests a second, a search for the resource's infrastructure code), landing every response as raw evidence first; code-written investigations when the rule explains the failure; the model picks a catalogue action and its parameters; code renders the diff from a versioned template, validates it offline and checks its scope; approval with payload and precondition hashes; the executor re-checks and opens the pull request with a one-repository, one-hour GitHub App token; PASS only from a re-read | Shorter time from FAIL to PASS, a record linking every step, and no AWS write credential | Drata and Vanta remediate through pull requests ([Drata](https://help.drata.com/en/articles/9930785-drata-compliance-as-code), [Vanta](https://developer.vanta.com/docs/quickstart/remediate-with-mcp.md)); model-chosen commands are rejected (Section 7.5) | L | MT1, MT2, MT4, MT6 | Customers without infrastructure code get guidance only; telling which resources are in code is unsolved (U7) | Zero executed diffs that differ from the approved hash; median time from FAIL to PASS; share of pull requests merged unchanged; reopen rate within 30 days |
| LT2 | L2 | Uploads must be PDF or text, and anything else is skipped (`connectors/manual/uploads.py:109-132`); scans and screenshots are not read | Segment each document version (sentence ids, table cells, pages); models cite segment ids or JSON Pointers and code renders the quotes; deterministic admissibility (date, period, subject, issuer); an adequacy verifier calibrated against auditors' accept-or-flag decisions; Claude's PDF input for scans; feedback seconds after upload; screenshots stored as raw evidence, transcribed and badged customer-attested or system-observed (6.10); shadow first | Customers fix weak evidence before the auditor sees it | Vanta's verifier is the best-documented approach ([Vanta, 5 Aug 2026](https://www.vanta.com/resources/build-verifiers-for-messy-problems)); CCP adds deterministic admissibility and pointer checks | L | MT7; a labelled sample per evidence type | Labelling cost; refusals on security screenshots (U5) | Agreement with auditor decisions of at least 85%; a false-accept upper bound under the agreed limit; re-upload rate |
| LT3 | L1 | No questionnaire feature | A fixed pipeline: applicability, library match, retrieval, tool-less fact extraction, answer direction from the current verdict where a question maps to a tested control, prose, then code checks; approved answers stored with their pointers and the verdicts they relied on, and marked stale when either changes; a person sends | Answers stay true as controls change, and each traces to evidence | Sprinto's staged passes ([Sprinto, Aug 2026](https://docs.sprinto.com/product-updates/august-2026.md)) plus CCP's live verdicts; no incumbent page describes invalidating an approved answer when its source changes (inferred) | L | MT3, MT4, MT7 | Messy formats; reviewer fatigue | Reviewer edit rate per question type; zero answers that contradict current state; zero unsupported claims in sampled audits |
| LT4 | L4 | Proofs exist only inside CCP, and nothing defines what CCP asserts outside itself | As-of packages from bitemporal state with each result's method, raw pointers, inclusion proofs and a signed manifest, sealed in compliance-mode storage, with corrections as deltas; a time-boxed, read-only auditor workspace; an attestation record of everything asserted outside CCP under a versioned publication policy keyed on provenance, completeness and freshness; a verifier customers and auditors can run | Auditors and buyers can check the record without trusting CCP | Auditor-side agents check that "every conclusion traces to its evidence" ([Fieldguide](https://www.fieldguide.com/products/agents/overview)); Thoropass freezes evidence per audit (Section 2.1) | L | MT4, MT5, MT8; interviews with two or three audit firms first | Auditors' acceptance is unknown (U1, U2) | Share of auditor requests answered from the package; an auditor verifies a package offline |
| LT5 | L5 | Mappings are hand-written YAML (`knowledge/controls.yaml`, `knowledge/frameworks/soc2.yaml`, `knowledge/frameworks/iso27001.yaml`) | Mapping proposals with computed confidence and abstention, reviewed one at a time, as an offline batch first; each accepted change makes a new catalogue version | New frameworks in days, every mapping reviewed | Drata's mapping agent proposes and applies nothing on its own ([Drata](https://help.drata.com/en/articles/16630433-framework-mapping-agent-ai-assisted-control-mapping-for-custom-frameworks)) | M | MT8; a named compliance reviewer | Reviewer load | Precision and recall at 3 on held-out frameworks; days to add a framework |
| LT6 | L6 | No published quality figures | A method card on every verdict and proposal; published results per AI workflow and release: method, datasets, false-PASS rate and pass^k | Buyers and auditors can check AI claims | Only Vanta and Sprinto publish numbers, and Trustero publishes figures without a method (Section 2.1, 2.3) | M | MT7; LT2 or LT3 live | Publication exposes weak spots | A published snapshot per workflow each quarter |
| LT7 | L7 | Three connectors against the 400+ integrations Vanta claims (Section 2.4) | Models draft declarative checks and connector mappings offline; path checks against recorded payloads, the conformance harness, a shadow diff and a reviewer gate each one; verdicts at run time stay deterministic | Breadth without model verdicts | Drata built 1,000+ tests this way, at about $20 to $30 per resource ([Drata, 8 Dec 2025](https://drata.com/blog/AI-compliance-tests)) | M, then per connector | MT1, MT8 | Reviewer fatigue; subtle errors, caught by shadow diffs and the notice period | Checks shipped per engineer-week; zero false PASS in shadow; defects in the first 30 days |
| LT8 | New | Every consequential action needs a person, yet users approve 93% of permission prompts (Section 3.6), so blanket approval adds little | Levels per (tenant, workflow, action class): shadow, suggest, approve each, sampled review. Only reversible, low-impact actions may reach sampled review; promotion needs a quality snapshot (a false-accept upper bound from blind expert re-review, pass^k, a minimum count) and an administrator's opt-in; an exposure budget per tenant; automatic demotion on an alarm or a bundle change; every auto-accepted decision cites its snapshot; a simulator replays past proposals through a candidate level | Review time goes where it matters, and autonomy is auditable | Measured levels rather than presets (Drata) or user pre-approval (Vanta); Ramp caps the exposure an agent can create ([Ramp](https://builders.ramp.com/post/agentic-risk-operations)); Drata simulates a policy "against a year of real historical traffic" before enforcing it ([Drata, 4 Aug 2026](https://drata.com/about/news/drata-extends-trust-management-platform-to-continuously-monitor-and-govern-ai-agents)) | M | MT4, MT7, LT6; Gate 3 | Over-trust; liability split (U4) | Share of eligible decisions auto-accepted; sampled false-accept rate within its bound; zero irreversible actions without a person |
| LT9 | New | One database in one region | PgBouncer in transaction mode; partitions with their own index for the largest tenants; a read replica; dedicated cells for regulated or very large tenants; EU cells on Bedrock EU inference profiles and Voyage's EU endpoint | Growth without a redesign; residency for EU buyers | The same code and migrations everywhere; Anthropic's own platforms offer only global or US inference ([Anthropic](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/manage-claude/data-residency.md)) | L | NT9; the tenants table | Too many partitions; keep dedicated partitions to the top few hundred tenants | p95 latency per tenant flat as tenants grow; a new cell stood up in under a day |
| LT10 | New | Resources not managed in code get guidance only | Only for a design partner who asks: a customer-deployed `ccp-remediator` stack; allowlisted AWS-owned runbooks; a 15-minute session with a session policy narrowed to the resource and `SourceIdentity` set to the approval id; the execution id recorded; approvals signed with a passkey over a challenge derived from the payload and precondition hashes | Fixes for resources outside code, approved and audited exactly | AWS-owned runbooks verify their own result ([AWS](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/systems-manager-automation-runbooks/latest/userguide/automation-aws-block-public-s3-bucket.html)); a session's permissions are "the intersection of the role's identity-based policy and the session policies" ([AWS](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/STS/latest/APIReference/API_AssumeRole.html)); no competitor documents a vendor-held write role (Section 6.2) | M | LT1; Gate 3 | A crown-jewel credential path; drift from the customer's code | Zero runbook runs without a matching signed approval and pre-state; CCP's own re-read confirms the fix in 100% of runs |
| LT11 | None (addition) | Coverage of a customer's cloud depends on hand-written tests, and new services or unmapped resource types stay invisible until someone writes one (6.12) | Service classes with reviewed lookups; the coverage map over the inventory; one playbook subgraph per class for gaps, which can raise FAIL or needs\_review only; the drafting pipeline promotes reviewed mappings to deterministic tests; a weekly diff of AWS's resource-type registry; tenant custom controls in the same format | Every resource is covered, excluded or visibly not assessed, and coverage converges on deterministic tests | Drata's offline test generation with auditor review, plus Stripe's measured question types; no model decides at run time (6.12) | L | MT8 for the inventory, MT10 for fan-out, MT3 for read-only tools, LT7 for drafting | A wrong class hides a gap, so a person approves each class; playbook sprawl, so classes stay at about a dozen; reviewer load | No resource without a status; zero false PASS from playbooks in shadow; median days from first sighting to a deterministic test |

Capacity (inferred): the first report's L1 to L7 total 29 to 40 engineer-weeks and LT8 to LT11 add about 16 to 22; over about 43 weeks that still leaves room for connectors drafted through LT7, catalogue content and customer requests.

**Gate 3, about month 12: before any workflow moves to sampled review (LT8) or the runbook path ships (LT10).** Blind expert re-review shows a false-PASS upper bound under 1% at 95% confidence for the workflow (about 300 expert-labelled FAIL cases with none passed; answer to U3); pass^5 meets its target; the exposure budget and automatic demotion have been tested; for LT10, a design partner has asked and passkey-signed approvals are live.

## 9. Top architectural decisions

The ten choices that shape everything else, each with the options considered, the evidence and what would change it.

### Decision 1. Where model judgement may sit in a compliance decision

- **Options:** (a) a model returns the status; (b) models extract typed fields with pointer citations, and a versioned rule decides; (c) a model decides and code audits the decision.
- **Recommendation:** (b) for every status, AI-derived ones included. AI widens coverage by drafting rules offline and extracting facts, never by deciding.
- **Evidence:** since API version 2026-10-21, requests sample at temperature 1.0 and "No other setting replaces it" ([Anthropic](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/api/migration-2026-10-21.md)); serving can shift behaviour under a fixed model id ([Anthropic](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/about-claude/models/model-ids-and-versions.md)); the best model scored 37.4% on one try and 20.6% across four on Sierra's τ-knowledge ([Sierra, May 2026](https://sierra.ai/blog/tau-knowledge)); CCP's own checker passed an "In progress" record as completed (Section 1.3).
- **Tradeoffs:** an extraction schema per interpretive test; more `needs_review` on nuanced evidence, which verifiers and reviewers absorb.
- **What would change it:** nothing in current evidence. Even a model with pass^k and a false-PASS bound at target on CCP's labelled sets would earn lighter review (Decision 5), not the right to set a status.

### Decision 2. What a PASS asserts

- **Options:** (a) missing data passes (today's effect); (b) missing data fails; (c) only a complete, authoritative read may assert absence, anything else abstains, and the rollup counts unassessed and stale subjects.
- **Recommendation:** (c).
- **Evidence:** a 404 becomes "nothing there" and then a GH-1 PASS (`connectors/github/client.py:55-60`, `evaluators/deterministic/gh1.py:42-49`), and GitHub answers 404 rather than 403 for private resources a request cannot see ([GitHub](https://docs.github.com/en/rest/using-the-rest-api/troubleshooting-the-rest-api)); on S3 Control, a 404 means "not configured", a real fact ([AWS](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonS3/latest/API/API_control_GetPublicAccessBlock.html)); the rollup cannot see unassessed subjects (`knowledge/rollup.py:76-78`); Comp AI reconciles only from complete scans (Section 2.2).
- **Tradeoffs:** more `needs_review` and `pending` at first; per-endpoint error tables to maintain.
- **What would change it:** a provider that offers a change feed with a completeness guarantee, for that source only.

### Decision 3. What identifies and reproduces a verdict

- **Options:** (a) a trigger-time key (today); (b) rule and parameter versions in the key; (c) a content address over pinned inputs and the whole method. And: reproduce by re-running models, or by rebuilding from stored artifacts.
- **Recommendation:** (b) now and (c) by month 8. "Reproducible" means rebuilt from stored inputs and stored model outputs; agreement across re-runs (pass^k) is a separate, measured gate.
- **Evidence:** the key omits rule and parameters (`evaluators/verdict.py:72-90`, `evaluators/context.py:55-58`), full runs reuse the newest observation time (`evaluators/runner.py:200`), and evaluators read the latest observations (`evaluators/context.py:116-129`); re-running a model cannot reproduce a verdict (Decision 1).
- **Tradeoffs:** input manifests and full requests and responses stored under CCP's own retention; one re-evaluation wave when the key changes.
- **What would change it:** auditors requiring agreement across re-runs as the standard (U1); pass^k would then become a per-test release gate.

### Decision 4. Which engine runs background work

- **Options:** (a) harden the Postgres queue CCP owns; (b) DBOS Transact; (c) Temporal; (d) Hatchet; (e) Procrastinate.
- **Recommendation:** (a). Run the DBOS spike in the medium term as a decision gate, and adopt DBOS only if it passes and a trigger fires: a workflow with more than three durable steps across external systems, two or more queue incidents a quarter, or more than about 20 worker processes. The audit record never lives in engine tables.
- **Evidence:** the four gaps are fixable in place (Section 3.5), and the first report's D4 recommends the same order; DBOS's pooling limit, its contradictory multi-process recovery docs, its version-matched recovery and its missing LangGraph integration (Section 3.5); Temporal workflows may do no I/O and its LangGraph plugin is a 0.1.0 preview ([Temporal](https://docs.temporal.io/develop/python/python-sdk-sandbox)); Hatchet's cron is UTC-only and skips ticks missed while it is down ([Hatchet](https://docs.hatchet.run/home/cron-runs)).
- **Tradeoffs:** CCP owns about a thousand lines of concurrency code and its tests (inferred); there is no workflow UI.
- **What would change it:** a trigger firing, or a spike showing correct multi-process recovery without Conductor on direct connections.

### Decision 5. How human approval and autonomy work

- **Options:** for authenticity, (a) typed names; (b) an SSO session plus server checks; (c) (b) plus payload and precondition binding and single-use grants; (d) (c) plus a passkey signature over a payload-derived challenge. For autonomy, presets (Drata), user pre-approval except destructive actions (Vanta), exposure budgets (Ramp), or levels earned from measurement.
- **Recommendation:** (c) for every consequential decision by month 8; (d) for the opt-in cloud write path; autonomy only as measured levels for reversible, low-impact actions, with exposure budgets, an administrator's opt-in and automatic demotion.
- **Evidence:** CCP's fail-open defects (`approvals/cli.py:88-90`, `approvals/actions.py:51-57`, `remediation/runner.py:243-289`); "Possession of a run ID or decision ID is not authorization" ([OpenAI Agents SDK](https://openai.github.io/openai-agents-python/human_in_the_loop/)); users approve 93% of permission prompts ([Anthropic](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/claude-code-auto-mode)); WebAuthn's signed client data carries the relying party's challenge ([W3C, 25 Aug 2026](https://www.w3.org/TR/webauthn-3/)); Drata's autonomy presets publish no metric gate ([Drata, 9 Sep 2026](https://help.drata.com/en/articles/16880162-tprm-agent-autonomy-settings)).
- **Tradeoffs:** passkey enrolment friction; review load until levels are earned; per-tenant labels cost money.
- **What would change it:** customers who will approve only through their own IdP (label those decisions session-only and add compensating checks); a regulator requiring per-item approval regardless of measurement.

### Decision 6. How CCP changes customer systems

- **Options:** a vendor-held write role by default; a pull request whose diff a model writes; a pull request whose diff code renders; an opt-in, customer-owned runbook role; model-chosen SDK commands; a browser agent in the user's session.
- **Recommendation:** code-rendered pull requests by default; the runbook role only for a design partner who asks, with passkey-signed approvals; never model-chosen commands or browser sessions.
- **Evidence:** Drata and Vanta fix through pull requests ([Drata](https://help.drata.com/en/articles/9930785-drata-compliance-as-code), [Vanta](https://developer.vanta.com/docs/quickstart/remediate-with-mcp.md)); none of the six incumbents documents a vendor-held write role, in our reading of their docs (Section 6.2); Comp AI's denylist and regeneration after approval (Section 2.2); AWS-owned runbooks verify their own result ([AWS](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/systems-manager-automation-runbooks/latest/userguide/automation-aws-block-public-s3-bucket.html)); Config auto-remediation "can be initiated even for compliant resources" ([AWS](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/config/latest/developerguide/setup-autoremediation.html)).
- **Tradeoffs:** slower closure; dependence on the customer's infrastructure code.
- **What would change it:** design partners who need fixes at volume for resources outside code (LT10 earlier).

### Decision 7. The tool surface

- **Options:** per-graph tools (today); one generated capability registry; third-party MCP servers as the integration bus; an agent-gateway product.
- **Recommendation:** one registry that generates agent tools and REST; one fail-closed policy check; provider reads on the connector clients. No MCP server in v1 (answer to U12); when customers' agents ask for one, the registry generates a read-only server as an output of CCP, never its internal bus. Act-class tools never appear on MCP.
- **Evidence:** Vanta exposes each action as one authorized service method to REST, CLI, its agent and MCP ([Vanta, Aug 2026](https://www.vanta.com/resources/give-your-agents-capabilities)); Harvey's engine fails closed ([Harvey](https://www.harvey.ai/blog/building-harveys-mcp-policy-engine)); CISO Assistant's read-only profile does not register write tools (Section 2.2); Vanta's hosted MCP has no read-only mode ([Vanta](https://developer.vanta.com/docs/vanta-mcp.md)); clients must treat MCP annotations as untrusted ([MCP](https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/2026-07-28/server/tools)).
- **Tradeoffs:** a generator to build; MCP spec churn.
- **What would change it:** customers' own agents becoming the main way CCP is used (propose-only MCP earlier).

### Decision 8. Evidence integrity and tenancy

- **Options:** for integrity, grants only; S3 compliance mode; governance mode with event holds plus a per-tenant chain and signed checkpoints; a managed ledger; a transparency log. For tenancy, pooled with row-level security; a schema per tenant; a database per tenant; pooled cells plus silo cells on contract.
- **Recommendation:** governance mode with event holds for raw evidence; a per-tenant chain over raw objects and database audit rows, with hourly signed checkpoints in a separate account's compliance-mode bucket; pooled cells with forced row-level security and tenant-first keys; silo cells on contract.
- **Evidence:** the Object Lock, QLDB, CloudTrail-digest and Postgres facts in 6.1, row 3; AWS's decision matrix ([AWS, Apr 2024](https://docs.aws.eu/prescriptive-guidance/latest/saas-multitenant-managed-postgresql/matrix.html)); Vanta's 2025 exposure ([TechCrunch](https://techcrunch.com/2025/06/02/vanta-bug-exposed-customers-data-to-other-customers/)).
- **Tradeoffs:** the verifier must run and the keys must be managed; row-level security has planner pitfalls, such as full-text indexes going unused ([Tom Lane, 30 May 2025](https://www.postgresql.org/message-id/2692148.1748637435%40sss.pgh.pa.us)).
- **What would change it:** buyers asking for public verification (add an RFC 3161 timestamp or a transparency log); a contract requiring a dedicated key or independent restore (a silo cell).

### Decision 9. Models and data posture

- **Options:** one model everywhere; Claude-first routes; a cross-vendor router. For judges: the same model; a different Claude model; a different family.
- **Recommendation:** Claude-first routes pinned by dated id inside release bundles: Haiku 5.5 for extraction with thinking off, Sonnet 5.5 for agents, Opus 5.5 only where measured. Production evidence only through a zero-retention organization and online calls; batches only in a separate eval organization on synthetic data; Bedrock as a rehearsed, manual standby. Judges use a different Claude model on customer data and a different family offline.
- **Evidence:** prices and lifecycle (6.3, Cost); batches, the Files API, code execution and Managed Agents fall outside zero retention, and the Fable models require 30-day retention ([Anthropic](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/manage-claude/api-and-data-retention.md)); Bedrock has no Message Batches and makes Amazon the processor ([Anthropic](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/claude-in-amazon-bedrock.md)); the 5.5 models' classifiers can refuse "Benign cybersecurity work" ([Anthropic](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/refusals-and-fallback.md)); Anthropic asks graders to be "a different model" ([Anthropic](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/test-and-evaluate/develop-tests.md)).
- **Tradeoffs:** vendor concentration; refusals on security evidence; a manual failover takes minutes.
- **What would change it:** a 5.5 refusal rate in production that review cannot absorb (then move those tests to Sonnet 5, committed to at least 30 Jun 2027); a customer DPA that allows a second vendor (cross-family judges online).

### Decision 10. How agents find evidence

- **Options:** (a) vector search alone; (b) agentic search alone, with the model calling search and read tools; (c) layered retrieval: hybrid full-text and vector search over chunks with added context, reranking, and exact-match, outline, read-span and typed fact tools for agents; (d) a knowledge graph built from documents; (e) long context in place of retrieval.
- **Recommendation:** (c), inside Postgres and per tenant (6.5). Tier 2 keeps one templated search that replay never repeats; agents get the search loop and the exact tools; long context serves only to read one document end to end, quotes first; typed facts stand in for a document graph. Retrieval is evaluated on its own, and a parser, chunker, model or prompt change ships only when recall holds and distractors do not rise.
- **Evidence:** Cursor reports 12.5% higher answer accuracy on average from adding semantic search to its agent's grep, and a stronger retriever lifted a research agent from 55.9% to 70.1% ([BrowseComp-Plus, Aug 2025](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.06600)); added context cut top-20 retrieval failures by 35%, and by 49% with the context also in the lexical index ([Anthropic, Sep 2024](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/contextual-retrieval)); document-built graphs kept about two thirds of answer entities and won only on multi-hop questions, by about 3 points ([Han et al., Nov 2026](https://arxiv.org/html/2502.11371)); accuracy fell with input length across 18 models ([Chroma, Jul 2025](https://www.trychroma.com/research/context-rot)).
- **Tradeoffs:** versioned chunk rows, partitions for the largest tenants and a golden set to label and keep; transcription and enrichment cost per evidence version; agents spend more tool calls than one search.
- **What would change it:** p99 latency that partitions cannot hold, or per-tenant keys required on the index itself (a dedicated engine); a current model with no loss at 200K tokens on CCP's set (more long-context reads); corpus-wide questions that typed facts cannot answer (a trial graph index, in drafting only).

These need a spike, a customer conversation or counsel before the decisions above are final. Section 8 refers to them by number.

| U2 | Do SOC 2 and ISO auditors test the completeness and accuracy of platform-generated evidence as strictly as financial auditors test information a company produces? This sets the value of the coverage rollup and sync receipts | The same audit firms; a read of the AICPA SOC 2 guide |

This decides how often the pull-request path applies | Customer conversations; a detection spike (stack tags, Terraform state, declared mappings) |

The EDPB holds that encrypted personal data is still personal data ([EDPB, 7 Jul 2026](https://www.edpb.europa.eu/system/files/2026-07/edpb_guidelines_202502_blockchain_v2_en.pdf)) | Counsel |

| U13 | At what chunk count per tenant does exact search at 1,024 dimensions exceed the latency budget? | A synthetic benchmark: 1,000 tenants with skewed sizes |
| U14 | What volumes should planning assume at 18 months: tenants, test-subject pairs, change rates, investigation rate? | Sales and product planning |

### Answers to the open questions (9 Nov 2026)

Answered by CCP's lead on 9 Nov 2026, while CCP has no customers yet. The sections above are updated to match. The PDF copy of this report and its diagrams predate these answers; where they differ, this Markdown wins (for example, the retrieval diagram's "Per tenant, exact" now means one HNSW partition per tenant).

| # | Answer | What it changes |
| --- | --- | --- |
| U1 | Rebuild from stored artifacts: a result is reproduced from its stored inputs and stored model output; agreement across re-runs (pass^k) stays a separate quality gate | Nothing: Decision 3 already says so |
| U2 | Assume auditors test the completeness of platform-generated evidence strictly | Sync completeness records and the coverage rollup stay top priority (MT1, MT8) |
| U3 | A 1% false-PASS upper bound at 95% confidence per test, about 300 expert-labelled FAIL cases with none passed | Gate 3 states the bound; gold sets are sized to it (6.6) |
| U4 | The tenant administrator opts in per workflow once the workflow meets its bound | Nothing: matches 4.4 and Decision 5 |
| U5 | Move to the 5.5 models now; every refusal becomes needs\_review, and the refusal rate is watched in production | Tier 2 moves to Haiku 5.5, thinking off, once NT10 lands, without waiting for a refusal measurement (8.1 amendment 4, Decision 9) |
| U6 | Keep hardening CCP's own queue; run the DBOS spike only if a named trigger fires | Nothing: matches Decision 4 |
| U7 | No customers yet, so nothing to measure; pull requests stay the default fix path | Revisit with the first design partners (LT10) |
| U8 | Spike one HR aggregator against the sync contract | Nothing: matches MT6 |
| U9 | Passkey-signed approvals are required on the opt-in cloud write path | Nothing: matches Decisions 5 and 6 |
| U10 | Keep key destruction for offboarding; get counsel's opinion before the first customer signs | Counsel review becomes a precondition of the first customer contract |
| U11 | CCP decides whether a rule change is a correction or an amendment and sends a notice with the before-and-after diff; no customer acknowledgement | Rule-change notices (6.1, row 11; 6.3, rules) carry the diff; no acknowledgement step is built |
| U12 | No MCP server in v1; REST only | 3.3, 6.1, 6.8, 6.11, MT3 and Decision 7 now generate REST first and a read-only MCP server later, when customers' agents ask |
| U13 | HNSW per tenant from the start | 6.3 and 6.5: every tenant gets its own HNSW partition; replay reads stored candidates instead of searching again |
| U14 | Plan for about 500 tenants at 18 months | One or two pooled cells, an HNSW partition per tenant and a real on-call rotation (6.3, scaling stages 1 and 2) |
| U15 | Keep Bedrock as a tested, manual standby | Nothing: matches Decision 9 and 6.6 |
| U16 | No batches: online, zero-retention calls only | Nothing: matches Decision 9 |
| RDS extensions | Checked 9 Nov 2026: RDS for PostgreSQL 16 to 18 offers pgvector 0.8.2 at most and no BM25 extension (no pg\_textsearch, pg\_search or vectorchord\_bm25) ([AWS](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonRDS/latest/PostgreSQLReleaseNotes/postgresql-extensions.html)). Stay on RDS and adapt | HNSW on 0.8.2 with insert-only chunks and an index rebuild after any erasure; Postgres full-text search (`ts_rank_cd`) instead of BM25; upgrade when RDS ships 0.8.3 or later (6.5) |
| AI Act Article 50(2) | Ask counsel before the first EU customer; keep the provenance badge on every AI output meanwhile | 6.6 standards row updated |
| Advisor tool | Not used for now; revisit when its advice is readable and zero retention is confirmed | Nothing: matches 6.4 |
